### A.21. Executive summary
(max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)


---
**Page 9**
---

1. Malawi is exposed to increasing temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, and extreme events (e.g., droughts, floods and high/low temperature events) which result in a range of health-related impacts.
2. The main project impacts will address these climate change impacts on health by strengthening the health system in southern Malawi with infrastructure that facilitates utilization of climate-sensitive health data through existing decision-making platforms.
3. Existing WASH and healthcare infrastructure is not well-equipped to withstand climate extremes, and healthcare staff are often overburdened due to high patient loads and lack of resources. This results in poor quality care for patients in poor levels of awareness of climate risk at community level. Climate-related health impacts are compounded by numerous factors such as: high levels of poverty, gender inequality, population displacement, and migration.
4. The project is aligned with the Global Climate Fund (GCF) Strategic Objective 2 to develop a climate-resilient healthcare system that will reduce the adverse effects of climate change on health and well-being. This intent is aligned with the GCF’s Guiding Principles for Financing Climate and Health Solutions, which are endorsed by the Ministry of Health of Malawi.
5. The project combines national-, district- and community-level interventions to create sufficient conditions for scalability and sufficient integration to catalyze the transition to a climate-resilient health system. Institutionally, the project will strengthen the capacity of the Ministry of Health to develop and implement policies that ensure that functions at national level and be able to provide appropriate early warning for the occurrence of climate-sensitive diseases, including malaria, dengue fever, cholera, typhoid, and other waterborne diseases; acute respiratory infections; malnutrition and diseases/conditions linked to high/extreme heat. The damage to the healthcare system and WASH infrastructure in southern Malawi is expected to be mitigated by strengthening the health system with improved disease surveillance, physical infrastructure, and human resources.
6. Strengthening health systems under climate change is already within the ambit of the government of Malawi (National Strategic Plan 2017-2035), but there is a discrepancy in the lack of structured guidance to support the operationalization of this ambition.
7. The focus of the project will be on strengthening the health system with infrastructure that facilitates utilization of climate-sensitive health data through existing decision-making platforms, which are likely to remain functional beyond the project life span. As regards resources, the project will support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy for Malawi, taking advantage of the growing impetus towards inclusion of health in climate change processes and plans.
8. The project’s goal statement is: If the health system in southern Malawi is strengthened in terms of governance, infrastructure, and human resources, then the adverse effects of climate change on the health status and well-being of women, children and men will be reduced. Because healthcare staff, facilities and communities will have stronger capacity to anticipate, plan and respond to climate-related health risks.


The GCF was an initiating party in the Guiding Principles for Financing Climate and Health Solutions, and these are endorsed by the Ministry of Health of Malawi and Save the Children. Please see: Green Climate Fund (2023) 41 funders, partners endorse new guiding

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Climate Context (500 words, approximately two pages)

#### Context and current climate

Malawi is among the most climate-vulnerable countries in the world, ranking 18th out of 195 countries on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Index. The country faces multiple climate change and other global challenges in combination with its readiness to improve resilience*. The proposed project focuses largely on Malawi's Southern Region, as it is projected to be more vulnerable than other regions due to high exposure to climate risks and impacts, in combination with high vulnerability.

*Source: Notre Dame Global Adaptation Index. Available at: <https://ndgadp.nd.edu/about.html>

#### Observed temperature and precipitation

Temperature and precipitation are computed from temperature datasets from the Climate Research Unit (CRU) T2.1.0 dataset and rainfall data from the WorldClim 1.4 dataset, respectively. The mean annual temperature range in Malawi is between 12°C and 35°C, with extremes of 40°C in semi-arid areas. Observed mean annual precipitation ranges from 725 mm to 2500 mm depending on location.

The warm-wet season occurs from November to April, during which periods most of the rainfall falls as a result of the summer (aqua) rainband pattern (refer to Annex A Section 3.1.2). Mean annual rainfall ranges from 725 mm to 2500 mm depending on location.

The frequency and duration of severe droughts in southern Malawi have increased since the early 1980s, consistent with evidence that climate change is already affecting precipitation patterns in the region. The projected changes in temperature-based extremes support the notion that southern Malawi will be exposed also to heat-related hazards, particularly considering the report's current temperature range and extremes, and the increase in mean annual temperature over time (page 15).

#### References

* Niles, J. et al. (2014) Updated high-resolution grids of monthly climatic observations: the CRU TS3.10 Dataset. International Journal of Climatology 34: 623-642. doi: 10.1002/joc.3771.

* Malawi, M. (2015) Climate change and land use change on the river regime in Wankumwamba River. Sino Basin in Malawi. Journal of Water and Climate Change 12: 1674-1693. doi:10.2166/wcc.2015.138.

### Climate change projections

11. Novel climate projections for southern Malawi were computed from bias-corrected outputs of Global Climate Models (GCMs) participating in the fifth phase of the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project (CMIP5). A bias-corrected ensemble of 20 GCMs was used to generate these projections, which are considered more reliable than individual model runs. The CMIP5 models have been bias corrected for a wide suite of global climate projections that have been bias-corrected for the African region and thus the AMMA2030 has incorporated these projections into its climate change impact modeling; for this analysis, an ensemble of 16 CMIP5 GCMs were examined (refer to Annex 2. Section 3.2 for further details). The models project a warming trend in southern Malawi over the course of the 21st century with daily temperatures expected to rise by 2°C under RCP4.5 and by more than 4°C under RCP8.5 (Figure 3). In terms of precipitation, models are relatively consistent in projecting an increase in rainfall over the course of the 21st century compared to temperature; models are relatively consistent in projecting a decrease across southern Malawi over the course of the 21st century, with the degree of projected change being greater in RCP8.5 than RCP4.5 (Figure 4 Annex 2 section 3.2).

12. In terms of temperature-related extremes, it is likely that maximum temperature, minimum temperature, warm days, warm nights, hot days, hot nights, cold days, and cold nights will all increase over the course of the 21st century for both RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emission scenarios, with the increase being more pronounced under RCP8.5 (refer to Annex A, Table 3). For example, maximum temperature is likely to increase by approximately 0.7°C per decade over the course of the 21st century for both emissions scenarios, with the increase being more pronounced under RCP8.5 than RCP4.5. By contrast, cold nights, cold days and cold spell duration are all likely to decrease over the course of the 21st century for both emissions scenarios, with the decrease being more pronounced in RCP8.5 than RCP4.5.

### With respect to precipitation-based extremes, 1-day and 5-day maximum precipitation, as well as total precipitation on very wet and extremely wet days, are all likely to increase over the course of the 21st century across southern Africa under RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 (see Figure S3.2.2 for details; see Figure 6 for the projected changes in 1-day and 5-day maximum precipitation, as an illustration). The ensemble median shows a general increase in total precipitation over the period from 1975 to 2060. It is projected that there will be a decrease in the number of tropical cyclones making landfall in the region at 1°C, 2°C and 3°C of global warming. They are projected to become more intense, so when they do make landfall, the impacts are expected to be high.

### 14. Finally, with respect to drought risk, consecutive dry day duration is likely to increase over the 21st century, and for the end of the century, the maximum number of consecutive dry days in a year increases by up to 30 days across parts of southern Malawi (Figure 7). The projected changes in dry day duration are more pronounced under RCP8.5 than under RCP4.5, while the change is less pronounced in the RCP4.5 emissions scenario (Figure 7; refer to Annex 2, section 2.2.3 for full details).

## Anticipated impacts from climate change on health and well-being, and vulnerabilities in the target region

### 15. The projected changes in temperature, rainfall, and extremes that have been detailed above have numerous implications for human health and well-being, and are expected to be experienced by communities in southern Malawi promptly. Given the major climatic drivers that southern Malawi faces (the CMIP5RC project seeks to respond to), increasing temperatures will lead to an increase in heat stress, particularly during the dry season; increased rainfall and increases in temperature-related extremes; the related climate hazards are increased flooding (as a result of an increase in heavy precipitation events) and droughts. The projected changes in temperature and rainfall will also lead to high-temperature heat events, and increases in droughts. The increased disease burden from climatic changes will reduce well-being and health outcomes for all population groups, but particularly women, children, the elderly, and other marginalized groups.

### 16. The main vulnerabilities in the target areas are related to the high levels of poverty and food insecurity, gender inequality, poor access to basic services such as education, water supply, sanitation facilities, and high unemployment (refer to Annex 2, Section 2.1). The health implications of climate change are exacerbated by existing inequalities, particularly gender inequalities, which will be illustrated further below.


---

---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

example, access to water in times of scarcity places a heavy burden on women and girls who miss opportunities for study, work, and self-care as a result (Annex 2, Section 3.5.6).

### 17. The impacts of climate change are expected to be exacerbated by a combination of environmental degradation, decreasing rainfall; and increasing demands placed on water resources, have cost implications for the provision of safe drinking water in some parts of the Southern Region, due to increased costs for water pumping systems (refer to Annex 2, Section 3.5.1), in addition to the need for more water and food will exacerbate pressure on existing ASH infrastructure that has already been observed in recent extreme events, such as the destruction of boreholes, water taps, and other water supply facilities.

### 18. The rain-fed nature of agriculture in Mali24 means that its productivity levels are highly dependent on the nature and timing of rainfall. In addition to this, climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods, which will have a negative impact on temperature negatively affect crops (refer to Annex 2, Section 3.2.3). Thus, climate change is anticipated to exacerbate existing food insecurity in Mali. The country has experienced significant declines in cereal production with higher temperatures, with sensitivity to heat intensity in drought conditions: by the 2050s, in parts of southern Mali, maize yields are expected to decline by up to 40% (FAO, 2013). Climate change is also expected to reduce agricultural productivity affecting food security by reducing the quality, quantity and diversity of food available25; these factors will have a severe impact on malnutrition, exacerbating an already challenging situation where 39% of under-five children are chronically malnourished. The combination of climate change and poor sanitation can weaken immune systems, increasing susceptibility to diseases.

### 19. Changes in the frequency, intensity, duration, severity, intensity, and geographical prevalence of climate-sensitive infectious and vector-borne diseases (refer to Annex 2, Section 3.5.3). For one example, there is a likely increase in malaria transmission risk due to changes in rainfall patterns and temperature, which can lead to an increase of cases in Mali26; with the country experiencing regular chikungunya outbreaks. For children younger than five years old, malnutrition is a major factor that increases their vulnerability to infectious diseases, and there are strong concerns over malaria, for which temperature, along with rainfall and humidity, is a key driver of the rate of transmission27. Based on stakeholder engagement (see Annex 7), knowledge levels at district and community level on changes in climate-sensitive diseases are low.

### 20. Non-infectious climate-sensitive diseases and conditions are also highly likely to increase in the future (refer to Annex 2, Section 3.5.4). These include heat stress, which is a major concern for vulnerable groups as it impacts heart strain or stress, the risk of which is exacerbated by dehydration28; it can aggravate by decreases in water

availability of non-communicable diseases (NCDs) make up 75% of the total burden of disease in Malawi and 29% of the mortality[1]. Many NCDs are related to heat temperature rise, including cardiovascular disease, stroke, renal disease, diabetes, and cancer. Children under five years old, pregnant women, and young adults, especially those with children are particularly vulnerable to the effects of heat. In Malawi, lower respiratory tract infections, stroke and ischemic heart diseases are all leading causes of death[2]. In the Southern Region, a quarter of the population is now up-under 15 years old, and in the North-Western region, 30% of the market is under five years old. The burden of NCDs when patients are exposed to excessive heat), yet across the globe heat-related mortality and morbidity is poorly documented[3].

21. Increased temperatures and heat-related extremes in Malawi will also affect people’s health and well-being more broadly, including through increased risk of infectious diseases, such as malaria, dengue fever, and cholera; adverse pregnancy and birth outcomes[4]; occupational health issues[5]; and increased healthcare costs[6]. To consider the full range of impacts on mental health, it is important to recognize that heat stress can have a direct effect on mental and behavioral disorders contributing significantly to the global burden of diseases[7], and multiple studies demonstrate that climate change has a significant impact on mental health. For example, a 2018 study found that climate change exacerbates many social, environmental and economic risk factors for problems in mental health and psychological well-being among vulnerable populations such as women and children[8]. A 2017 study of the 2015 heat wave in France found that the heat wave was associated with an increase in suicide rates. A 2013 study found that extreme temperatures are associated with increased risk for mental health problems, including depression and anxiety among pregnant women[9]. A recent and as yet unpublished study conducted in 2022 found that 85% of women reported that their mental health has been affected by heat stress. Women who have experienced heat stress during pregnancy report higher levels of anxiety and breastfeeding mothers noted the stress and anxiety that extreme events bring because of the uncertainty they create about whether they will survive to see an actual childbirth event ever[10].

22. Some of the ways in which the above impacts on mental health are likely to exacerbate existing inequalities in health impacts, particularly gender inequalities in the target areas, are: climate-related impacts on food availability (e.g., droughts and floods), water scarcity, and sanitation; increased burden for women and girls due to heat stress, including women’s responsibility for household food security[11] (Annex 2, Sections 3.3.4 and 3.3.6); water and sanitation scarcity may make it more difficult for women and girls to access safe drinking water and sanitation facilities; increased burden on women in the workplace, and community[12] (Annex 2, Section 3.3.8); disruption caused to WASH facilities (at schools) by extreme precipitation events; and heat stress during pregnancy and breastfeeding[13]. For example, a study of the impact of climate change on food security found that women in Malawi are more vulnerable than men due to their greater responsibility for household food security[14] (Annex 2, Section 3.3.6). **B**

number of marriages in daughters[2]), and child and adolescent marriage and pregnancy rates are already high, particularly in rural areas (Annexes 2, Sections 3.3.3 and 3.3.5) and the availability and accessibility of sexual and reproductive health services is low (Annexes 2, Section 3.3.6). The combination of these factors (see Annex 3.3.5) These gendered impacts combine and interact in multiple ways (Annex 2, Section 3.3.6). For instance, increased incidence in GBV as a result of climate stresses (e.g., floods) means there is increased demand for GBV services at a time when these services are already overburdened. The combination of the increased incidence and prevalence of GBV together with loss of access to sexual reproductive health services lead to unwanted pregnancy and non-consensual pregnancies, which in turn can result in poor maternal and child health outcomes (Annex 2, Section 3.3.6). This has a negative impact on overall health outcomes for girls and babies. For example, a study conducted in 2022 in both Nainoa Bay and the project district of Mafeteng found that 51% of women who had experienced GBV were not accessing sexual reproductive health services by adolescent young men and women, leading to early and unwanted pregnancies and marriages, which did not have any impact on their education or future life opportunities (Annex 2, Section 3.3.6).

**20.** Natural extreme events, particularly floods, cause physical damage to healthcare infrastructure and to transport infrastructure, which impedes the availability of safe access to health care. Infrastructure includes buildings, WASH facilities, equipment, and other resources that are required for the delivery of services (Annex 2, Section 3.3.7). This has occurred across the project areas (Annexes 2, Sections 3.3.6 and 3.3.7). An annual average of 100 education and health-care facilities are affected annually by flood for 1–10 years, and 145 facilities in 1-5 year floods, with over 40% of the areas experiencing flooding for more than five years (Annexes 2, Sections 3.3.6 and 3.3.7). This has a negative impact on the healthcare provision system, which relates both to the state and uptake of facilities and the presence and capacity of staff in these facilities.

## Related projects/Interventions

* **Mafeteng:** Has demonstrated commitment to addressing climate change through progress made on national climate change policies, the NAP and the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC). The proposed project aligns closely with the NAP and NDC. Mafeteng has a strong track record of implementing projects that have resulted in improved infrastructure, buildings and energy systems[3]. Mafeteng has accessed GCF readiness funds and as part of this funding, has developed a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and is currently implementing the NAP under the Sustainable Development in Malateng (LUMIP)-implemented developed capacity and tools for the coordination and execution of NAP processes. The proposed project aligns with the Mafeteng Climate Resilience Stocktaking Report 2018 and provides information on climate change risks, vulnerabilities, and activities are fully aligned with the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and Malateng’s health sector is developing a Health National Adaptation Plan (HNAP). The proposed project will directly contribute to the NAP by strengthening the capacity of key national stakeholders in Mafeteng for early warning systems in preparedness and management of climate change health effects; i) climate-resilient health infrastructure and services, ii) climate-resilient health service delivery, iii) climate-resilient health workforce, iv) climate-resilient health information systems, v) climate-resilient health financing. The proposed project will also contribute to the NAP by strengthening the capacity of key national stakeholders are also benefiting from capacity strengthening for climate change programming thanks to GCF readiness funds.

The project is a joint initiative between the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) and the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), with funding from the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The project aims to improve climate information services, including meteorological capacity for early warning and forecasting, development and dissemination of tailored forecasts for prioritized sectors, and strengthening of national early warning systems in response to extreme weather events (floods). The project installed weather stations, lightning detection and thunder alert system based on radar.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

## Figure B Theory of Change diagram for the Community Health and Well-being for Rural Communities in southern Malawi

### Barriers

The project will address the following barriers:

- Barrier 1: inadequate availability and use of climate information/data in the health sector; integrated surveillance (climate-informed) systems are key for enhancing the capacity of health systems to prepare and adapt to climate-sensitive diseases and conditions. Climate-informed surveillance can enhance the preparedness of health systems to respond effectively to climate-sensitive diseases and conditions, including early warning and response. The impact and prepare for effective response.
- Barrier 2: limited institutional capacity to anticipate, prepare for and respond to the health risks of climate change and this is a further barrier to sustainable development in Malawi. This barrier will be addressed by strengthening the National Meteorological Service and Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services to enable the functioning of an integrated surveillance and climate-informed health early warning system in Malawi.
- Barrier 3: within the healthcare system, healthcare staff do not have country-specific information contributes to gaps in integrated risk monitoring, early warning and response; thus, there is a lack of an integrated surveillance and climate-informed early warning system.

### Mitigation

#### Mitigation 1: Climate-informed Surveillance System
- Strengthening the National Meteorological Service and Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services.
- Enhancing the capacity of health systems to prepare and adapt to climate-sensitive diseases and conditions, including early warning and response. The impact and prepare for effective response.

#### Mitigation 2: Early Warning System
- Developing a country-specific information system that integrates climate data with health data.
- Ensuring that healthcare staff have access to up-to-date and relevant climate information to inform their decision-making processes.

#### Mitigation 3: Capacity Building
- Providing training and capacity-building programs for healthcare staff on the use of climate information in their daily work.
- Developing guidelines and protocols for integrating climate information into routine health services.

### Conclusion

The project aims to address these barriers by strengthening the National Meteorological Service, Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services, and enhancing the capacity of health systems. This will lead to a more integrated surveillance and climate-informed early warning system in Malawi.

sufficient countries use climate-informed health surveillance and early warning and response systems, and are not aware of how climate change will affect health outcomes and how they need to alter their service delivery and messaging around these issues in order to improve the effectiveness of their health services and their ability to detect, respond, and respond. This means that health risks of chronic changes are overlooked and under-addressed, leading to poorer health outcomes, and an inability to sustain the healthcare improvements that have been attained over recent years.

3b. The lack of integration between climate change adaptation planning and national health systems and planning processes: While preliminary investigations of the links between health and climate have been undertaken, and a Health Needs Assessment has been conducted in some countries, there is a lack of integration with other sectors or capacity at local level, and multi-sectoral collaboration on health and climate change is lacking at district and sub-district levels.

3b. Barrier 5: Lack of community capacity to reduce climate health risk: The lack of information and awareness around climate change and its impacts on health, coupled with a lack of knowledge about how to adapt and respond to these changes, means that communities are unable to take measures to reduce risk, which means that health burden from climate change will continue to increase.

3b. Barrier 6: Marginalised groups, particularly women and children, are more vulnerable to climate-related health impacts; people living in poverty have less capacity to adapt to the health impacts of climate change. Vulnerability to climate-sensitive diseases is often exacerbated by other factors such as gender inequality, lack of access to education, limited adaptive capacity and higher sensitivity to the health impacts of climate change. Vulnerability to climate-sensitive diseases can also be increased when there are changes in food security (Section 3.2.1), water availability (Section 3.2.4) or food provision to women. If agricultural production decreases as a result of a variable climate, this can lead to food and nutrition insecurity (Section 3.3.2, Annex 5). The stress created by such circumstances has impacts particularly on the mental health of women and children, which in turn can have negative consequences for their physical health. This stress further reinforces gender inequality, for example pulling girls child from schooling and encouraging early marriage (Section 3.4.1).

The theory of change

The theory of change is that if the capacity of southern Haiti is strengthened in terms of governance, health and climate information systems, service delivery and community engagement,

THEN the vulnerability to climate-sensitive diseases will be reduced; the health of women, children and adults will be reduced.

BECAUSE healthcare facilities, staffs and communities will have stronger capacity to anticipate, plan and respond to climate change impacts on health.

3b. The project aims to achieve impact through four interlinked outcomes (described in full in Section 8.3): Outcome 1: Reduced risk from climate-sensitive diseases and conditions; Outcome 2: Healthcare infrastructure is able to deliver services that are more resilient to the health impacts of climate change, including providing quality care for women's health care in the context of changing climate risk; Outcome 4: Community level health is more resistant in the context of changing climate risk. The project will achieve these outcomes through a multi-pronged approach that targets several priority areas identified as being critical to the foundations of climate-resilient health systems, including strengthening and enabling environment (Outcome 1), healthcare infrastructure (Outcome 2), enabling healthcare service provision, including through staff capacity (Outcome 3) and community engagement (Outcome 4).

The potential for transformation comes from taking a multi-pronged approach that targets several priority areas identified as being critical to the foundations of climate-resilient health systems, including strengthening and enabling environment (Outcome 1), healthcare infrastructure (Outcome 2), enabling healthcare service provision, including through staff capacity (Outcome 3) and community engagement (Outcome 4).

The project combines national, district, and local level interventions to create strong conditions for scalability, and ensures that the project is not only sustainable in terms of its outputs but also in terms of its enabling environment at both national and district levels. There is scope to establish critical mass among health staff in government and healthcare facilities so that districts not targeted directly by the project could take on board

### Assumptions

45a. The Ministry of Health (as the custodian of the health EWRIS) and the Ministry of Climate Change & Natural Resources (as the producer and custodian of weather and climate information) need to be able to effectively collaborate in order for the project to achieve its objectives. The ability to share and exchange relevant decision-making information can vary, for example, the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS) queries may not be able to access the Ministry of Health’s EWRIS data or vice versa. In the event that there is a major outbreak (e.g., potential disease outbreaks), then it is likely that Ministry of Health should issue that warning. Activities under Outcome 4 are designed to strengthen these linkages, and in particular, the institutional arrangements between different ministries as river-related flood risk warnings are currently issued by the Ministry of Climate Change & Natural Resources.

Building on the assumption in Outcome 1.4 (community capacity to reduce health risks from climate change increase) is contingent upon the assumption that people are able to act on the information they receive (e.g., alerts, WASH and nutrition advice). This assumes that the community has access to the information and can understand it. The assumptions enabled by Outcomes 1.2 and 3 ensure that which the institutional, infrastructure and staff capacity are in place to allow communities to make climate-related health decisions. The assumption is that people will be able to act on this. 45. Assumption: The overall success and sustainability of the project is based on the assumption that there will be ongoing political, budget, and staffing support from government. Extraneous circumstances such as a major disaster or political instability could occur which would impact on the sustainability of this assumption here are current commitments remain.

46. Assumption: The resilience building will be undermined significantly by major climate-related disasters during implementation. Although the long term aim is to build resilience of the health system, this resilience building will be a challenge in the short-term as there may be significant setbacks from such events. This could have an impact on progress, not least because the attention of the health sector, facilities, and staff would be diverted into emergency response.

## Target groups and beneficiaries

47. The CHW/BRIC project is designed to maximize impact by combining national-level and district level activities that will:
- Increase access to health services for climate and health (Outcome 1, Outcome 3) with targeted investments at 79 health facilities in six target districts (Outcome 2), investments to equip health staff so they are better prepared to manage climate-related health risks (Outcome 4).
- Strengthen the capacity of health facilities to respond effectively to disasters that threaten the lives and livelihoods of extremely vulnerable to climate change (Outcome 4). The selected health facilities and communities are located in the six target districts.

48. The selection of the target districts and TAs was determined through a rigorous and consultative process that involved provincial, district, and community stakeholders. This process included a vulnerability assessment (Annex A) and a vulnerability assessment (Annex 2, Section 6.2). The vulnerability assessment used quantitative methods that have been adopted in previous projects to assess the vulnerability of communities to climate change. The selection was based on criteria for sensitivity and adaptive capacity that were selected from among the range of indicators in climate vulnerability assessments taking into account the availability of existing data at appropriate resolution[1]. The results of the health vulnerability assessment are presented in Annex 2, Section 6.3.

49. The target districts were selected to represent a wide range of climatic conditions and levels of exposure to climate change impacts. The vulnerability index for each district was calculated using a combination of indicators that reflect the level of exposure to climate change impacts and the capacity of communities to adapt to these changes. The results are presented in Annex 2, Section 6.3.

50. The focus is on health centres due to their unique status as the most common primary health facility within the relevant catchment areas, serving the most vulnerable and remote community members within the Traditional Authorities while being largely underutilized by these communities[1]. This project will support the implementation of climate change adaptation infrastructure modifications.

51. The villages that are targeted for this project are those identified in Annexes 3 and 4 to make up a total of 500 target villages. These villages will be selected in a consultation between the District Health Management Team (DHMT) and the communities themselves, with the DHMT providing technical guidance on the selection criteria within the TAs and communities themselves.

52. The communities will be selected based on a combination of the following factors:
- Willingness to participate in the project intervention
- Involvement in previous projects (priority given to communities without prior projects)
- Existence of ongoing donor-funded projects
- Social cohesion
- Population size

## B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

This comprehensive adaptation health system project focuses on enhancing climate resilience in healthcare and addressing risks and impacts from droughts, heat stress and floods and delivering against two GCF results areas: The Green Climate Fund’s Adaptation Results Area (ARA-1) and the Mitigation Results Area (MRA-4). It will improve healthcare facility standards, and capacity building. It aligns with the national agenda, Malawi's National Adaptation Plan (NAP), and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will directly benefit 798,878 vulnerable people and will indirectly benefit a further 2,359,182 vulnerable people within six districts and 500 communities. It will also contribute to Malawi’s NDCs by reducing greenhouse gas emissions from the health sector.

The project aims to develop a climate-resilient healthcare system that will reduce the adverse effects of climate change on health and wellbeing in Malawi, while enhancing disease surveillance and outbreak response through a multi-pronged approach as set out in the project TcG (Figure 8).

# OUTCOME 1: Reduced risk from climate-sensitive diseases and conditions

The activities under this outcome will enable better surveillance of climate-related diseases and conditions; enhance the use of climate information in the health sector for effective early health warning; and build institutional and human capacity to support the sustainable management of climate-sensitive diseases and conditions. This will be achieved by increasing availability and use of climate information/data in the health sector, the barrier of limited institutional capacity for addressing climate-sensitive diseases and conditions, and the lack of a coordinated approach to surveillance and response that is mainstreamed into local strategic plans and planning processes. The project will produce greater understanding of the impact of climate change on health through improved surveillance and early warning systems.

### Output 1.1: Climate-informed health surveillance system and Early Health Warning Response System (EWRs)

#### Activity 1.1.1: Strengthen the health surveillance system by identifying alert triggers for key climate-sensitive diseases/conditions

55. Malawi has a Health Management Information System (HMIS) that includes surveillance of disease outbreaks, but there is no systematic approach to identify and respond to climate-sensitive diseases and conditions. The project will build on the understanding of disease patterns in relation to weather variables in Malawi to inform the design of a climate-informed Early Warning Response System (EWRs). This will be achieved by identifying alert triggers for key climate-sensitive diseases/conditions; alert triggers need to be set so that it distinguishes different potential threat levels for different diseases/conditions. These include diseases linked to high temperature extremes: malaria, cholera and dengue fever and those linked to heavy rainfall: meningitis, typhoid fever, and leptospirosis. The project will also ensure that should the thresholds be reached through surveillance or anticipation. Triggers will be calculated based on a combination of weather variables (e.g., temperature, precipitation) and disease prevalence data for each district. There is ongoing work by the Ministry of Health, the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS), and other partners to develop climate information products that can be used in surveillance systems. The project will also pilot district maps, with some nascent work on understanding associations between climate data and cholera and dengue fever. The project will build on this work by developing a national EWRs system for malaria and scale-up the role of the EWRs to five additional districts through inter alia the establishment of sentinel sites. The final objective is to have an operational EWRs system in place, with at least 10 sentinel sites across Malawi. The health surveillance system by identifying alert triggers for key climate-sensitive health conditions will be a collaborative effort between the Ministry of Health (MOH); the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS) and the Department (East); the Department of Disaster Management Affairs (DDMA); academic, and technical consultants - either through direct engagement or through the use of existing partners. The MOH will lead this activity as part of the start of the project, which will then meet regularly to oversee the work of the consultants, and the consultants will provide regular updates and progress on the ongoing work. The EWRs system will be developed in collaboration with a range of stakeholders, including community, where 43 sentinel sites are reported under one site (Kilosa), which is an improvement from the past where each district had its own surveillance system. For many diseases/conditions, there may not be enough data to develop alert triggers for all diseases/conditions listed malaria and cholera, two of the focus diseases for this project – reporting occurs on a weekly basis. With regard to acute malnutrition, there is no systematic approach in place to identify and respond to acute malnutrition in Malawi. In place for these diseases/conditions, the project currently aims to contribute towards a climate-informed EWRs that is not necessarily linked to sentinel sites (e.g., through the use of satellite imagery). However, if additional data is leveraged current health services activities to conduct acute malnutrition surveillance will be explored).

56. This activity will: i) improve the existing health surveillance system to incorporate climate information; and ii) set alert triggers for the specific diseases and conditions listed above. Targeted studies and expert validation will be undertaken in collaboration with the Ministry of Health, the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS), and other stakeholders. Malawi-specific thresholds for these diseases/conditions will be determined via collaborative work between the MOH, DCCMS, and other relevant partners. The project will also engage the Department of Climate Change and Meteorological Services (DCCMS), international organisations (e.g., WHO), and other relevant partners to ensure that the EWRs system is sustainable in Malawi. Further details about this activity (and under all other project activities, as relevant) are listed in Section 6E.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

## Activity 1.1.2 Strengthen the institutional architecture for managing the ongoing operation of the climate-informed health Early Warning and Response System (EWSARS)

57. Significant national efforts in Malawi have been made to improve the availability of weather and climate information and to ensure that this information is being adequately utilised within the health sector.

58. The proposed project will improve the institutional architecture for effective communication of weather and climate information, including early warning signals, to all levels of the health system. This will be achieved through a fully-functioning committee, terms of reference or similar administrative processes. The project will strengthen the capacity of the MoH to interpret and disseminate this information in a timely manner, with clear guidelines for interpretation of the significance of this information for particular diseases/conditions, in order to develop a climate-informed health Early Warning System (EWSARS). This will be achieved through the establishment of an EWSARS committee, which will meet at least on a quarterly basis for governing purposes, with clearly defined processes for sharing data and information between the MoH and other relevant stakeholders. The project will also strengthen the MoH and the regulator. Outlines of the quarterly governance meetings, there will be more regular communications (at least weekly) between technical and data colleagues regarding weather alerts and automated sharing of data, which links to the health surveillance system. This will ensure that the EWSARS committee is informed on a timely basis with information (e.g., seasonal forecasts), this forum should enable interpretation to inform annual planning for short-term interventions in the health sector.

59. The resulting climate-informed health EWSARS will be integrated with existing Community Alert Protocols and potentially other early warning systems, such as those used by the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA). The institutional architecture will ensure that data also includes epidemiological data (linking to the data generated from the health surveillance system) and will enable the MoH to provide a more comprehensive picture of the situation in Malawi.

60. At community level, the project will assess the accessibility needs of target communities and develop inclusive indicators for their response to context-specific barriers; are gender-responsive; and reach the most vulnerable and marginalised.


## Activity 1.2.1 Establish sentinel sites at selected healthcare facilities to provide improved climate and health data for the health Early Warning and Response System (EWSARS)

61. Selected healthcare facilities will be strategically designated as sentinel sites to provide better data for surveillance, monitoring and evaluation of the climate-informed health EWSARS. Sentinel sites will be established in the District Health Information System and the national HMIS, and this data will then be combined with meteorological data at regional level. The sentinel sites will be established by the MoH and the MCH for the existing malaria sentinel site at the Zomba central hospital, establishing additional sentinel sites (e.g., sentinel sites for other diseases such as dengue fever) in the six districts. Sentinel sites will receive dedicated training under Activity 3.1.1 to so that data can be appropriately collected. Where there is no existing sentinel site within a district (e.g., if the Zomba central hospital is not located within the district), the project will invest in providing such equipment to the healthcare facilities where the MoH can guarantee that they are able to collect and report data on a regular basis. The establishment of sentinel sites – will also benefit from increased and better-quality data from surrounding health centres. Therefore, the project will also invest in providing essential data collection equipment to these surrounding health centres.

## Output 1:2 District Health Adaptation plans

### Activity 1.2.1 Facilitate preparation and local endorsement of District Health Adaptation Plans in six districts

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 24

## District Health Adaptation Plans (DHAPs) will be developed in alignment with the priority actions of the Health National Adaptation Plan. Their development will include: analysis of the nature of district specific climate risks to health, including those related to extreme weather events, water and sanitation, food security, vector-borne diseases, inclusive actions; risk communication and prevention strategies; mechanisms to direct resources in case of new disease outbreaks/surge capacity (linked to the health EINARS); policies and protocols informed by current and predicted climate change impacts on health; and adaptation plans for specific climate events affecting public health (linked to the health EWARS).

### Activity 1.2.2 Advocate for stronger integration of climate-resilient health within adaptation planning at district and sub-district levels

- Holistically addressing climate risk requires maintaining/strengthening of climate and health into broader district development planning activities.
- This activity will actively engage other parties that are involved in planning processes, including the District Executive Committee (DEC), District Health Management Team (DHMT), Disaster Risk Reduction, climate adaptation and other sectoral planning; and the area and village committees whose development plans are being reviewed. The DEC, DHMT, and area/village committees will be engaged to ensure that disaster risk reduction, climate change and disaster risk management are integrated into their respective District Plans and District Budgets on health (climate change and disaster risks) will be reviewed in the six target districts.
- The activity will also engage other key actors such as local government officials, civil society organizations, and community-based organizations. These actors will be engaged at different levels: from national level coalition-building to district-level engagement with the DEC, DHMT, area/village committees, and other relevant stakeholders (facilitated by the project working in collaboration with other civil society groups) to build the advocacy agenda and conduct joint activities.
- At the district level, the project will use the existing governance structures, for instance, the District Executive Committee, that are responsible for overall development work at the district level. Working with key members of the DEC, the project will ensure that climate change is integrated into all aspects of planning, including the visibility and prioritization of health and climate in the District Development Plans and District Profiles.
- Civil society actors (such as local NGOs, community-based organizations, youth groups, women’s groups, and other local/NGOs), youth groups, and key actors from across relevant sectors (e.g., health, education, climate change adaptation) will be engaged at all levels.

### Activity 67. Coalition-building meetings will be organized nationally, as well as in the six target districts, with other organizations/networks that work on climate adaptation, disaster risk reduction, and sectoral planning; and joint

advocacy strategies and action plans will be developed collaboratively with non-government groups. At sub-national level, in each region (that has its own youth network as a separate entity), there are the Cabinet, Principal Secretary and Parliamentary Committee on Social Development. There is the Malawi Health Network and Coalition on Universal Health Coverage.

**OUTCOME 5: Healthcare infrastructure is able to deliver service and care in the context of changing climate risk**

68. This outcome focuses on ensuring that healthcare system physical infrastructure (e.g., health centres, hospitals) are able to continue providing healthcare services in light of climate hazards (e.g., heat and floods), addressing the built-in resilience of these facilities as well as their ability to adapt to new conditions. The project will provide guidance for climate-resilient health infrastructures; improve the climate resilience of health facilities, and improve the capacity of the Malawi Health Network and Coalition on Universal Health Coverage (MHN) to support GH security and overall health (Annex 2, Section 3). For full details of this Outcome, refer to Annex 7 (Section 6.1.4).

**Outcome 5.1 Climate-resilient health centres, district and central hospitals and schools for community health**

Activity 2.1.1 Develop a national standard for climate-resilient healthcare facilities

93. Extensive WHO guidance exists on the development of standards for healthcare physical infrastructure[6], but this guidance is not specific to Malawi. This activity will translate the WHO guidance to the Malawi context, taking into account aspects such as construction standards and design, sustainable energy supply (including for vaccine cold chain), water supply and sanitation, waste management, and information technology. The standard will ensure a reliable supply of technology to enable effective surveillance of climate-sensitive health conditions. It will also build upon Save the Children's guidance on building climate-resilient infrastructure in schools[7]. This standard will be used as a guideline for the development of national standards for healthcare physical infrastructure and public facilities. This standard will be produced through a consultative process, and training on the national standard will be provided to relevant stakeholders at district level (including MHN) and nationally at national level. The MoH will be able to use the standard when commissioning/upgrading infrastructure throughout the country.

Activity 2.1.2 Strengthen climate resilience of healthcare facilities

70. This activity will apply the standards developed under Activity 2.1.1 to build the climate resilience of a range of health facilities in the six project districts. Physical interventions will be in line with Save the Children Australia's E3S Category 4[8] and WHO guidance on building climate-resilient infrastructure, as well as other relevant guidelines in order to enhance climate resilience (i.e., solar energy for vaccine cold chain, lighting, water pumping and cooling fans), and is expected to result in a reduction of operational costs.

71. As part of the installation of equipment, suitable health facility staff will be trained in basic operations and maintenance for the solar and WASH equipment; and local artisans who can conduct larger-scale repairs will be developed. This activity will also provide training on how to carry out regular inspections, as well as replacement parts and complete maintenance tasks beyond the technical capacity of local artisans. Infrastructure management plans will be developed for each facility that includes a schedule of maintenance activities for solar and WASH equipment at health facilities.

72. To support this activity, a tool for assessing climate resilience of healthcare facilities using the guidance produced under Activity 2.1.1. This tool can be used by healthcare facilities in other districts and nationally to screen their current facilities

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

### Activity 2.1.3 Build capacity of Malawi’s health sector to apply the climate-resilient healthcare facility standard

The transformative potential of this project beyond the target districts will be augmented by providing training in the application of the climate-resilient healthcare facility standard (CRHCS) for all new and existing facilities, as well as capacity building. Activity 2.1.1 and Activity 2.1.2: Training will take place through the MoH at national level and will include national MoH staff, provincial health officers, district health officers, and other relevant stakeholders. The training will also target the non-government healthcare sector (i.e., private not-for-profit institutions) which serve up to a quarter of Malawians in certain areas, as well as the small number of private for-profit institutions. Staff visits to health facilities across all regions, national level and non-target districts to resilient healthcare facilities upgraded by the project will also be arranged.

### Activity 2.1.4 Develop guidelines for climate-resilient WASH facilities

Beyond healthcare facilities, there are other public buildings where climate-resilient WASH is necessary, including government offices, schools, and community centers. The project will develop climate-resilient WASH facilities and retrofit existing facilities in public buildings, following a consultative process that will include marginalized groups such as women, children, the elderly, people with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups. The guidelines will consider the most environmentally-appropriate and economically-efficient methods according to the local context. The project will also develop a training manual for WASH staff at national level, and in project districts, to apply the guidelines will be built. This activity will also advocate for the inclusion of climate-resilient WASH facilities in the construction of new public buildings.

### Activity 2.1.5 Upgrade WASH facilities at schools to improve children’s health under climate change

Building on the guidance developed under Activity 2.1, this activity will implement climate-resilient WASH solutions at public schools across the 25 target TAs, in selected villages. To enhance safe water supply (to build resilience to the effects of climate change), the project will provide rainwater harvesting systems and/or boreholes, as well as the necessary filtration devices for providing safe drinking water. In addition, small-scale water treatment solutions for existing boreholes will be provided where appropriate. The project will also ensure that all schools have access to clean water is crucial to reducing the transmission of diseases such as cholera, and for sufficient hydration in the face of higher temperatures. The improvement of WASH facilities at schools will enable teachers, parents, and other people in the community to use these facilities more frequently than healthcare facilities; maintenance committees, responsible for the upkeep of the WASH facilities, will be established or/and strengthened at each school, and water education campaigns will be conducted.

### OUTCOME 3: Healthcare staff are able to deliver service and care in the context of changing climate risk

This outcome will build the capacity of healthcare staff to understand how climate risk will alter public health risks, how they can inform surveillance of conditions, how they can interpret and disseminate climate-informed E-WARN messages, and how they can provide appropriate advice on adaptation measures. It also aims to strengthen the capacity of healthcare facilities to respond for addressing climate risks in the sector. For full details of this Outcome, refer to Annex 2 (section 6.1.7).

### Output 3.1 Healthcare staff trained in managing climate-related disease monitoring, health messaging, and disease treatment and prevention

Activity 3.1.1: Build data collection capacity to strengthen surveillance of climate-related diseases

## Data analysis of technicians working in healthcare facilities within the districts and HSGAs working in communities will be empowered to understand what type of surveillance inputs are required to augment the existing District Health Information System (DHIS) data collection tools. This will be done through a series of workshops, training sessions, and project district visits, to inform the design of a training course which will be delivered at national level to all relevant district data analysts and trainers. Subsequent district level trainings will be undertaken in each district to cascade the training to local HSGAs.

### Activity 3.1.2 Build knowledge and capacity among district and community healthcare staff on climate change and health and use of EARS of BWS

This activity will train healthcare staff at district and community level on climate impacts on health, including how to understand and interpret surveillance data developed for the EARS of BWS. This will be the primary responsibility of this staff to disseminate such information to the local (community) level. Since there are regularly new entrants into the health workforce in Malawi, a cadre of national trainers will be established that can be deployed as necessary. This cadre of trainers is likely to be three individuals per district and will be drawn from the existing cadre of HSGAs. The training will be gender representative. Trainings will then be run from healthcare facilities, covering resident staff and the HSGAs that work with them. These trainings will also include a component on how to use EARS of BWS data in conjunction with Community Health Action Groups (CHAGs), which are both essential and existing staff at the community level.

Further training will be provided by the district health management team, including the District Health Officer (DHO) and other relevant personnel. This training will include a component on how to use EARS of BWS data in conjunction with Community Health Action Groups (CHAGs), which are both essential and existing staff at the community level.

### Activity 3.1.3 Provide medical supplies and technologies for climate health risk reduction and response

Climate change creates additional needs for medical supplies and technologies to manage the impacts on disease burdens and health; this activity will support meeting the additional needs for medical supplies and technologies to manage these impacts. This includes procuring, storing, and managing the supply chain of these items as well as reviewing and modifying as necessary the processes for identifying and procuring the annual needs for treatment and prevention of climate-sensitive diseases in the context of the national health plan. The activity will also support the additional prevention needs for managing malaria, including malaria prophylaxis via Long Lasting Insecticidal Nets (LLINs) and other preventive measures such as insecticide-treated bed nets (ITNs), which are provided at no cost to households by healthcare facilities. This activity will target children in the T&G of two districts for implementation of SMTC, benefiting the request of the Ministry of Health, Government of Malawi.

1. This activity will provide additional supplies of Oral Rehydration Solution and Zinc treatments through the health care facilities in all 25 target TAs, because while they are essential for supportive treatment during cholera and diarrhea outbreaks, current levels are inadequate and cannot meet the increased need that will result from climate-related events (Annex 2, section 5.3).

2. This activity will further deliver therapeutic feeding for treatment of acute childhood malnutrition (Ready-to-Use Therapeutic Food, RUTF) to children under five years old who screen positive on the Rapid Screening Tool for Moderate Acute Malnutrition), which will be provided on the basis of screening and identification by HSA using assessments based on anthropometric measurements. This activity is expected to respond to the nutritional treatment needs that result from climate changes (Annex 2, sections 5.3 and 4).

The Health Service ATMS – the USAID program has a significant role to play in this regard by ensuring maximum-efficiency. ### Activity 2.1.4 Equip healthcare workers with MHPSS capacity to address mental health impacts of changing climate

3. Considering the growing evidence that climate risk poses additional threats to mental health, this activity aims to provide training in Mental Health and Psychosocial Support (MHPSS) for all Health Surveillance Assistants (HSAs) and Senior Health Surveillance Assistants (SHSAs), to identify and provide better Mental Health and Psychosocial Support (MHPSS) to communities. This includes the assessment of current practice versus standard MHPSS, as well as training in MHPSS for HSAs and SHSAs. The training will be conducted by mental health professionals (MHFs) in the target districts, including assessing the extent to which healthcare workers understand the links between climate change and mental health.

4. This assessment will inform the development of contextually-appropriate capacity-building materials adapted from existing resources such as the Mental Health Action Plan (MHAP). The training will be conducted by MHFs with experience in providing MHPSS, and will focus on the intersection of MHPSS, maternal and child health, and reproductive health. The training will also cover the importance of mental health for women working in PHC and young child health, specifically addressing the intersection between MHPSS, maternal and child health, and reproductive health. The training will be conducted by MHFs with experience in providing MHPSS.

5. This activity aims to provide training in Sexual Reproductive Health (SRH) services. Training materials will focus on increasing the capacity of healthcare workers to respond appropriately to the increasing need for MHPSS in PHC and SRH services, as well as the importance of mental health for women working in PHC and young child health. The training will be conducted by MHFs with experience in providing MHPSS.

6. This activity aims to provide training in Sexual Reproductive Health (SRH) services. Training materials will further address how healthcare workers can use short-term and longer-term weather and climate information to prepare for expected changes in the incidence of certain diseases, conditions, and health-seeking behavior as a result of changing climate and mental health and well-being.

7. These activities aim to provide training in Sexual Reproductive Health (SRH) services. Training materials will further address how healthcare workers can use short-term and longer-term weather and climate information to prepare for expected changes in the incidence of certain diseases, conditions, and health-seeking behavior as a result of changing climate and mental health and well-being.

8. As with Activity 2.1.2, the regular entry of health care providers into the system will necessitate that training is run regularly. In addition, a cadre of national trainers will be trained to ensure that training will be necessary. These trainers will be drawn from within the Ministry of Health and placed on rotation of staff trained in MHPSS. Embedding these staff within the Ministry will allow them to be deployed for further training as staff is necessary.

### Activity 2.1.5 Build capacity among district and community health care staff to address the gendered impacts of climate change

9. Reflecting the evidence that climate changes impact GBV, CEFM and Sexual and Reproductive Health and Rights (SRHR) (Annex 2, Sections 3.3.4, 3.3.5 and 3.3.6), this activity aims to expand the capacity of health care staff in PHC and SRH services to address gendered impacts of climate change.

## OUTCOME 4.1: Equip community structures to reduce health risks from climate change.

### Activity 4.1.1: Equip community structures to provide knowledge and skills for climate-resilient WASH facilities to community members.
88. This assessment will inform the development of a contextually appropriate training module on climate and GBV, CEFM and SRHR in the community. The training will be conducted by the district health team and community level will be trained within the training occurring under Activity 3.1.2. to reduce traffic fatigue, using the WHO-developed curriculum for GBV, CEFM and SRHR. This training will cover topics such as climate change information to prepare for expected increases in GBV, CEFM and loss of access to quality SRH services, based on the current state of practice relating to GBV, CEFM and SRHR. It is known that droughts can lead to spikes in CEFM; health care providers will be made aware of this association so that when the need arises they are prepared for the expected increase in gender-related impacts. When GBV/CCEFMSRHR/GBV risk assessments are conducted, if there are any concerns about existing or potential gaps in services that exist in the community and/or other government institutions, health care workers will be trained on how to refer patients appropriately.

89. Recognizing that climate risk will increase the number of girls and women in need of treatment for GBV, CEFM and SRHR, and that these conditions may also be exacerbated by climate-related events (Annex 2, section 3.1), this activity will also review current practices for delivering services to GBV survivors, including the use of trauma-informed care, and propose modifications as necessary, based on climate information and expected risks. This activity will also review current practices in CEFM and SRHR service delivery and propose modifications of greatest need based on the assessment of the current state of practice relating to GBV, CEFM and SRHR, and on the perspectives with women and girls.

### OUTCOME 4.1: Community awareness is realized as a result of changing climate risk.
90. This outcome will address the need to increase community awareness, skills and competencies on climate risks to health, and strengthen the capacities of communities to better manage their risk through collective action. It addresses the need for increased awareness among vulnerable groups in the project districts, particularly women and children, being more vulnerable to climate-related health impacts. Capacity strengthening training will be provided by the district health team and community level will be trained within the training occurring under Activity 3.1.2., using the WHO-developed curriculum for GBV, CEFM and SRHR. The training will engage communities in collective action, social and behaviour change, and inclusive and participatory training methods to ensure that all members of the community are involved. The training will be facilitated by district-level government departments and staff as well as Malawian academic institutions and international experts, as appropriate.

## Activity A1.2 Embed understanding of early warnings and alert protocols within communities, including children

92. This activity focuses on sensitizing communities to the meaning of health EARWS messages and appropriate responses to these messages. Sensitization and community engagement will be conducted through multiple channels to maximize reach and impact. The key components are:

* **Community-based training:** Community members will be trained in understanding, interpreting, and responding appropriately to health EARWS messages.
* **Awareness raising:** Awareness campaigns will be conducted using various media such as radio, television, social media, and community meetings.

93. This activity will be conducted in an up-down analysis of which stakeholders involved in early warnings of various types (e.g., civil protection committees, disaster risk reduction teams) are most appropriate to target for training. These activities will be complementary to existing efforts. District-specific plans will then be developed by the implementing partner in collaboration with district authorities and other relevant stakeholders.

94. Schools will be targeted through awareness raising of early warning messages. School-based health workers can be trained on how to disseminate and guide students on interactions for teachers, how to integrate this into the existing curriculum. This is partly because children need to understand early warning alerts for their own health and safety as well as that of others in their community. The school-based health worker can also provide information to younger siblings and families passing crucial information to adults, considering low levels of literacy in some communities.

95. Recognizing the difficulty of 'top-down' communication to vulnerable groups, dedicated attention will be paid to engaging with these groups through a variety of channels including:

* **Community radio:** Targeting children and youth.
* **School-based health workers:** Providing information on climate change and related health risks.
* **Traditional Healers:** Reaching people with disabilities.

96. Once healthcare staff have been trained (in OCP 3.1), they will be able to partner in similar training that targets and engages vulnerable groups, including children and youth, through a variety of channels as described above for communicating climate change and related health risks. This will include engagement with Traditional Healers, who remain influential health actors at the community level.

97. The range of public health risk from climate change is variable and often socially differentiated. This activity will also provide training to healthcare staff on how to communicate climate change-related health risks by considering the nature of climate risk to their health and that of their family members. This tool will cover a range of social and behavior change effects that will occur in the district. Efforts could include public campaigns on climate and health risk thresholds, such as heatwaves, floods, droughts, and storms. These campaigns can be supported by project districts distributing relevant information on early warning (using materials produced as part of Activity A1.2.1) as well as other messages related to mental health, maternal and child health, GHR, SRHR, and cardiovascular, respiratory and adverse pregnancy and birth outcomes). Likewise, the district will also provide training for healthcare staff in how to communicate climate change-related health content among certain community health leaders leading to community household and collective action.

98. The district will ensure that all relevant stakeholders are aware of the importance of communicating climate and health content into other fora and channels. This includes programmes and community training materials

## Activity 4.1.4 - Support families with pregnant and breastfeeding women and children under two to produce complementary foods for their children

### Background
99. Existing development and adaptation deficits mean that chronic food and nutrition insecurity exists in Malawi, and the first few days from when a child is conceived until the age of six months are critical periods for ensuring adequate food and nutrition. To address these current deficits, this activity will target vulnerable groups, e.g., households with pregnant women, breastfeeding mothers, and/or children under 2 by, supporting household to produce complementary foods for their children using locally available ingredients and resources (e.g., seeds, water, and other available foods) to support their and their young children’s daily diets. As dictated by community needs, this activity may also include the production of food for sale in order to generate income for the households involved.

### Activity 4.1.5 - Strengthen communities’ capacities to reduce their vulnerability to the health impacts of climate change

#### Background
100. This activity recognises the complex intersection of underlying causes of malnutrition and thus adopts integrated approaches that are based on community needs, e.g., food production, nutrition education, and improved farming (which includes integrating plants, trees and small livestock around the home), and soil and water management practices. The project will also support communities to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices.

101. However, this activity recognises that there are many barriers to adopting these practices that can be addressed by community members themselves. To address these barriers, the project will conduct a review of the integrated farming model and programme, modifying it to be stronger on climate resilience and more inclusive of women and youth. The project will also support communities in developing an integrated farming model, based on the assessment, an intervention strategy will be created at village level involving 500 villages. Proven health centre, community groups, and household approaches will be used to support integrated farming models that are climate-resilient and inclusive of women and youth.

102. Climate impacts affect men, women, children, youth and vulnerable populations differently. Community-led approaches that include the participation of all members of society are required so that boys, girls, women with disabilities who have equal access to health care services can be better protected from the impacts of climate change.

103. To ensure greater gender equality and social inclusion in health outcomes, as well as supporting those most affected by climate change impacts to adapt and respond, the project will adopt a community-based adaptation approach that includes the participation of all members of society. This will involve engaging men, boys, women, girls, youth, representatives of health service providers, government and relevant civil society organizations in discussions (a series of focus group discussions) on how communities can better prepare for climate change impacts and what the future might look like. The sessions require participatory facilitation and being conducted with segregated groups who work separately (groups of men, boys; women, girls; etc.) and then come together to share action plans and priorities

## B

### Activities:

The project will design and develop manuals and discussion guides for different engagement groups, train facilitators to facilitate participatory group discussions using adult learning principles, establish different engagement groups in each of the three countries, and provide training and support to these groups through an iterative process that culminates with all groups brought together to reach consensus on desired community actions. The project will also provide a platform for each action. Training and support will be provided to these parties to equip them with necessary skills to carry out their roles effectively.

This approach, communities will identify the socio-cultural barriers/enablers, resources, risk factors, especially for those most vulnerable, and develop strategies to address these issues. The project will also provide training on how to identify bottlenecks to accessing services – and will work with internal and external partners to address these barriers. This kind of approach is a combination of having the information, discussing it through two-way communication at different levels of the community, and then taking action. It is an empowering approach that can be used by communities to make positive choices, collectively and individually, while addressing underlying social norms that ultimately leads to changes in behavior. The project will also provide training on how to identify bottlenecks to accessing services – and will work with internal and external partners to address these barriers.

### 104. Discussions will have a particular focus on gender-based violence prevention, gender norms, joint household decision-making, and the role of men in family planning. These discussions are designed to provide a safe space for men to work together to identify harmful attitudes, beliefs and practices and to develop alternative approaches that can be used by communities to address these issues.

### 105. By addressing these underlying social norms, it is expected that the project will lead to reductions in child mortality rates, as well as improvements in maternal health. These interventions have also been shown to lead to reductions in malnutrition and improvements in maternal and child health, thus complementing Activity 4.1.4. Men can be encouraged to participate more actively in family planning discussions and decision-making processes for children and mothers, equitable control of money and resources in the family, food sharing and consumption, encouraging women’s empowerment.

### 106. Finally, these interventions can also lead to improvements in mental health, thus complementing Activity 3.1.4, as men are more likely than women to suffer from depression and anxiety disorders. These interventions will also improve couples’ communication and relationships around the care of children. Stigma comprises a major barrier to accessing sexual and reproductive health services in developing countries, particularly among vulnerable members of the population including the poor and women.

### 107. This intervention will draw on Save the Children’s work in Eastern and Southern Africa including Malawi. Mobilizing resources for this activity is expected to be most cost-effective, will be supported with this intervention, the outcomes of which will be used for learning purposes.
