## The Save the Children Fund (Save the Children UK or SCUK are trade names and abbreviations) on the executing entity responsible for channeling funds internationally.

109. SCA, as the FGD, will enter into legally binding subsidiary agreements (subsidiary agreements) with the three Executing Agencies (EA) of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in Malawi: the Children International, Malawi Country Office (SCI MW) and both the national channeling executing entity in Malawi and as implementing executing entity, and the Ministry of Health (MOH). These subsidiary agreements will be signed by SCI MW on behalf of GCF. They will enter into a delivery contract with Catholic Health Commission (CHC) and Creative Center for Community Mobilization (CCCM) to deliver the project.

### Co-financing - The sources of co-financing (i.e., donors – FCOID, Foundation 5, GSK, and MoH Foundation and I in-kind contribution from the Government of Malawi) will be managed by the three EEs with SCI MW managing co-financing for the CHC and CCCM. Co-financing from FCOID will be channelled through the MOH Health Sector Joint Fund (HSJF) to SCI MW, which will then enter into a delivery contract with CHC and CCCM. Co-financing from Foundation 5 GSK and MoH Foundation will be channelled directly to SCI MW. Co-financing from I in-kind contribution from the Government of Malawi will be channelled through the HSJF governance documents in place between FCOID and MOH. Co-financing from Foundation 5, GSK and MoH Foundation will be directed to SCI MW. The funds will be directly disbursed to communities in cash. Co-financing from the Government of Malawi will be disbursed by the HSJF governance documents in place with FCOID and MOH. SCI MW will enter into a subsidiary agreement with the SCA and GCF which will include the terms of the in-kind contribution.

110. The National Designated Authority for the Green Climate Fund in Malawi is the Environmental Affairs Department (EAD) under the Ministry of Environment, Energy and Tourism (MEET). The EAD will be responsible for convening the Project Steering Committee described below, as well as maintaining a working relationship with the GCF through regular communications and meetings.

## Executive Summary

111. SCI MW is registered as an international Non-Governmental Organisation under the Non-Governmental Organisations Regulatory Authority (NGORA) in Malawi and has legal status in the country since its initial registration under the NGO Act of 2004. SCI MW has been working with the MOH at national level to oversee the entire project through a Project Implementation Unit, which is led by the Country Director. SCI MW will continue to work closely with the MOH at district and sub-district levels in order to ensure that the project is implemented effectively. SCI MW will also be responsible for delivering community-based health education, including gender transformative and child-centred approaches in its restorative programming. SCI MW can receive and release GCF funds directly from the FGD. SCI MW has a strong track record of working with the MOH at national level to ensure that projects are implemented effectively. SCI central office holds donor income and funds that are transferred to SCI Malawi based on realized project expenditures. The SCI central office will also be responsible for ensuring that all GCF funds are used in accordance with the international channeling executing entity for the project and will provide account management support to SCI Malawi.

112. The Ministry of Health (MOH) is the implementing executing entity, and its mission is: To provide strategic leadership for the delivery of a comprehensive range of quality, accessible, and efficient health services to all Malawians through the creation and implementation of policies, strategies, guidelines, and standards that are in line with national priorities. The MOH will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented effectively at district level within the Ministry will be Preventive Health Services. In recent years, the Preventive Health Services Department has developed a number of initiatives to improve health services delivery in Malawi. These include the National Maternal and Child Health (NACH) Project (Section B3, and Annex 2, section 2.1.5). Working through the preventive health directorate will ensure that the project is implemented effectively at district level within the Ministry.

113. The Ministry of Health will lead and provide oversight for most of the coordination and capacity building activities at national and district level; especially building capacity of Government of Malawi staff – for example, on improving health (WHM) and climate change adaptation (CCA) services delivery. These activities are expected to be delivered through Adaptation Actions (1.1.2). These activities make up almost all of Outcome 1, a majority of Outcome 3, and development of the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) (Outcome 4). The project will also contribute towards infrastructure improvements in health facilities (2.1.2).

### B

#### 114. In 2015, the Fund was supported by three bilateral donors** established a project management structure called the Health Sector Joint Unit (HSJU), to support the MoH's implementation of the Health Sector Strategic Plan (HSSP). It is a joint venture between the Ministry of Finance and the MoH. The HSJU is funded through a parallel funds flow (no cash from the MoH) with strong fiduciary and procurement arrangements in place. The HSJU will support the MoH to develop the Development Plans, monitor progress towards achieving the HSSP goals, and provide technical assistance to the MoH for implementing the HSSP procedures. The HSJU therefore allows the double benefit of using Government systems for planning, budgeting, procurement and reporting, but also provides a parallel funds flow (no cash from the MoH) with strong fiduciary and procurement arrangements in place.

The principal operationalizing the HSJU are set out in the Joint Declaration of intent for the HSJU and the Communique of the 1st Meeting of the HSJU Management Committee on 20th October, 2015. The Fund is also supported by the Fiduciary Agent (FA), a private sector entity established to manage the funds under the HSJU. The FA was appointed by the Government in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the Memorandum of Understanding between the MoH and the Development Partners of 1 December, 2015. ### Other parties

116. In addition to the two Executive Entities, the project will be delivered by two procured parties, operating across the six target districts who have been determined by a rigorous assessment. The two parties are CARE Health Component (CHC) and Community Health Workers (CHWs) of the Mwamba Mobile Clinic Organization (MMCO), all of which are registered NGOs in Malawi under NGOIRA. The two parties were identified through a comprehensive mapping exercise, using the Participatory Assessment Tool (PAT), and the two parties were chosen due to their suitability for selected activities, combined with their experience working within the project districts. Both parties have extensive experience implementing health activities and deliverables as the water project. The budget for each implementing partner is clearly defined and there will be a clear delineation of roles and responsibilities between the two parties in order to ensure that all activities are completed and select the final beneficiaries.

117. CHC has implemented several national level interventions in Lilongwe, opening through District commissions in 6 districts across Malawi, covering all six districts in the country. They coordinate and implement health facility-based care for pregnant women and children under five years of age (PMTCT, ANC, IYCF, etc.). The assessment found that they have a strong governance structure and adequate financial systems and controls in place. CHC will lead on community-based activities in Outcomes 3 and 4 particularly, leading on the rollout of additional treatment for TB, HIV, and malaria; strengthening health facilities to improve quality of care; improving access to resident WASH facilities (2:1:1), and the nutrition component for mothers of children under 2 years old (activity 4.1.4). The CHC will also lead on the implementation of the water project in the Southern district of Zomba (one of the target project districts) with offices in other districts across southern Malawi, including Lilongwe, Phalombe, and Machinga.

118. CMMCO has implemented several national level interventions implementing public health projects. SCI MMW has worked with CRECCOM previously and has observed their strong governance structure and adequate financial systems and controls in place. They will lead on the Southern district of Zomba (one of the target project districts) with offices in other districts across southern Malawi, including Lilongwe, Phalombe, and Machinga.

119. Both organisations will be present in all six districts. These two partners will act as implementing partners for SCI MMW who will lead on community-based activities, with funds flowing directly from GCF to the two parties based on the budget allocated to the activities they will carry out. For more information, see Annex 4: Detailed Budget.

B


---
**Page 39**
---

120\. A Financial Management Capacity Assessment (FMCA) of the Ministry of Health was undertaken in March 2023. The overall results of the assessment were mixed, with some positive findings balanced by a number of strong caveats that could impact delivery of the project. Positive results of the assessment included:

- a well-staffed finance department, with adequate staffing in place and a clear organisational hierarchy in place,
- structural arrangements for financial management are in place,
- experience implementing a range of donor-funded projects, including with the World Bank, Global Fund, and USAID,
- functional internal audit systems which are operating regularly to provide regular checks on project and expenditure management,
- the existence of a dedicated internal finance compliance unit, providing additional checks to the audit department,
- an effective procurement function in place, supporting ongoing expenditure transactions and ensuring accountability for underperforming finance staff.

121\. The FMCA also identified several areas where the Ministry of Health is operating outside what was recommended within the FMCA report. These will be mitigated through the implementation arrangements which will support government capacity to manage future climate finance. One key issue uncovered by the assessment, as well as being mentioned by the technical team in their review of the project proposal, relates to the lack of a dedicated Procurement Unit (PU) at the MOH. This meant that procurement was often delayed. To mitigate potential procurement challenges, the Ministry of Health and CGI Management Services will establish a dedicated Procurement Unit within the MOH. The PU will be responsible for managing procurements through Save the Children for all large procurements including all goods and non-contracting services. This includes procuring equipment and supplies required to support the project activities as well as other procurement requirements, with oversight of smaller consultancy procurements (with an absolute maximum value of US$80,000 – most fall below US$50,000). The PU will be responsible for ensuring that all procurements are in line with the MOH’s procurement policy and existing Ministry policies to ensure all guidelines and rules are adhered to, and the Ministry will use the International Development Association (IDA) Procurement Guidelines as a reference. The PU will also be responsible for establishing thresholds for disease burden related E-WARD. Procurement for this activity will be managed by Save the Children in accordance with their procurement policy and existing MOH guidelines and policies.

For more information about financial risks and mitigations see Annex 20: First level AML/CFT assessment. Procurement detail is available in Annex 10 and section G3.


### Project Management and Staffing Structure

122\. At the request of Save the Children, managed by a Project Implementation Unit (PIU), which will consist of newly-hired project staff and Government staff on secondment to the PIU from Ministry of Health. The PIU will be hosted by the Ministry of Health in its headquarters building in Port Moresby. The PIU will be led by a Chief Executive Officer (mainly line) to the Director of Preventive Health Services, MoH). Staff at the PIU will be contracted by Save the Children. The PIU will have a dedicated Project Manager and a Technical Advisor for Solar and WASH. A Climate and Health Compliance Manager (with responsibility for compiling financial reports across the Executing Entities and partners, as well as working closely with the Fiscal Agent) for the project. A dedicated Procurement Coordinator will also sit at the technical team to ensure that all procurements are in line with the MOH’s procurement policy and existing guidelines and policies. The PIU will be government secondments.

The technical team will include a Climate and Health Technical Advisor, a GESI Advisor, and a Technical Specialist for Solar and WASH to oversee the physical infrastructure improvements.

- **Role**: Overall project management, including financial reporting for the Ministry component.
- **Support**: PIU staff and Field Officers working directly with implementing partners in Traditional Authority (TA) levels.

## Financial Transactions

- **Accessed via**: MoH bank account linked to GCF funds.

### Project Funds
- **Source**: National Health Fund (NHF), Children's Fund, and other sources as per the project plan.
- **Management**: PIU staff will manage dedicated bank accounts for each project component.

### Field Officers
- **Role**: Implementing partners in TA levels.
- **Support**: Community mobilizers to ensure effective implementation at the community level.

who are expected to be based at the TA in all TAs and will not require dedicated office space. In addition to the national-level PSC, there will be a Project Steering Committee (PSC) comprising Permanent Secretariat of the Council of Ministers (CSM), Ministry of Health, Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MNRE), Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (MALF), Ministry of Community Development and Social Welfare and the Department of Disaster Management Affairs. In addition to the government representatives, there will be two EE members representing Save the Children and the MOH, which will manage day-to-day operations. The PSC will be co-chaired by two of the EE’s being SCI MoH and the MOH.

The AE will be responsible for the project’s implementation and will meet regularly with the PSC to discuss progress and challenges. The AE will also hold regular meetings with the core project team, including the Chief of Party and the Country Director of SCI MoH. This way, the AE will be updated on all key decision points, both during and ahead of time, so that they can inform the implementing partners about any changes in direction or priorities. The AE will also hold a kick-off meeting with the core project team session during the project inception period on the AMA and the FAA so that all partners understand their responsibilities prior to the start of implementation.

No financial flows will take place between SCI MoH and the PSC; the working relationship will be solely based on reporting and coordination. The AE will report directly to the TA, who in turn will report to the PSC.

128. Finally, there will be a Technical Advisory Group (TAG), which will meet more regularly – at least monthly - than the PSC. The TAG will consist of EE members representing Save the Children, SCI MoH, and partners (e.g., CFS, CSi Health Network, WHO), and or financiers (GSK, Foundation 50). The role of the TAG will be to advise the PSC on project implementation issues, including impacts of EWRAS, implementation of medical treatment and supplies, and health and nutrition interventions under Outcome 4.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1,000 words, approximately 2 pages)

129. Malawi has developed plans for adaptation financing and a National Climate Change Fund is currently under development. The total estimated cost of the NAP and associated projects are estimated to be US$45.4 billion through to 2040, with around one third in the current decade, and two thirds in the decade from 2031-40. The NAP implementation plan is based on a phased approach, with the first phase (2018-2020) being funded for three quarters (76%) conditional on international support and that the bulk of the support is required over the coming decade.

This reflects the fact that the majority of the costs are in the later years of the NAP. The total estimated cost of the NAP and associated projects are estimated to be US$45.4 billion through to 2040, with around one third in the current decade, and two thirds in the decade from 2031-40.

The projected costs for adaptation are also on order of magnitude of difference from different available financing, with less than 1% of the national budget made available for both environment and climate change activities. Current estimates indicate that the total cost of NAP implementation is US$45.4 billion through to 2040, with around one third in the current decade, and two thirds in the decade from 2031-40.

Costs USD4.76 billion of GCF readiness funds have been allocated. There has been one dedicated adaptation project (project ID: 1000000000) under the GCF Readiness and Project Facilitation component, which is a total cost of US$25 million (which is coming to an end); and Malawi is part of the multi-country Cooling Fund (PFIT7) which is cross-sectoral in nature. The NAP has been developed with support from the Global Environment Facility (GEF), which has provided funding for the development of the NAP, as well as a total cost of US$10 million for 2018-2020 and climate change focal area projects under implementation with a value of US$442.675, with further projects at the stage of preparation and/or implementation (total value US$39.5 million). The NAP also has a total cost of US$2 billion which is anticipated to be needed to be met by international support under the NAP Framework and updated NDC.

131. It also comes against a backdrop of increasing economic costs of climate change through extreme events. At the end of the last decade, Malawi was losing approximately 1.4% GPD annually to the combined effects of droughts and floods.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WIND ENERGY PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 40

## Background
- **Floods**: More than five times higher than the average for Least Developed Countries of 3.6%.
- Five of the last nine years have had national disasters declared due to extreme weather events, all of which led to extensive economic losses in Malawi.
- In 2015, there was a 4-year drought with Cyclone Idai (costing 0.13% GDP), in 2016 a severe drought linked with El Niño caused a food and humanitarian crisis (costing 0.8% GDP).
- In 2015 there was a 1–5 year flood in January (costing 0.3% of GDP). In 2023, Cyclone Freddy cost $4 billion.
- The country is highly vulnerable to climate change and extreme weather events.
- Climate forecasts suggest increasing confidence in a strong El Niño by late 2023, and therefore poorly distributed rainfall across the country.

## Project Overview
- **Project Title**: Green Climate Fund Wind Energy Proposal V3.0
- **Funding Source**: Green Climate Fund (GCF)
- **Country**: Malawi
- **Implementation Period**: 2024–2029
- **Total Cost**: $1 billion
- **Funding Requested from GCF**: $500 million

## Project Objectives and Activities
- **Objective 1**: Increase renewable energy capacity in Malawi.
- **Objective 2**: Strengthen the institutional framework for climate change adaptation.

### Key Results Indicators (KRI)
- **KRI 1.1**: Increased renewable energy capacity by 50%.
- **KRI 1.2**: Improved institutional capacity to manage climate risks and disasters.
- **KRI 1.3**: Enhanced community resilience to extreme weather events.

## Project Implementation
- **Project Duration**: 6 years (2024–2030)
- **Implementation Period**: 2024–2029

### Project Activities
- **Activity 1**: Wind Energy Development
- **Activity 2**: Institutional Strengthening
- **Activity 3**: Community Resilience Building

## Financial Plan
- **Total Cost**: $1 billion
- **Funding Requested from GCF**: $500 million
- **Other Funding Sources**: Domestic resources, international partners

### Project Timeline
- **2024–2026**: Project Preparation and Implementation
- **2027–2029**: Project Execution and Monitoring
- **2030**: Project Completion and Evaluation

## Conclusion
The project aims to significantly reduce Malawi's vulnerability to climate change by increasing renewable energy capacity, strengthening institutional frameworks, and building community resilience. The proposed GCF grant is provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study, Sections 2–4 and in Annex 3: Economic analysis.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Long-term ownership – The project also builds on previous learning on building sustainable local capacity — which has been a key lesson from the pilot phase of the project. This will be achieved by ensuring that there is potential high turnover of government staff, and to ensure a scalded approach that ultimately results in an overall increase in capacity for climate change adaptation at all levels of government. The project will also build on a legacy of local capacity. This will be enabled by training local cadres of trainers (on early warning messages alongside other key skills) who can then train others within their communities and beyond, including those outside the project, but after the project — thereby the need for an exit strategy that would require it deliver a sustainable model for climate change adaptation in the long-term. The project will also build on a strong mass understanding of climate and health risk at national and local level, and within and outside government. This will be achieved by ensuring that the project is embedded into the national disaster risk reduction system, and that the project builds on existing community-based early warning systems. Health facilities and public WASH facilities will further support continuity. Sustainability will also be achieved through building on the strong commitment of the Ministry of Health to ensure that climate change adaptation resource allocation within government budgets and by the private sector.

## Community engagement – The project will be designed in such a way as to ensure that communities are central to the design of the activities, ensuring that provision of infrastructure is embedded in a strong understanding of its importance and relevance. This will be achieved through engaging with communities at all levels (from national to local) and involving communities in the planning and implementation of interventions complemented by community social mobilization and capacity building. The project will also build on existing community-based early warning systems as a platform for continued feedback to the project. Engaging communities is also key for norm shifting, addressing misinformation and ensuring that the project is embedded into national disaster risk reduction system. This will lead to dialogue and reflection on power, and vis-à-vis to ensuring the interventions remain locally-led and responsive to the needs of the community. The project will also build on existing community-based early warning systems and manage their resources ensures sustainability.

### Operations and Maintenance plan (to ensure long-term effectiveness of infrastructure investments) – Provision for maintenance and operations is critical in order to ensure that the infrastructure investments made (e.g., provision of solar panels for sustainability of lighting and cold chain, and WASH facilities). Support for the maintenance and operation will be provided through a combination of technical assistance from the Ministry of Health and capacity building of health facility staff on screening for climate risk, the importance of early warning systems and the role of community-based early warning systems. To complement the system strengthening activities in the health sector, the project will strengthen resilience of communities to climate change impacts by ensuring that they are better equipped to respond to disasters. This leads in implementation, monitoring of WASH interventions in schools. The project will equip a maintenance plan for each facility and ensure that there is a clear understanding among all stakeholders on their respective responsibility for the upkeep of equipment. There will also be coordination at district level with the Ministry of Health.

### Alignment with other projects investments – The EWRAS system by WHO that will work towards consolidating and building interoperability of the existing mechanisms for early warning, disaster risk management and health systems in order to ensure a more sensitive health surveillance system. This will enable data documentation and analysis to inform the forecasting and response to climate change impacts on health. The project will also build on the existing implementation and coordination structures across the disaster risk management and health structures from the national level down to the community level, including the National Disaster Risk Management Committee (NDRMC), District Disaster Risk Management Committees (DDRMCs) and Village Civil Protection committees from disaster risk management. Area and Village Nutrition Committees will be established as part of the project in order to ensure that there is a clear understanding among all stakeholders on their respective responsibility for the upkeep of equipment. There will also be coordination at district level with the Ministry of Health.

### Evidence generation and dissemination of knowledge nationally – To ensure project benefits are captured and lessons learned, the CWHRC includes a focus on monitoring, evaluation, and knowledge sharing of best

## 137. The GCF funding will capacity building activities across the entire health system, from national level through to community level with community health volunteers and community health action groups. The capacity building includes an overview of the impacts of climate change on health, as well as more detailed training in the following areas:

- Training on data collection and surveillance for specific diseases covered in the EAWARS (district level staff)
- Training on the climate change impacts on mental health (district and sub-district level staff)
- Training on climate change impacts on gender-based violence, Child and Early Forced Marriage, and Sexual Reproductive Health (district and sub-district level staff)

The project will also work to establish clear plans with district executive committees to ensure adaptation planning is integrated into the existing health system. The plan will be developed in collaboration with the District Health office, but the broader district, with representatives from each department taking responsibility for different aspects of the plan.

138. Technology development and transfer will also apply in several outputs of the project. At district level, district hospitals will be provided with modern computers, functioning internet and software needed to link to the


---
**Page 50**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 - PAGE 47

district level (EWARS) dashboard and to the national level. At health centre level, each facility will be provided with two modern tablets to enable dedicated staff members to aggregate health data collected by HSA(s) to feed into the district level EWAR system. The electronic version of the Health Centre Information System (HCIS) health EWARDS is included in Activity 1.1.3. Staff will be trained in data entry and submitting data up to the research and information process (3.1.1 and 3.1.2).

### 140. As well as the EWARS, solar improvements and WASH improvements will be implemented at health facilities (Activity 2.1.2 – solar and WASH), and schools (2.1.5 – WASH only). Solar improvements include:

- Solar direct drive freezers and fridges
- Solar water systems, i.e., photovoltaic panels, batteries and associated equipment
- Solar lights
- Cold storage

### 141. Improvements to WASH will include:

- Rainwater harvesting systems
- Small-scale WASH solutions (clay filters, borehole improvements)
- Ecosan latrines

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The project will meet the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### D.1.1 Project impact on the GCF’s overall objective: contribution to increased climate-resilient sustainable development – by directly increasing the climate resilience of the health system in Malawi, with direct benefits for the population and indirect benefits for other sectors (e.g., agriculture). The project will benefit 2,359,162 beneficiaries (1,178,581 women and 1,179,581 men) in southern Malawi (10% of the country’s total population), a particular vulnerable region. The project will contribute to the GCF’s overall objective through three gender-sensitive and social inclusion initiatives: in activities, ensuring women, children and people with disabilities have equal opportunities to benefit. In practice this means recognition of differential vulnerability to climate-sensitive diseases (Outcome 1); providing access to health services that are responsive to the needs of vulnerable groups to respond to these challenges (Outcome 3), and it enable people themselves to identify and reduce their own risk (Outcome 4). The project will also contribute to the GCF’s overall objective by increasing the capacity of the health system to respond to climate-related food and nutrition support (Outcome 4) to reduce vulnerability to drought. The number and diversity of interventions in the project will ensure that all groups are included.

#### D.1.2 Project impact on the GCF’s overall objective: contribution to increased climate-resilient sustainable development – by directly increasing the climate resilience of the health system in Malawi, with direct benefits for the population and indirect benefits for other sectors (e.g., agriculture). The project will result in substantial reductions in the risk of loss of lives and wellbeing by focusing on disease prevention and management, as well as strengthening the capacity of the health system to respond to climate changes: Malaria, dengue diseases, malnutrition, diseases linked to heat exposure, mental health and psychosocial problems, respiratory diseases, waterborne diseases, vector-borne diseases, food insecurity-related morbidity and mortality through its focus on strengthening the capacity of the health system (including its infrastructure) to respond to climate change. The project will also contribute to the GCF’s overall objective by furthering, as most health facilities in Malawi currently do not have climate-resilient infrastructure, the project’s focus on increasing the capacity of the health system to respond to extreme weather events and thereby reduce the negative knock-on effects on health outcomes; for example, increasing the resilience of WASH facilities will help reduce the spread of cholera and diarrheal diseases. The project will also contribute to the GCF’s overall objective by increasing the capacity of the health system.

#### D.1.3 Project impact on the GCF’s overall objective: contribution to increased climate-resilient sustainable development – by directly increasing the climate resilience of the health system in Malawi, with direct benefits for the population and indirect benefits for other sectors (e.g., agriculture). The project focus is in alignment with the Guiding Principles for Financing Climate and Health Solutions developed at COP23. These principles were endorsed by 41 funders, partners and are an initiative that was an initiating party and which are endorsed by both the Ministry of Health of Malawi and Save the Children. These principles provide a framework to guide investments in climate-resilient health systems with respect to health, and build resilient, environmentally sustainable health systems, in recognition of the critical need to protect people from the adverse impacts of climate change. The project will contribute to these principles by following priority areas of the Guiding Principles for Financing Climate and Health Solutions: implementing proactive public health measures (section D.1); building adaptive capacity through a focus on early warning systems; addressing the range of climate risks to health, with an emphasis on the most vulnerable and impacted communities; building average resilience in health systems; increasing the capacity of the health system to respond to climate change preparedness and adaptive capacity; elevating country ownership and leadership (see section D.3); and catalyzing international action by mobilizing resources and leveraging national and international finance for climate and health solutions, while enabling the human and other resources needed to ensure the long-term impact of investments in climate and health solutions.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### D.2.1 Project contribution to paradigm shifts – by directly increasing the climate resilience of the health system in Malawi, with direct benefits for the population and indirect benefits for other sectors (e.g., agriculture). The project will contribute to a paradigm shift through its focus on strengthening the capacity of the health system (including its infrastructure) to respond to extreme weather events. This is particularly relevant given that most health facilities in Malawi currently do not have climate-resilient infrastructure, and by increasing the capacity of the health system to respond to climate change. The project will also contribute to a paradigm shift through its focus on implementing proactive public health measures (section D.1); building adaptive capacity through a focus on early warning systems; addressing the range of climate risks to health, with an emphasis on the most vulnerable and impacted communities; building average resilience in health systems; increasing the capacity of the health system to respond to climate change preparedness and adaptive capacity; elevating country ownership and leadership (see section D.3); and catalyzing international action by mobilizing resources and leveraging national and international finance for climate and health solutions, while enabling the human and other resources needed to ensure the long-term impact of investments in climate and health solutions.

### D3. Sustainable development (max: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed project activities will remove the transformative change as described in the Theory of Change (Section B.1), thereby enabling a step change in the implementation of climate-resilient health systems as reflected in the GCF sectoral guidance on health and well-being [2]. This will be achieved by the demonstration of integrated approaches at district level to strengthen the resilience of infrastructure such that service provision continues during extreme weather events. At each level of the project, each of the participating districts will have a DEC that underpins the nature of climate risk to health. A District Health Emergency Plan (DHEP) will be developed and implemented in each district, including a plan for medical supplies access, early warning from the EAWATs [one district surveillance is being tested], information systems, and other key elements. The DHEPs will be supported by trained staff who are able to anticipate and respond to climate risk to health, including through treatments and MHPSS, and provide care in a timely manner. This will be achieved through the development of the EAWATs at national level and training (for example on how to screen infrastructure and WASH for climate resilience). The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Climate Change Policy and institutional and enabling environment, thus catalysing impact beyond a one-off investment project.

### D3.1. Monitoring and evaluation

The proposed project activities will be underpinned by a robust MEL plan and enabled through oversight of project implementation by the Health and Climate Change Unit (HCCU) at national level, in collaboration with the National Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management [NCCDRM]. The NCCDRM will be the primary forum for knowledge sharing on climate change adaptation. The HCCU will also provide technical support to build an international network to inform health sector adoption in other countries, including through SADC climate fora and the UNFCCC COPs (through the HCCU). Support for advocacy is activity 1.2.2 builds stronger coalitions with key stakeholders at national level to ensure that the project’s lessons are shared widely and that a sharing of knowledge (e.g. a toolkit on how to develop a DHP) under activity 1.2.1.a; a tool for applying the climate resilience framework in health sector planning under activity 1.2.1.b; a guide for climate-resilient WASH under Activity 2.1.4; and a guide for climate-resilient infrastructure under activity 2.1.3; a guide for climate-resilient healthcare facilities look like under activity 2.1.3.a; a guideline for climate-resilient WASH under 2.1.4.a; and a guide for climate-resilient infrastructure under Activity 2.1.3.b; a guide for climate-resilient WASH under Activity 2.1.4.b.

### D3.2. Contribution to the regulatory environment and policies

The proposed project creates an institutional and enabling environment at national level (including the health early warning system) that will enable the implementation of the National Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management Policy [NCCDRM] and the National Health Adaptation Plan [NHAP]. The NHAP is a key policy document that sets out the strategic direction for climate change adaptation in the health sector. It provides guidance on how to integrate climate change considerations into national policies, including the National Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management Policy (2018), the Updated NCCDRM (2021) and the Health National Adaptation Plan [See Annex 2]. The NHAP also sets out a framework for action that includes the development of sectoral adaptation plans at district and sub-district level (Activity 1.2.2). These activities and the project’s broader contribution to achieving the goals set out in national policies (described below) will strengthen the enabling environment for climate resilient healthcare in Malawi.

These activities and the project’s broader contribution to achieving the goals set out in national policies (described below) will strengthen the enabling environment for climate resilient healthcare in Malawi.

#### SDG 1: The suite of healthcare interventions, including equipping communities with improved health practices and enabling better support for healthcare provision (including MHPSS) and will enable healthcare staff to deliver services in a more effective manner. Improved nutrition practices and integrated homestead farming will also contribute to reducing poverty.

SDG 2: The project interventions will work together to achieve good health and well-being at the community level through improved access to healthcare facilities, including equipping communities with improved health practices and enabling better support for healthcare provision (including MHPSS) and will enable healthcare staff to deliver services in a more effective manner. Improved nutrition practices and integrated homestead farming will also contribute to improving anticipatory procurement and provision of treatments for acute malnutrition.

SDG 3: The project interventions will work together to achieve good health and well-being at the community level through improved access to healthcare facilities, including equipping communities with improved health practices and enabling better support for healthcare provision (including MHPSS) and will enable healthcare staff to deliver services in a more effective manner. Improved nutrition practices and integrated homestead farming will also contribute to improving anticipatory procurement and provision of treatments for acute malnutrition.

SDG 4: The project interventions will work together to achieve good health and well-being at the community level through improved access to healthcare facilities, including equipping communities with improved health practices and enabling better support for healthcare provision (including MHPSS) and will enable healthcare staff to deliver services in a more effective manner. Improved nutrition practices and integrated homestead farming will also contribute to improving anticipatory procurement and provision of treatments for acute malnutrition.

SDG 5: The project interventions will work together to achieve good health and well-being at the community level through improved access to healthcare facilities, including equipping communities with improved health practices and enabling better support for healthcare provision (including MHPSS) and will enable healthcare staff to deliver services in a more effective manner. Improved nutrition practices and integrated homestead farming will also contribute to improving anticipatory procurement and provision of treatments for acute malnutrition.

SDG 6: The development of guidelines on climate-resilient WASH facilities in public buildings, combined with improved access to healthcare facilities, including equipping communities with improved health practices and enabling better support for healthcare provision (including MHPSS) and will enable healthcare staff to deliver services in a more effective manner. Improved nutrition practices and integrated homestead farming will also contribute to improving anticipatory procurement and provision of treatments for acute malnutrition.

SDG 13: The entire scope of this project is in line with SDG 13, climate action, in taking urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts through education, awareness raising and training for school children specifically on the health EAWAS and how they can reduce their risks to their health as well as improving access to early childhood development.

SDG 5: The project emphasis is gender equality and social inclusion, with a focus on ensuring the full participation of women in capacity building and employment opportunities, and access to healthcare provision that reduces poverty.

The project will also have the following co-benefits:

1. Co-benefit 1: Social improved social inclusion for marginalized groups will result from improved dissemination of information on public health, particularly reaching hard-to-reach groups including out-of-school children, the elderly and people with disabilities.
2. Co-benefit 2: Environmental: Very small-scale mitigation co-benefits through solar installations at health facilities reducing reliance on diesel generators at facilities and on the national electrical grid.

Co-benefit 3: Social inclusion: The project will contribute to the engagement of the whole community in conversations about power and gender dynamics (Activity 4.1.5). This community transformative approach will be underpinned by a gender-responsive and socially-inclusive approach that recognizes the underlying structural barriers to women's empowerment, including access to education, health services, and employment opportunities.

### D.4. Detailed Description (max. 300 words, approximately 1 page)

**155. Vulnerability of the country and/or specific vulnerable groups, including gender aspects: Malawi has high vulnerability to climate change. Among the most common climate hazards affecting Malawi are floods and droughts. The country is also highly vulnerable to extreme weather events such as cyclones, which have increased in frequency over time, with more than 5 times higher than the average for least developed countries of 0.3%**.

Changing climate and extreme weather events have a significant impact on food security and nutrition. Malawi has experienced high levels of poverty, with 48% of its population living below the national poverty line in 2019. The country also faces challenges such as low agricultural productivity and slow structural transformation.

The Integrated Food Security Phase Classification (IPC) 2020 analysis report on the chronic food insecurity status - Malawi. Environment and Development Economics 16: 177-204, doi: 10.1080/13557525.2019.1608847.

**156. Climate change is a major driver of poverty and food and nutrition insecurity and malnutrition in Malawi. The country has high vulnerability to climate change, which moves out of poverty between 2010-19, fell back into it due to the impact of weather shocks**. There is high vulnerability to climate change when natural resource base and agricultural sectors are impacted by climatic changes, chronic food insecurity household coping result with large scale migration, currently 4 million people are facing food insecurity in Malawi.

The country has a high burden of malnutrition, with 51% of children under five years old being stunted. This is over half the total population**. Climate shocks are a major driver of poverty and food and nutrition insecurity and malnutrition in Malawi. The country has high vulnerability to climate change when natural resource base and agricultural sectors are impacted by climatic changes, chronic food insecurity household coping result with large scale migration, currently 4 million people are facing food insecurity in Malawi.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 52

### Alignment to climate change adaptation strategies and policies

- **Alignment to climate change adaptation strategies and policies:** This project is entirely aligned with the National Climate Change Management Policy, and implements priorities as outlined in the National Adaptation Plan framework.
- The objective of this project is to improve the capacity of the Malawian health sector that is relevant to climate change effects. It builds on and leverages the country’s first health and adaptation strategy (2018–2030) and its alignment with the national development plan (NDP). This project will strengthen links between disease burden and climate variables under Outcome 1), strengthening the institutional framework (on Outcomes 2 & 3) and improving the capacity of the health sector to address the mental health and gender impacts of a changing climate in Outcome 4) and developing local training materials (for outcomes under Outcomes 1-4) and ensuring all components start their activities at the same time to avoid delays. The project will also leverage the improved climate change adaptation framework for the health sector.
- Alignment to gender action plans policies: - The project has been designed through a bottom-up and inclusive process, with the involvement of key stakeholders in the development of the project proposal. It is under the oversight of the HCCCT of the multi-stakeholder JNCCMAT (Malawi’s primary multi-stakeholder coordination body on climate change-related activities which is chaired by the Environmental Affairs Department as the lead).

which have been from the entire process of project design through to the nominated task team members. Both the HCCTD and JINTCCDRM comprise representatives from government, academia, civil society and the private sector.

The project design takes into account the needs that were expressed by women, children and people with disabilities. The project design takes particularly into account the needs that were expressed by women, including to support them in their roles as primary caregivers for their families and communities (activity 4.1.2), and their capacity to grow their own nutritious food to feed children (activity 4.1.4), and to ensure that climate-resilient nutrition and safe water are available at home (activities 4.1.3, 4.1.5). The project design also takes into account the needs expressed by children for climate-resilient WASH facilities in primary schools (activities 4.1.1 and 4.1.4) as well as their need to be protected from gender-based violence and other public health information (activity 4.1.3).

All arrangements are made with the Executing Departments - The HCCTD endorsed the Ministry of Health as the Executing Entity, alongside SMCW as the other Executing Entity. Implementation arrangements have been designed to ensure that the project is fully aligned with the Government’s National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), where lessons from ongoing implementation and evaluation will be shared with other relevant stakeholders.

To ensure that the project is fully aligned with the Ministry of Health and other agencies, the PMU will have staff seconded from the MoH so that their time is dedicated to project tasks like a lesson learned from GCF’s Adaptation for Africa – see Annex 2.0, section 2.5.1 for more information.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Concessionality
As outlined in Section B.5., building a climate-resilient health system is a key component of Malawi’s response to climate change. As also noted in Section B.5., accessing financing for support adaptation measures – both within and outside the health sector – at the scale required to cause transformational change has been difficult for Malawi, as well as other countries in Sub-Saharan Africa. The Government of Malawi recognizes that climate change is causing climate change, but they are among the first to suffer the impacts given Malawi’s high vulnerability and because it is a small island developing state (SIDS) and least developed country (LDC), and also because it is one of the countries with the highest per capita carbon emissions. The Government of Malawi is clear that significant external financing will be required – including specifically referencing GCF funds in their NAP – if the country is to effectively manage the unavoidable impacts


---
**Page 58**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 55

climate change there is a clear argument for high co-finance and private sector engagement in mitigation action.

The argument for poor countries to finance their own adaptation actions in response to a problem stemming from the uncontrolled emissions of rich countries is not strong, especially when the ultimate beneficiaries are communities in one of the poorest countries in the world, facing extreme weather events – not at all of their own making - with increasing regularity. Investing in health infrastructure to effectively respond to these events will have an immediate impact on reducing mortality and morbidity from climate change outcomes, but also for the national economy. The financial argument for investing in resilience-building within the health sector is therefore strong, as it has a direct bearing on the economic costs of extreme weather events associated, especially given the reduced turnaround for recovery periods highlighted in Section B5. The project will allow for an assessment of the impact of climate change on the health sector and the economic costs associated with each extreme event.

The project will also seek to find resources to meet current development needs within communities, let alone proactively address anticipated future climate change impacts. The funding amount requested from the Fund is therefore consistent with both the scale of the problem and the Malawi Government's desire to take a nation-wide approach to addressing human adaptation.

## Financial structure

The financial management structure for the project has also been designed with efficiency in mind, making the best use of existing structures within the Ministry of Health. Working through the Ministry of Health as an implementing entity with responsibility for coordinating both within the ministry and across government, means that the connections and knowledge will already be in place to coordinate the project effectively, and provide strong governance and oversight. The project will also benefit from the skills and experience of the existing district health staff seconded onto the project – these staff will be retained by Mott post-project implementation period. In addition, the project will benefit from the technical expertise of Save the Children in relation to infrastructure improvements as well as any higher-voice consultations will be pursued using Save’s children’s rights framework.

Working through national partners at the community level also allows for efficiencies for the project, because they are already in place and have a strong track record. The project will work with existing structures based on the traditional Revenue Authorities. This is an effective way of working not only because it will save on recruitment costs from central to district to sub-district levels to manage the large territories, but also because the partners already have relationships with community members and understand how to operate effectively in those contexts.

## Economic and Financial Analysis

A detailed analysis and financial analysis (Annex C) undertaken as part of the project’s design phase examined five specific adaptation measures that are broadly representative of the overall project’s intervention strategy:

- Operational Health Early Warning and Response System (EWS4R)
- Improved water supply, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) facilities
- Solar and WASH improvements
- Improved climate-resilient school WASH facilities to improve children's health
- Provision of early warning systems for extreme weather events and disaster response
- Reduced malnutrition through production of climate-resilient foods and quality complementary feeding

The analysis showed that overall, all five of the adaptation measures analysed have either a very high or high economic internal rate of return and can be justified on economic grounds. Furthermore, the selected measures will have a positive impact on the environment and social development in Malawi for many years to come.

The economic analysis also undertook assessment of the incremental adaptation benefits of the five selected measures in the context of the overall project budget. This analysis showed that the project has an ERR of 13%, which means that every dollar invested will generate $1.13 worth of additional benefits over and above what would be expected to enable and support the adaptation investments, to ensure their long term sustainability and to catalyse a paradigm shift in resilience in Malawi. The analysis found that the project presents a strong investment for the GCCF. While the ERR is lower than the 20% threshold set by the Fund, this is due to the high costs of public goods (which are often challenging to quantify for any credible economic analysis), without these activities the


---
**Page 59**
---

project’s incrementally quantifiable adaptation investments would be significantly less impactful in the immediate term, would be less sustainable in the longer term and would fail to generate transformational change.

#### Disease burden and conditions, lack of institutional capacity in the Ministry of Health, among districts, and insufficient integrated health systems are enabling climate-based disease outbreaks. In order to implement the EIMARS, the project will build a climate-resilient health system allowing for at least four different climate change early warning signals, enabling effective early warning messaging. At the community level, the project will provide training to 100 community members directly in 500 villages across the 31 districts of Malawi on hygiene and WASH facilities at schools to improve communities' resilience. The project is working with vulnerable groups to improve nutrition services for around 35,000 households.

The project will also strengthen the Government's capacity building exercise to procure medicines and treatments aligned with the impacts of climate change in Malawi, as well as further district health staff capacity to deliver appropriate care.

  - 1.1.1.1 Determine Malawi-specific thresholds and warning levels for diseases linked to specific climate sensitive health conditions
  - 1.1.1.2 Determine Malawi-specific thresholds and warning levels for diseases linked to high / extreme heat exposure

#### 1.1.5 Determine Malawi specific thresholds and warning levels for malnutrition linked to drought

#### Output 1 District Health Adaptation Plans

##### 1.2.1 Facilitate preparation and localisation of district health adaptation plans in all project districts

- **1.2.1.1 Assess the state of knowledge of climate change risks to health among district-level government staff (across sectors) if a project disease**
- **1.2.1.2 Based on results of 1.2.1.1, build capacity to understand the dimensions of climate risk and health in each of the six project districts (six distinct areas) in 6 project districts**
- **1.2.1.3 Enable production of DHAPs in each of the 6 project districts (facilitating district inputs through consultation, drafting, validating and presenting for adoption)**
- **1.2.1.4 Build capacity to implement the DHAPs across sectors at district level (district council)**
- **1.2.1.5 Build skills and knowledge on the dimensions of climate change risk to health and implementation of the DHAPs in CHAGs in the 23 target TAs across 6 districts**
- **1.2.1.6 Support cascading of knowledge on dimensions of climate change risk to health and implementation of the DHAPs from CHAGs to village mentors**
- **1.2.1.7 Develop a toolkit for use in other target districts on how to develop and implement a DHAP at district, CHAG and community level**
- **1.2.1.8 Organize coalition-building meetings nationally and in the 6 target districts with other organizations/networks that work on climate adaptation, disaster risk reduction, and sectoral resilience**

## Outcome 2: Healthcare infrastructure is able to deliver service and care in the context of changing climate risk

### Output 2.1 Climate-resilient health centres, district and central hospitals and schools for community health

#### 2.1.1 Develop a national standard with multi-sectoral stakeholders creating climate-resilient healthcare facilities that can be nationally implemented.
##### 2.1.1.1 Undertake a multi-level consultative process to develop and validate a national standard for climate-resilient healthcare facilities, incorporating gender-responsiveness and social inclusion.
##### 2.1.1.2 Develop and deliver training on the national standard to health infrastructure planners in the public and private sector at national level.

#### 2.1.2 Strengthen climate resilience of healthcare facilities
##### 2.1.2.1 Assess the extent of alignment of 79 healthcare facilities in the 6 project districts with the national standard developed under 2.1.1; and finalise the prioritisation of planned climate resilience strengthening activities.
##### 2.1.2.2 Scope, undertake tender process and oversee service providers applying modifications to strengthen resilience of healthcare facilities.

#### 20 national level training sessions on the national standard by Q2Y2

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 89

### Project progress towards project objectives in line with the NDCs for Malawi and contributions to key GCF Investment Criteria and the Adaptation Performance Measurement Framework.

#### Monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with Save the Children's MEL systems and processes as well as aligned to the Malawi Government National Development Plan (NDP) and the broader 2030 National Planning framework, clarifying the role and responsibilities of government ministries and aid coordination. The M&E Plan will emphasise monitoring and evaluation within the broader 2030 National Planning framework, clarifying the role and responsibilities of government ministries and aid coordination.

#### Project level results and indicators Monitoring

175. The project level monitoring and evaluation will involve different steps. The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation rests with the Project Manager. The Project Manager in consultation with key stakeholders will develop annual work plans to ensure the efficient implementation of the project. A project progress report will be prepared at least once a quarter, which will include an update on the status of all project activities, including any delays or deviations from the approved plan.

176. The Project Manager and/or the M&E team will undertake the following monitoring steps: a) review the project implementation plan; b) assess progress against the over-arching objective of the inception report to is: to assist the Project Team and stakeholders to understand and take ownership of the strategy, objectives and outcomes and discuss any changes in the overall context that influence project implementation; c) discuss the roles, support services and complementary responsibilities of the project team members and other relevant stakeholders; d) review the project progress against the approved work plan; e) assess the performance of the project team and outsourced consultants will undertake all data collection and analysis as per the processes and templates finalised during this process); c) review the results framework, in particular the indicators included in the project results framework.

177. The Project Team will ensure that the indicators included in the project results framework are monitored and reported on annually and will objectively report progress. Project performance against these indicators will be reviewed at least once a quarter by the Project Manager and/or M&E team.

178. The Project M&E will each cover two levels of performance: GCF-level performance (expected performance against investment criteria) and project-level performance (expected performance against project objectives). Data collection, analysis and reporting on both levels will be undertaken using the M&E tools – the MoI and SCI MVI. Annual reviews will be led by the PRM and with the participation of district council staff and other government ministries involved in the project.

### Evaluation

The project mid-term evaluation process will include an internal impact evaluation and an independent process evaluation. An independent final impact evaluation will take place within three to six months prior to operational closure of the project. Evaluation data will be collected by the PRM, implementing entities and outsourced consultants using designs agreed by the steering committee.

179. The evaluations will rely on key evaluation questions (to be developed during inception planning) to respond to the performance and impact of the project's completed activities. These questions will focus on the following: a) whether the project has achieved its intended results; b) whether the project has been implemented in an efficient manner, with respect to the quality of implementation, the efficiency of processes; sustained impact and coherence in climate finance delivery; gender equity and inclusiveness; innovation and potential for paradigm shift; country partnership approach; c) whether the project has contributed to the achievement of the intended results; d) whether the project has been implemented in a manner that is sustainable and learning by reviewing emerging evidence on the performance and the impact (and/or likelihood) of the project and discerning that evidence to project implementers, beneficiaries and other stakeholders. The evaluation will also provide recommendations for future projects, including lessons learned from this project; e) whether there are operational and strategic recommendations – to improve implementation, setting out any necessary corrective measures for the remaining period of the project. The final evaluation will assess the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, as well as sustainability and scalability of results, differential impacts and lessons learned.


---
**Page 93**
---

The evaluation will also assess the extent to which the intervention has contributed to the Fund’s higher-level goal of achieving a paradigm shift in adaptation to climate change in Malawi.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

**F.1 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures (max. 3 pages)**

The top 5 risks for the project are provided below with further details on all potential risks (including low risks) in Annex 2.

| Selected Risk Factor | 1 - Limited Technical Capacity |
| --- | --- |
| Category | Technical and operational |
| Probability | High |
| Impact | Medium |

**Description**

Lack of technical capacity and oversight for government and partner organisations, affects implementation, resulting in delays

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project will mitigate this risk by ensuring National PIJ and Sector PMU staffing include technical specialists on infrastructure projects. This will ensure that the necessary skills are available to manage the work assigned to the contractors. Division of labour between the EEs or supervisory responsibilities will be clarified to avoid delays in communication. The project will also provide direct technical assistance to Government departments and other delivery partners, including training and capacity building for key staff. The Sector PMU will ensure that all projects also help ensure community capacity not is overwhelmed. Working across six provinces will enable the project to spread the workload more evenly among the PIJ and Sector PMU staff. This will ensure that there are sufficient technical capacity and ownership supported by national and international expertise will help ensure sustainable engagement and management of infrastructure projects.

Strong partnerships between the AE, EEs and RIs is a critical. During the design process, EEs worked closely with all partners throughout to ensure that all perspectives were taken into account. This will continue throughout implementation through the project’s management structure and the procurement engagement plan (Annex 7 of the Funding Proposal Package). Ensuring that the project’s management structure and the procurement engagement plan are in place will help ensure that the project is managed effectively, with clear lines of accountability and responsibility.

The project will also ensure adequate staffing for the partner organisations. Setting up of District PIJ or DO offices, with strong partnerships between the AE, EEs and RIs is a critical. During the design process, EEs worked closely with all partners throughout to ensure that all perspectives were taken into account. This will continue throughout implementation through the project’s management structure and the procurement engagement plan (Annex 7 of the Funding Proposal Package). Ensuring that the project’s management structure and the procurement engagement plan are in place will help ensure that the project is managed effectively, with clear lines of accountability and responsibility.

Implementation table will ensure careful planning of the work, close collaboration between 2 partners, spot checks by PIJ/DO staff to ensure quality of work, and regular reporting on progress. This will ensure that there is sufficient visibility and coordination with relevant members of the District Technical and Executive Staff, be critical for the project’s success.

**Selected Risk Factor | Project Safety and Sustainability**

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

**Description**

Lack of financial capacity for the government to cover costs causes project activities ceasing, exposes key project infrastructure such as solar installations in health facilities to theft and to damage.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project will mitigate this risk by embedding a strong focus on improving the governance framework and empowering government ministries to increasingly lead implementation of project activities. This will help ensure the knowledge and skills are available for the government to manage projects effectively, with clear lines of accountability and ensure that the contractors identified make adequate budget provisions for security of the items termed. The project will also incorporate learnings from previous experiences from MoH and other donors. We will work with community stakeholders to ensure that there is sufficient capacity for maintenance and repair, and that training and guidelines are available for healthcare staff for upkeep minor damages and repairs


---
**Page 95**
---

## Selected Risk Factor 2 - Lack of Community Ownership

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

**Description**

Communities are not engaged resulting in a lack of community ownership of the project hampering project sustainability and results.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

SCI MWF will partner with RECCOM, a CSD well known for its strengths in community mobilization approaches to engage internally with communities. SCI MWF has worked with RECCOM for about eighteen months. This has ensured community buy-in and strengthened project ownership. In addition, Save the Children has a 40-year history of working in Malawi and has established strong relationships with communities. Community members are fully engaged in projects. Stakeholder engagement at the community level has proven this to be the case, with all community members consulted across a range of targeted areas expressing a desire to be involved in the project.

## Selected Risk Factor 4 - Financial Management

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MLFT | High | Medium |

**Description**

Limited capacity in the financial management systems, causing external fraud and corruption, resulting in misuse or theft of funds.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The following management procedures that will be utilized for the project under the Malawi Government will reduce the overall risk. This includes conducting annual audits, maintaining internal audit reports, managing procurement through SCI MWF, providing training on fraud policy and procedure. The MoH and SCI MWF have agreed that most of the funds will be disbursed directly to Save the Children. However, there is a need for SCI MWF to establish an independent procurement officer within the PNL who will work closely with ministry officials on any low-value procurements they are involved in. This will ensure that all procurements are conducted through the appropriate channels and procedures to reduce further mitigate this risk. There are no intentions to distribute or disburse to beneficiaries, either directly or indirectly, cash payments.

## Selected Risk Factor 5 - Climate Induced Disasters

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | High | Medium |

**Description**

High exposure to climate hazards in Malawi, including increasing temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, causing extreme events such as floods and droughts, destroying project sites and investments.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

To mitigate this risk project workers and communities will be trained on personal risks such as flooding and droughts in collaboration with MoH to determine best course of action and use of resources. In addition, the project will start implementing emergency health interventions identified during the inception phase of the project. The project will also leverage existing early warning systems and disaster preparedness plans to ensure that communities have a better understanding of early warnings and alert protocols within communities to reduce vulnerability to climate-induced risks.

## Selected Risk Factor 6 - Insufficient Climate and Health Data Integration


---
**Page 96**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 93

## Category: Technical and operational | Probability: High | Impact: Medium

### Description
The project's success, particularly in establishing disease surveillance systems and developing early warning systems (EWS) for infectious diseases on the basis of high-resolution spatial and social data, can lead to earlier warning system development, leading to increased disease burden, reduced health system resilience, and undermining the project’s overall goal of enhancing climate change adaptation within the health sector.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The MoH have advised that an MoU for the sharing of data between the Meteorological department and the Ministry of Health has already been signed during 2022. Additionally, the Project Steering Committee which includes representatives from both departments, will facilitate for efficient operation of the MoU and information sharing.

## Selected Risk Factor 7: Data Availability and Quality

### Category: Technical and operational | Probability: High | Impact: Medium

### Description
Insufficient high-resolution data can hinder the accurate targeting of interventions, potentially affecting those that rely on data collection, analysis, and application in intervention design such as those potentially linked to Activity 1.1.2.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The most recent available social and spatial data sets at district level and below are the 2018 Census and 2017 Integrated Household Survey (IHS). The IHS includes a module that captures local-level data. The proposed development stage has already created access to additional relevant information, for example through the Meteorological Department. However, it is important to note that while the project team has been engaging with key stakeholders and other actors are also collecting relevant data (including as part of monitoring and evaluation of complementary interventions). Hence, there may be a need to collect additional data in order to ensure that all relevant data is captured.

In addition the results of the various project M&E tools, notably the baseline survey, will inform intervention activity. The data Plus tool developed by WHO will be used to capture the social and spatial data that is obtained throughout the project lifespan categorized and metadata made available to the project team for further access in accordance with the relevant data owners’ requirements.

## Selected Risk Factor 8: Limited Institutional Capacity and Coordination

### Category: Technical and operational | Probability: High | Impact: Medium

### Description
Limited institutional capacity, and lack of coordination between health and climate agencies, can impede project execution and delivery of project activities; lack of coordination is most likely to affect project activities related to interagency collaboration, training, and capacity-building initiatives.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will leverage the Project Steering Committee and the national level Project Implementation Unit to enhance communication and collaboration between health and climate agencies and enhance smooth project execution and achievement of desired project outcomes.
