# GCP POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 181. The CHWRC project confirms the assessment of Category C (low risk) as a result of the Environmental and Social Screening completed as part of a project development discussion, stakeholder meetings, a desktop study of similar projects, and an environmental and social impact assessment. This screening process is designed to identify potential environmental or social risks or impacts. Furthermore, any minimal identified risks or impacts can be easily avoided through mitigation measures that are in line with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Environmental and Social Policy (ESS) (B.BA-2018/1), an environmental and social assessment was required to adequately screen and assess potential environmental and social impacts.

### 182. In addition, a number of other screening criteria have been considered that include direct and indirect, ongoing, long-term and cumulative impacts and have taken into account the proposed activities area of influence (SAF) as well as the project’s alignment with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the Environmental Assessment and Residual Risk Management Plan (ERRM) Annex 6 to prevent and respond effectively to SEAs in a survivor-centred manner, and the GCF ESS.

### 183. As per guidelines, the screening has been carried out at the pre-mitigation stage to enable the most serious potential environmental or social risks to be identified early on. The assessment was conducted in accordance with CHWRC as per GCF requirements; the risk category of the highest risk activity is applied to the entire project.

### 184. The SCA PSSMES has been expanded and tailored for the proposed project and is used to screen the project activities against specific ESS standards. The screening process is a two-part process, which consists of two parts – Part I determines the approximate extent and type of environmental and social assessment required based on the project’s alignment with the SDGs and the GCF ESS. Part II assesses any potential environmental and social risks including requirements based on specific ESS standards.

### 185. The Full Environmental and Social Safeguards Assessment and Residual Risk Management Plan (ESARMP) is annexed as Annex 6.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 186. Climate change disproportionately affects marginalized groups who face inequality and barriers in daily life (UN). The project aims to transform institutions, traditional and human capacity to improve the health and wellbeing of all people, including women, children, youth, and people with disabilities. Gender equality and social norms reflect structural dimensions of differential vulnerability which need to be recognised and understood in order to enable equitable benefits from project participation, and support gender transformative approaches that address these underlying causes of inequality (UN). The GCF requires a gender analysis (based on a literature review, key informant interviews and focus groups in target communities across the six project countries) to identify how the project will contribute to overall gender equality. This analysis should include the budget and what that ensure equitable benefits from project participation and how the project contributes to overall GEF strategic objectives.

### 187. Gender mainstreaming encompasses the existing gender and social inequalities and marginalisation in Malawi: differential vulnerability for women, children/youth, and people with disabilities to climate change and extremes arises from socially constructed norms and practices that limit their access to resources and opportunities for meaningful participation. Despite a comprehensive array of policies and laws related to gender equality and social inclusion issues, Malawi suffers from weak policy implementation. Political participation by women, people with disabilities and children/youth is limited across all tiers: from the national to the local. A deeply entrenched

patriarchal culture constrains the opportunities that are available to women and girls[1]; people with disabilities typically benefit through the charity model of providing assistance rather than being supported to play active roles in society[2]; and family-enriched health norms and social beliefs (e.g., violence against children) are one of the critical barriers to achieving gender equality, rights for women and girls, and access to education[3]. Women are often disproportionately affected by disasters, both in the short- and longer-term. For instance, after tropical cyclones, women are more likely than men to be displaced[11]. Women and girls are also more likely to be counted among the internally displaced people, with 65% of those in shelters in Machinga, Malawi; Baka and Zomba districts[12]. In addition, women and girls are disproportionately affected by climate change-induced food crises. For instance, incidents of gender-based violence have been shown to increase after disasters in Malawi[13]. Women and girls are also more likely than men to be displaced following natural disasters in several Southern Malawi districts, including Phalombe[14]. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 96

A high risk for children with climate-induced water scarcity is the main factor [1]. Climate shocks are increasingly occurring and increasing intensity, threatening health, nutrition, education, development, and the survival of children.

### Objectives
128 Discussions were held with communities to understand the impacts of climate change on community members. Focus group discussions were held with men, women, children/youth and people with disability separately to understand their perspectives on the impacts of climate change. The project will address the negative impacts of climate change on health, with diverse impacts mentioned: heat exhaustion exacerbated by dehydration; increased incidence of malaria, dengue fever, and other vector-borne diseases; reduced yields in agriculture; gendered increases in agricultural production); malnutrition from changes in climatic patterns; and cholera and diarrhea. The project will address the negative impacts on education, with diverse impacts mentioned: increased gender- and socially differentiated nature of vulnerability to the health impacts of climate change. The health impacts of poor food and nutrition security were recognised as a particular risk for women (particularly pregnant and breastfeeding mothers) and children (particularly girls). Women and girls also noted that they had difficulty accessing antenatal, sexual and reproductive health services, and other health care, with increases in unplanned pregnancies and unsafe abortions. The project will address the negative impacts on livelihoods, with diverse impacts mentioned: increased vulnerability of women and girls to climate change; increased gender- and socially differentiated nature of vulnerability to the health impacts of climate change.

### Gender Equality and Inclusion
The GESI Action Plan will set out a range of actions and targets to ensure that the implementation of activities is inclusive. The project will mainstream gender equality and inclusion by disaggregating data to account specifically for the inclusion/participation of women, youth, men and women with disabilities in all aspects of the project. The project will also promote the participation of those whose voices are less often heard: women, girls, youth and people with disabilities.

### Implementation Arrangements
129 Implementation arrangements will engage partners with a track record of successfully implementing GESI activities. A gender equality and inclusion strategy will be developed for the project. Training materials on GESI will be provided through the Project Management Unit and Save the Children to raise institutional awareness of the importance of commitment to gender equality and social inclusion in internal and external management.

### G3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project’s financial management and procurement approach follows SCA’s approved processes to manage any cash flow and ensure that all funds are used in accordance with the terms of the Agreement, SCF and category C ESS, basic fiduciary standards and project management standards. This approach is further guided by the Financial Management Plan (FMP) which will be developed for this project and completed during the design phase. A summary of the due diligence completed for the EEs and associated mitigations can be found in Appendix 1.

Fiduciary arrangements will follow a decentralized model, that involves SCWII as the international channeling agency, the National Executive Council (NEC) as the national channeling agency, and the National Executive Unit, SC WI, who will provide direct funding tranches to the MoH, as the other national executing entity. Following completion of work plans and budgets by the Project Steering Committee and the NAEU, funds will be released from the project’s budget in the form of disbursements to the relevant district offices account opened for this SCF project, and would be opened by the Fiscal Agent*, which is a function within the Treasury Department. The fiscal agent will ensure that all transactions are recorded on the treasury system. Funds B4a will be managed by the FAU, who will have responsibility for day-to-day spending, cash transfers, payment vouchers, petty cash, and other financial management functions as required to support the implementation of this portion of the project.

Management contracts and contractors will be used in the project, and the portion of the overall budget that is allocated to procured activities will flow from SCI MW directly, as per the terms of reference and contracts for each supplier. The procurement process will follow SCF’s approved guidelines and procedures. Funds will be disbursed to the relevant district offices operating under the project. This is in line with Save the Children’s operating structure and its ability to manage funds at this level.

Child and partner district offices receive funds from the national office, and provide financial reporting including receipts of money and other documentation to the national level P&L unit who will compile the overall financial reporting for the project.


#### Management and audit

SC WI will ensure that implementation and financial reporting in line with our established project management systems and in conformity with the FAA and Subsidiary Agreements (SA) (when developed and approved by SCF), as well as the terms of reference and contracts for each supplier. In accordance with existing funds E, SCI MW will provide account management support to SCI MW in accordance with the account management system employed by the wider Save the Children movement to ensure compliance and high-quality delivery of projects. The P&L unit at SCI MW is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are within budget and on schedule. The P&L unit is also responsible for monitoring project audits, reviewing financial reports submitted by the PMU in Malawi; and conducting regular reviews with the PMU to ensure that all project activities are being implemented as per the terms of the GCF. Each implementing partner’s financial accounts will be required prior to further disbursements to ensure spending is on track and within budget. The P&L unit at SCI MW will also provide support in ensuring project funds are effectively managed and SCAS fiduciary standards are adhered to, which are compliant with the terms of the GCF.

### Procurement

As per the request of the MoH, all major procurements for the project will go through Save the Children systems. By using Save the Children’s system, we mean Save the Children will be the lead EE and budget holder for that particular procurement, and the procurement will be fully managed by PMU staff employed by Save the Children in partnership with SCI MW. The procurement process will follow SCF’s approved guidelines and procedures as well as GCF procurement and still fit within the EEs’ rules. Furthermore, Save the Children has established software systems to ensure that all procurements are conducted in line with the terms of reference and contracts for each supplier to use to raise all procurement requests. For items listed as ‘long-going’ through Save the Children’s systems, we will use our well-established software which ensures fully compliant procurement in line with the AMA and global Save the

Children’s rights and rules. The AE will monitor and engage in oversight of the procurement process, in line with its normal procedures clarified in the subsidiary agreement. However, most of the Government-funded procurement is valued at less than USD200,000 and is primarily for low-value consultations. For example, the Children’s Fund has a system for electronic tendering (e-tender) for low-value and burdensome process with government procurement systems, and historic delays in procurement leading to overall delay in project implementation. # Secretariat's Assessment of FP244

## Proposal name: Climate-Resilient Health and Well-Being for Rural Communities in southern Malawi (CHWBRC)

### Accredited entity: Save the Children Australia

### Country: Malawi

### Project: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project targets the most climate-vulnerable districts in Malawi, a least developed country, by employing a holistic climate-resilient health and well-being strengthening approach to reduce the adverse impacts of climate change on health and well-being. Proposed project activities remove barriers to adaptation and change and enable a step-change in the climate resilience of Malawi's rural communities through implementing integrated approaches at the district level to strengthen the resilience of infrastructure and basic services, while also contributing to promoting climate-resilient health and well-being at the community level. | The upcoming 2025 general election in Malawi presents a risk to project stability and continuity due to potential political shifts and changes in the national governance and regulatory environment. |

This is only the third of GCF's health-specific climate action proposals, following the approved funding proposals for the Lao People's Democratic Republic (approved at the 37th meeting of the Board [B.37]) and the Cook Islands (B.38).

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms that were agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE), and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II of document GCF/B.40/II.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 106**
---

1. The objective of this project is to strengthen the climate resilience of the health system in Malawi, with direct adaptation benefits to Malawi’s four southern districts of Nkhata Bay, Machinga, Mangochi and Phalombe, as well as in the Zomba district, which is centrally located.
2. The five main components of this project are: (a) improving climate information systems; (b) strengthening health surveillance system and early warning system that functions at the national level and provides timely and accurate early warning for the occurrence of climate-sensitive diseases and outcomes (outcome 1); (c) physical health care infrastructure will be retrofitted and adapted to withstand climate change impacts, including heat stress, through capacity building and development of national standards (outcome 2); (d) health staff will be equipped to reduce climate health risk through improved disease monitoring, health messaging, and disease treatment and prevention (outcome 3); (e) communities will be better prepared to manage the impacts of climate change on health (outcome 4) as a result of a more resilient health care system and empowerment to


---
**Page 107**
---

The proposal builds on the GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme funds that have supported various climate policies, strategies, and the necessary institutional capacities for the implementation of national adaptation plans (NAPs). The NAP Process: Climate Resilience for Sustainable Development in Malawi” implemented by United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) has provided a framework for the development and execution of future national adaptation plan (NAP) processes. And on the GCF-funded project, “Scaling Up Early Warning Systems for Drought and Flood Early Warning Systems (FP042, implemented by United Nations Development Programme), which aims to enhance early warning systems, reduce risks to livelihoods by enhancing hydrometeorological capacity for early warning and forecasting of drought and floods, improve the management of water resources in vulnerable areas, and strengthen capacities of communities to respond to climate-related disasters such as floods.

Additionally, the project builds on the Adaptation Fund’s “Adapting to Climate Change Through Improved Water Management and Enhanced Ecosystem Services: Strengthening Resilient Food Security and Livelihoods” (approved in 2019).

The proposed project is aligned with the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and responds to the following USP-2 targets: (a) T3: Climate information and early warning systems (CIEWS); (b) T7: Clean energy; and (c) T9: Adaptation and local-led action.

The project will be implemented in Malawi in the form of grants with co-financing of USD 4,508,208, comprising of USD 1,721,800 from GCF, USD 1,064,272 from the United States Agency for International Development (USAID), USD 930,000 from the Government of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland; USD 148,167 from the Moi Foundation Trust, and USD 1,264,919 in-kind co-financing from the Government of Malawi.

Save the Children Australia (SCA) is the GCF AE and will undertake all key fiduciary and operational functions for the project. Save the Children International (SCI), as the AE, will enter into legally binding subsidiary agreements with three executing entities (EEs):

(a) The Ministry of Health (MoH) of Malawi, as the implementing EE;

(b) Save the Children International - Malawi Country Office (SC CI MW), as the funds-channeling EE; and

(c) Save the Children UK (Save the Children United Kingdom [SCUK]), as the trade name and abbreviation, as the EE responsible for channelling funds internationally.

The project will be implemented in 10 districts of Malawi over a period of five years. It emphasizes local planning (e.g., the establishment of district and subdistrict health adaptation committees) and community-based approaches to climate change adaptation through Action Groups and the engagement of Community Healthcare Volunteers in community-level planning and activity implementation, policy advocacy, knowledge-sharing and robust monitoring and evaluation systems. The project will be implemented by Save the Children International in collaboration with local and national frameworks, empowering communities and institutions to continue practices and support each other beyond the life of the project.

The project’s risk assessment identifies political factors such as collaboration challenges between government stakeholders and changes in the government's development priorities as significant risks. Other identified risks include limited cooperation between partners (i.e., different ministries and departments), which could hinder effective implementation and knowledge sharing regarding climate-resilient

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Reduced risk from climate-sensitive diseases and conditions (total cost: USD 2.42 million; GCF contribution: USD 0)

### Output 1.1: Climate-informed health surveillance system and health EWARS
- Activity 1.1.1: Strengthen the health surveillance system by identifying alert triggers for key disease indicators.
- Activity 1.1.2: Strengthen the institutional architecture for managing the ongoing operation of the climate-informed health EWARS.

### Output 1.2: District health adaptation plans
- Activity 1.2.1: Facilitate preparation and local endorsement of district health adaptation plans in all districts.
- Activity 1.2.2: Advocate for stronger integration of climate-resilient health within district health adaptation plans.

## Component 2: Healthy water infrastructure is able to deliver service and care in the context of changing climate risk (total cost: USD 14.56 million; GCF contribution: USD 14.71 million)

### Output 2.1: Climate-resilient health centres, district and central hospitals, and schools for community health


---
**Page 109**
---

### Activity 2.1.1: Develop a national standard for climate-resilient health care facilities;
- Activity 2.1.2: Strengthen climate resilience of health care facilities;
- Activity 2.1.3: Build capacity of Malawi's health sector to apply the climate-resilient health-care facility standard;
- Activity 2.1.4: Develop guidelines for climate-resilient WASH facilities;
- Activity 2.1.5: Upgrade WASH facilities at schools to improve children’s health under climate change;

#### Component B: Health care staff are able to deliver service and care in the context of changing climate risk (Total USD 73,000)
This outcome will build the capacity of health care staff to understand how climate risk will alter public health risks, how they can interpret surveillance data, how they can interpret early warning signals, and how they can use this information to better anticipate and manage physical and mental health needs in their communities. It addresses the barrier of low capacity of communities to reduce climate health risks and of marginalized groups, particularly women and children, to access services. Specific activities include:
- Activity 3.1: Train health care staff trained in managing climate-related disease monitoring, health messaging, and disease treatment and prevention;
- Activity 3.1.1: Build data collection capacity to strengthen surveillance of climate-related diseases;
- Activity 3.1.2: Build knowledge and capacity among district and community health care staff on climate and health use of EAWS alerts;
- Activity 3.1.3: Equip health facilities with medical supplies and technologies for climate health risk response and reduction; 
- Activity 3.1.4: Equip health care workers with Mental Health and Psychosocial Support (MHPSS) skills to address the mental impacts of a changing climate;
- Activity 3.1.5: Build capacity among district and community health-care staff to address the gendered impacts of climate change;

#### Component C: Community-level health is more resilient in the context of changing climate risk (Total USD 109,000)
This outcome will address the need to increase community awareness of and skills and competencies for managing climate risks. It aims to build capacity among communities to better manage their risk through collective action. It addresses the barriers of low capacity of communities to reduce climate health risks and of marginalized groups, particularly women and children, to access services. Specific activities include:
- Activity 3.2: Develop a community-based training manual with knowledge and skills for climate-resilient WASH facilities to community members;
- Activity 4.1.2: Embed understanding of early warnings and alert protocols within communities through training and engagement methods; 
- Activity 4.1.3: Train communities to reduce their own vulnerability to climate-induced health risks;

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A
- The project will directly benefit an estimated 1,768,025 beneficiaries (99% women and 99% children) who will directly benefit from an estimated 1,250 facilities (1,143 health centers, 107 women and 1,179 men) in southern Malawi (21 percent of the country's total population), with a particular focus on women, children and other vulnerable groups.
- Adaptation benefits will be captured through improved resilience in infrastructure and built environment of 79 health facilities, which will sustain health services in 500 communities. These adaptation benefits include: reduced reliance on diesel generators for power supply; increased capacity to withstand extreme weather events (e.g., floods); increased preparedness in the event of climate-driven extreme weather events; (iii) increased training for government staff of Malawi in the utilization of the health information system and early warning system; and (iv) community-based adaptation solutions in food security and treatment and prevention of climate-sensitive health outcomes. The total monetary value of physical assets and equipment protected will be captured as a total target.
- Mitigation benefits to be captured are in the number of target health facilities with reduced estimated reliance on diesel generators for power supply; increased capacity to withstand extreme weather events (e.g., floods); increased preparedness in the event of climate-driven extreme weather events; (iii) increased training for government staff of Malawi in the utilization of the health information system and early warning system; and (iv) community-based adaptation solutions in food security and treatment and prevention of climate-sensitive health outcomes. The total monetary value of physical assets and equipment protected will be captured as a total target.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: N/A
- Proposed project activities will remove barriers to transformative change and enable a step change towards achieving the desired healthy pathway through the implementation of integrated, district-level approaches to strengthening the resilience of infrastructure and health service provision and promoting climate-resilient health and well-being at community level.


---
**Page 111**
---

30 At the end of the project, each of the participating districts will have a district executive committee that understands the nature of climate risk to health, a district health adaptation plan; and capacity to integrate climate risk to health into development planning, including annual/long-term plans. The districts will also have a community-based health worker with knowledge on how to build climate-resilient health care facilities and WASH facilities. They will also have health care workers trained in the use of EWBRS and other early warning systems, including through treatment for and prevention of climate-driven morbidity; and provide greater public health support to communities. Because of the focus on building systems (for example, EWBRs at national level), the project is not a stand-alone project. However, because it focuses on climate resilience, these approaches can be replicated and scaled out to other districts, further improving their capacity for planning and responding to climate change, thus having impact beyond the life of our project investment.

31 Knowledge-sharing and evidence generation will be underpinned by a robust monitoring system that is strengthened through oversight and implementation by the Health and Climate Change Core Team or the multi-stakeholder Joint National Technical Committee (JNTC) as appropriate. The JNTC will also provide technical support to districts.

32 The proposed project creates an institutional and enabling environment at national level, including for EWBRs, the standard for climate-resilient health care facilities; and the guidelines on building resilient WASH facilities, all of which are accompanied by awareness-raising and training.

33 Additionally, the project contributes to the regulatory environment and policies by developing a policy framework for climate change adaptation in Malawi. This includes: (i) development of climate resilience within adaptation planning at district and subdistrict level (activity 1.2.2); (ii) alignment with the National Climate Change Policy (2016), the National Health Policy (2018), the National Environmental Health Policy (2018), the Updated National Development Plan (NDP) (2017-2035), and the Biennial Investment Programme (BIPAP).

34 The project leverages Malawi’s initial commitments to adaptation in the health sector through the policy framework for Climate Services Adaptation for Africa project and the draft HNFAP. Its integrated and holistic approach to health system climate resilience at multiple levels creates the conditions that will allow districts to achieve greater levels of health system climate resilience like those at the district level. This allows for ease of replication and self-sustainability, as well as a clear pathway for scaling up and mainstreaming this development pathway for Malawi, in line with national planning documents such as Vision 2063 and the Malawi Growth and Development Strategy.

### Sustainable development potential

The project is fully aligned to Malawi’s sustainable development agenda and its commitments under the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). It contributes particularly to SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 4 (Quality education), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation) and SDG 13 (Climate action).

The project incorporates a number of co-benefits, including:

(a) Social co-benefits: Improved social inclusion for marginalized groups will result from improved access to health services. This includes the provision of services reaching hard-to-reach groups, including out-of-school children, the elderly and people with disabilities. Moreover, improved social outcomes for household economies will derive from using improved water sources and sanitation facilities, which will reduce the increased need to purchase/purchased food for nutritionally vulnerable household members.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: N/A

Malawi’s Human Development Index value for 2021 is 0.512, putting the country in the low human development category position (i.e., 169 out of 184 countries and territories). Around half of Malawi's population lives below the poverty line, with rural areas having a higher proportion than urban ones. The poverty headcount ratio is higher in rural areas and higher in the southern part of the country; poverty headcounts in the target districts range from 40.6 per cent in Zomba to 63.7 per cent in Phalombe.

### 3.5 Country ownership

Scale: N/A

This project is expected to align with the National Climate Change Management Policy and the programs defined in the NAP framework, the updated NAP and the HNP, which aims to "create a health sector that is resilient to climate change effects." It builds on and leverages the country's first health and adaptation project (Global Framework for Climate Service: Adaptation and Resilience Project) and the National Health Sector Strategic Plan. The project places particular emphasis on: sustainably building local capacity, developing local training materials and ensuring all components start their activities at the same time to avoid delays.

**Page 113**

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The proposal demonstrates efficacy and effectiveness of the project by presenting a high-quality economic assessment with clear and thoroughly referenced and cited data and assumptions. The economic analysis evaluates the economic viability of five measures that have been identified as able to be valued and monetized, and that are broadly representative of the project’s overall impact.

- **Economic viability in the long run is demonstrated through the results against economic indicators such as GDP growth rate, poverty reduction, and health outcomes.** The analysis uses various methods, including climate change-attributable disability-adjusted life years, avoided costs regarding morbidity and mortality, and value of statistical life in Malawi. In all five models, the project interventions are found to be economically viable with a net present value (NPV) of 9 per cent. The overall project-level economic net present value is US$12.4 million, with an internal rate of return of 30 per cent. Consequently, the project requests the highest level of concessionality, seeking full grants from the GCF. These high levels of concessionality are justified by the macroeconomic control and fiscal constraints of Malawi, with its fiscal deficit and public debt rising in recent years.

- **The financial viability and profitability of the project interventions are not demonstrated,** as the financial analysis was not submitted. The proposal justifies this omission by stating that the project interventions are public in nature and the beneficiaries are largely subsistence farmers who do not have access to markets or credit facilities for private investment.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The proposed project is categorized as C for environmental and social impacts, in accordance with the GCF Safeguard Policy (GSP) and the AEF's accreditation level. Minimal environmental and social risks are expected from this project. The infrastructure works related to upgrading of health and school facilities (i.e., rainwater harvesting systems, water supply systems, sanitation systems, classrooms, toilets, etc.) have been developed with an environmental and social assessment and residual risk management plan in place. This plan also includes the identification, evaluation, and management of the potential minimal environmental and social residual risks and impacts. The plan allows for the exclusion of activities that are likely to result in significant or high environmental or social risk categorization. The exclusion list will avoid activities that will involve deforestation to clear land for construction, mining, agriculture, and other things.

The Project Implementation Unit (PIU) will be establishing implementing safeguards measures on a day-to-day basis. Stakeholder engagement will continue during implementation and a grievance mechanism will be established in the project area to channel and address any issues or complaints from communities and other stakeholders.

GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESST (Indigenous Consensus). Consistent with its categories, the project is subject to the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and the Indigenous Peoples Policy.

Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH): The AEF adopts a zero-tolerance policy for sexual exploitation by representatives about adults or children in the communities where the project will be implemented. Based on the results from the AEF Safeguarding Risk Assessment, specific SEAH measures are incorporated into the ESARMP, including project staff/representatives, consultants, volunteers, partners and suppliers spending time in community settings. The PIU will ensure that all project staff/representatives are trained to report instances of SEAH, despite rigorous prevention efforts and reporting channels that are in place. Specific SEAH measures are incorporated in the ESARMP, such as the provision of training for project staff/representatives on how to identify and respond appropriately to referral services for victims and survivors. The PIU, through the Safeguarding Specialist, will be responsible for monitoring SEAH measures during implementation.

##### 4.2 Gender policy

The AEF provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy. The gender assessment is based on primary and secondary sources. These included secondary data in the form of national statistics, reports, and studies; focus group discussions (FGDs) conducted with women, men, and people with disabilities across the six targeted districts; and lastly, key informant interviews for key district-level personnel.


---
**Page 115**

### Malawi's constitution enshrines gender equality and disability

Malawi is a signatory to regional, continental, and international equity agreements, and gender is a cross-cutting issue across all policies, including those on climate change. Malawi has put in place the Gender Equality Act 2015, which seeks to address gender inequality by promoting women's participation, influence, empowerment, dignity, and opportunities, and the National Disability Mainstreaming Strategy 2016-2030, which aims at improving the lives of people with disabilities. Additionally, the country has the National Climate Change Management Policy 2015 in place, which addresses gender, population dynamics, and HIV/AIDS as cross-cutting issues and commits to mainstreaming gender equality into all climate change policies, plans, and programs.

### For improved resilience

Malawi is highly vulnerable due to the dominance of monoculture and pervasive gender norms, which affect equity and inclusion, and inadequate resourcing to the relevant stakeholders and duty bearers. Women and girls with disabilities continue to face discrimination in accessing resources and services, and this vulnerability is exacerbated by climate change. Women are progressively gaining access and control over some productive assets such as land, livestock, and small-scale enterprises due to limiting. Malawi has low political representation of women at national and other levels, and land ownership is still largely male-dominated. Women with disabilities face additional barriers in the acquisition and control over ownership of land; gender norms still favour male ownership. Gender-based violence (GBV) is high, and 41 per cent of women have experienced gender-based violence at least once in their lifetime.

### Climate-resilient health and well-being for rural communities

The project area focuses on climate resilience, health, and social justice and addresses root causes of gender inequality in the project focus area. The actor analysis phase will prioritize actions to support women who have girls, boys, youth, and care facilities vulnerable to climate change-induced triggers and adverse health conditions by enhancing health surveillance mechanisms. Specifically, the All Will determine whether there are any existing or planned interventions that cater more to differences at thresholds for women, children, men and women with disabilities. To embed more sustainable gender equality into the project, the All Will will work in support systems by strengthening management processes and ensuring women and other groups facing vulnerability are included in decision-making structures.

### The All Will

The All Will will reinforce relationships on gender, power inequality, and decision- making to identify where the impacts of climate change are likely to occur and plan accordingly. The project area is highly vulnerable due to its reliance on rain-fed agriculture under two to ensure appropriate infant feeding and produce climate-resilient, complementary nutrition interventions. Community-level staff overemphasize women's roles in food production will receive training on community-based participatory research (CBPR). All Will conduct a gender and power analysis as part of the baseline study, explicitly examining how gender inequality and other social inequalities are embedded into project activities. The All Will will subsequently develop a gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) strategy. The All has included GESI in its work plan, including specific roles, responsibilities, timeframes, timelines, indicators of who is responsible for what roles, and budgetary costs against each activity. The All Will will engage with women's groups, men's groups, youth, children, and other vulnerable populations, and the GESI assessment findings will be disseminated to all actors. The All Will engage in regular monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of gender equality across project activities. The GESI specialist will be an integral part of this project, working closely with the project staff and largely with three key government secondments.

### Risks

#### Overall project assessment (low risk)

The aim of the project is to achieve climate-resilient health and well-being in Malawi through a comprehensive approach. This approach integrates actions across national, district, and community levels to address the complex interplay between climate change and development challenges. Its purpose is to enhance and synchronize efforts to strengthen early warning systems for climate-sensitive diseases; improve access to essential services; and empower communities to manage climate-related health risks.

The project will achieve climate-resilient health and well-being in Malawi through four main outcomes:

1. Reduced risk from climate-sensitive diseases and conditions.
2. Improved access to essential services, including water, sanitation, and hygiene.
3. Health care staff are equipped to provide service and care in the face of changing climate risks; and outcome 4. Communities have improved control over the evolution of climate risks.

The total project cost is USD 37,068,208, which will be further financed by a grant totaling USD 4,664,208 from FCDO, Foundation for Climate Resilience (FCR), and a small contribution from the Government of Malawi, resulting in a co-financing ratio of 1:0.1.

#### Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current project (low risk)

The AEI for the project is SCA, which will manage all fiduciary and operational aspects. The EEs include SCI MW and the MoH in Malawi. SCI MW has been operating in Malawi since 2012 and has a strong track record in integrating climate change adaptation education, child protection, and health services with proven expertise in gender-informative and child-centered approaches. The MoH has successfully led key climate-resilient health programs and established the National Climate Change Unit (NCCU) under the leadership of the Government of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, KfW Germany, and the Norwegian Agency for Development Cooperation (Norad). SCI MW is a leading partner in Malawi in managing donor-funded projects and improving health infrastructure. The AEI has been approved to accept GCF aid under implementation, namely SAPFAP, SAPSIA, SAPS30, SAPS17, and FP104.

#### Project-specific execution risks (low risk)

Inflation pressure: We recognize the key challenge of inflationary risks currently impacting Malawi, as highlighted in the funding proposal, which may affect project execution by influencing the exchange rate between Malawian Kwacha (MK) and United States dollars (USD) for all transactions. The inflationary environment may necessitate ongoing budget adjustments to ensure that the AEI has sufficient funds to execute the project and fund transactions in USD with budget allocations for activities subcontracted to EEs, inclusive of cost escalation.

Insufficient climate and health data integration: The project's success in establishing effective disease surveillance and early warning systems relies significantly on the timely integration of climate and health data. Without a formalized approach to data integration, there

things, GCF are not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which needs to be further assessed. The Global Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities against the GCF in this country, as they are subject to laws of this country along with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in this country, or has been provided with adequate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP244

## Proposal name: Climate Resilient Health and Well-Being for Rural Communities in southern Malawi (CHWRC)

### Accredited entity: Save the Children Australia

#### Country: Malawi
#### Project: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

Malawi is among the most climate-vulnerable countries in the world. The proposed project focuses largely on Malawi's southern region, as it is prioritized for climate adaptation in the national development plan. Malawi has a population of approximately 20 million people, with high poverty levels and limited access to health services, in combination with high vulnerability. Malawi is exposed to increasing temperatures, changing rainfall patterns, and extreme events which result in a range of health-related impacts.

The project will focus on strengthening the health sector at all levels, including the private sector, in southern Malawi. The project combines interventions at the national, district, and community levels. It will build capacity for early warning systems to detect climate-sensitive diseases and conditions (outcome 1). This aims to mitigate the risk of increases in the occurrences of malaria, dengue fever, cholera, and other waterborne diseases due to increased rainfall and heat. The resilience to climate hazards of health centres, hospitals, and water, sanitation, and hygiene facilities will be improved through the construction of new buildings and renovations for climate-resilient facilities (outcome 2). Seventy-nine health-care facilities will be targeted for physical improvements, including the installation of solar power systems. This will lead to improved disease monitoring, health messaging, and disease treatment and prevention is planned in the project area.

The project targets seven villages selected in a consultation between the District Health Management Team and the Children International Malawi Country Office. The project will reach around 1.8 million direct beneficiaries and 23.6 million indirect beneficiaries.

The project is executed by the Save the Children International Malawi Country Office, as the channeling entity responsible for implementing the project in Malawi. The executing entity responsible for channelling funds internationally is the Save the Children Fund, as the executing entity responsible for channelling funds internationally. The independent technical advisory panel will act as a monitoring and evaluation entity.

The sources of finance for the USD 37 million project are: (i) USD 33 million in grant finance from GCF; (ii) USD 2.8 million in grant finance from foundations, the Government of the United Kingdom, and other donors; and (iii) USD 1.3 million in kind from the Government of Malawi. The project implementation period is five years.


---
**Page 121**
---

1.2 Impact potential

Scale: Medium-High

The project has an adaptation impact related to the areas of "most vulnerable people and communities" (40 per cent of finance), and "health and well-being" (50 per cent of finance). It also has a minor mitigation impact through the provision of solar-powered systems on health posts, an impact which has not, however, been quantified by project proponents.

The expected adaptation outcome has 9.16 million direct and indirect beneficiaries of which 7.84 million are in the target districts (Zomba, Phalombe and Zomba district) and the central hospital in Zomba) in the target districts and target traditional authorities for climate-resilient health systems.

The adaptation outcome was calculated by including all direct beneficiaries who represent the entire population of the 25 targeted traditional authorities which is commensurate with all people living in the target districts. The indirect beneficiaries are those who live within a 10 km radius from the target area and, therefore, the intensity level (level of support provided per person) is considered low to medium.

The direct and indirect beneficiaries represent the entire population of the six target districts. The district-level project activities include strengthening health systems in the six districts through district plans, an improved health early warning and response system that will cover the entire district improving access to health services, and a community-based approach for the six district projects (Zomba - the sixth district – has already had a site established as a pilot by the World Health Organization [WHO]).

Climate change increases the diffusion and severity of the core identified diseases linked to climate change (malnutrition, diarrhoea disease, food and nutrition insecurity). The project will reduce the incidence of these diseases through improved access to health services for health and well-being, and the availability, accessibility, and quality of healthcare. The increased disease burden from climatic changes will reduce well-being disproportionately affecting women and children who are more vulnerable to climate change. These core identified diseases are well-known; their cause-effect relationship is established, and treatment and prevention methods exist. However, these diseases have a significant impact on the population of existing districts which creates additional stress on the health system.

The project takes a holistic health systems approach, as advocated by WHO, to develop a climate-resilient health system that will reduce the vulnerability of communities in Malawi on health and well-being. The project addresses climate-induced stresses by preventing a deterioration in health services at all levels (strengthening community-level capacity; strengthening institutional level [strengthening of institutional capacities]; at the health system staff level [improving access to health services, improving quality of care, increasing service outputs and improving the population's knowledge]); and at the physical infrastructure level [upgrading of health centres]).

The project aims to make the health system more effective at detecting, treating, and preventing these climate-induced diseases. Early detection and access to efficient medical care lower the incidence of these diseases; this is achieved through strengthening the national health surveillance system by identifying triggers for key climate-sensitive conditions and a health early warning and response system, while other activities under this output strengthen the


---
**Page 122**
---

### Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: Medium

1. Increasing access to and the visibility of climate and early warning information, and strengthening health workforce and community capacity to address climate change impacts shall result in a more effective response to climate-related disasters.
2. A combination of integrated approaches at the district level. A combination of climate-responsive health plans, guidelines for climate-resilient health care facilities, and the local staff shall ensure the continuity and spread of measures.
3. Knowledge-sharing and evidence generation shall be enabled through oversight of project implementation by the National Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management Committee and the Joint National Technical Committee on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management, which will be chaired by the Ministry of Health and other relevant stakeholders and technical actors.

The project aims to help in creating an institutional and enabling environment at the national level through the health early warning system, the standard for climate-resilient health-care facilities, and the guideline for climate-resilient WASH facilities. At the district level


---
**Page 123**
---

The rural environment is improved by advocating for stronger integration of climate-resilient health within adaptation planning at district and sub-district levels.

### Structural problems of the Malawi health system, such as the lack of finance and lack of fund commitment by the government for the health sector, are not directly tackled by the project. Over the period 2012 to 2016, the share of public expenditure on health in Malawi has decreased from 5.8 per cent to 4.7 per cent as a percentage of the gross domestic product and has decreased in the period from 2012 to 2016 in Malawi, compared with an average increase of 3.9 per cent for other low-income countries and 1.8 per cent for African States to allocate 15 per cent of their total budgets to the health sector.* The National Health Accounts (NHA) data show that the share of public expenditure on health in Malawi’s health sector funding is new. The newly introduced Health Financing Strategy for 2023–2030 strategic plan aims at increasing domestic resource mobilization and improving the efficiency of health-care spending.

The project seeks to expand its expansion to other districts, and a continuation of service levels within the targeted districts, the project relies on tapping external funding sources as well as on current government budget allocations. The project is also dependent on in-kind contributions in terms of the sustainability of project operations. The ITAP notes and appreciates the in-kind financing provided by the Government of Malawi.* A financial strategy, including an increasing share of domestic resource mobilization, is needed to ensure that the project can continue to meet its commitments related to the sustainability of the project and the actual potential for a paradigm shift.

### Questions

1. The project has not yet demonstrated how it will address these challenges in terms of scaling up and amplifies existing approaches without making a structural change to the health system.
2. Without tackling the funding problem, the paradigm shift potential is deemed to be limited, with interventions being more likely to be seen as incremental rather than transformative.

### Conclusion

Guidelines and plans are important elements in achieving a paradigm shift, but if not linked with financing, they may not lead to improved outcomes overall. Overall, the ITAP rates the potential for the project to affect a paradigm shift as a medium.


## Sustainable development potential: Scale: Medium to high

The project is aligned primarily with Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), and SDG 13 (Climate action). It also contributes to the achievement of other goals, including SDGs 2 (Zero hunger), 5 (Gender equality), and 17 (Partnerships for the Goals). The main emphasis is therefore on good health and well-being through improved access, outcomes, and capacity to treat and prevent the diffusion and severity of communicable diseases and improve maternal and child health. Guidelines and plans are important elements in achieving a paradigm shift, but if not linked with financing, they may not lead to improved outcomes overall.

The project shall also achieve co-benefits, including enhanced social inclusion for marginalized groups; increased access to clean water and sanitation; better health; better social outcomes for households due to improved integrated homestead farming and nutrition practices; small-scale environmental benefits from solar installations replacing diesel generators in rural areas; and an increase in the capacity of communities to engage in the engagement of the whole community.

The project shall also achieve good health and well-being at the community level through improved capacity to anticipate and respond to climate risk, infrastructure, and supplies. An improved health-care system has a highly positive social and economic impact with reduced mortality rates and increased life expectancy. The overall improvements in the area of improved health care have, in general, a high economic rate of return.

* Data sourced from Malawi’s National Health Accounts, 2012.
* The 2023/2024 health sector budget is worth 8.7 per cent of the total government budget.


---
**Page 124**
---

23. While the sustainable development impact is important and considered to be high during project implementation, the sustainability of efforts is questioned as no structure is in place to ensure continuous financial support once the project implementation period is over. Overall, the ITAP rates the potential for sustainable development of the project as medium to high.

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient
**Scale: High**

24. Malawi has high vulnerability to climate change resulting in high-gross domestic product losses and reducing the progress on reducing poverty. The gender impacts of climate change severity are also significant, with women being disproportionately affected compared to men, especially marginalized groups, such as people with disabilities. The proposed project focuses largely on Malawi's southern region, as it is prioritized in the national adaptation plans. Malawi is exposed to both drought and floods, which have a negative impact on food security and livelihoods over time. Projected trends indicate that southern Malawi will be exposed increasingly to heat-related health impacts, with women being disproportionately affected by these changes.

25. Malawi's Human Development Index (HDI) value for 2011 is 0.531, putting the country in the low human development category of countries. Around half of the population lives below the poverty line in 2019–2020 with higher poverty levels in the southern part of the country.

26. Annual climate change adaptation expenditure needs for Malawi's health sector, adapted to be financed through international support, are estimated at USD 4.5 billion through to 2040 with three-quarters expected to be financed through international support. This reflects the current national budget situation, which is highly dependent on external funding.

27. Malawi has a very limited fiscal space to support adaptation action. Public expenditure available for climate change adaptation is insufficient to meet the needs across all sectors. The project therefore aims to complement existing adaptation to climate change in the health sector and the existing national-level policies.

28. Overall, the ITAP rates the needs of the recipient as high.

### 1.6 Country ownership
**Scale: High**

29. The no-objectition letter of Malawi has been provided.

30. The project is aligned with the National Climate Change Management Policy, the national adaptation action framework, the own country's national budget contribution, and the health national adaptation plan.

31. The project is also aligned with Malawi’s health sector strategic plan 2023–2030.

Malawi’s Health and Climate Change Governance Team of the Government of Malawi (HCCCT) endorsed the Ministry of Health and the Ministry of Health, with oversight by the HCCCT. Project implementation arrangements have been designed to ensure close engagement with both HCCCT and the Ministry of Health, with oversight by the HCCCT.

32. The project is also aligned with a range of other mechanisms of stakeholder engagement.

33. Save the Children has worked in Malawi over the past 40 years implementing a wide range of activities in education, health, nutrition, child protection, resilience, livelihoods, and social protection. These have been implemented through community-based participatory approaches sensitive to gender, mostly in agriculture. Save the Children is a recognized Ministry of Health


---
**Page 125**
---

partner in Malawi implementing facility and community-based reproductive, material, and newborn health interventions.

34. The Ministry of Health is responsible for developing, reviewing, and implementing health and related policies for the health sector; spearheading sector reforms; and developing and revising national health service delivery plans and standards for health service delivery. The ministry also has experience in delivering large donor-funded programmes.

35. The Malawi National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) [Malawi] identifies traditional development projects and their outputs and outcomes, including in the health sector. Experience with NAPs in climate resilience is less evident due to the established entity Save the Children International (SCI) being responsible for the implementation of the project. The methodology to assess the expected outcomes and outputs in climate resilience improvement and the monitoring and evaluation system are not yet clear as this will be addressed during a field visit presented. In the question-and-answer session, the Al mentioned that this shall be realized as a first step towards the development of a national adaptation plan (NAP) for Malawi and could also have provided more clarity on the budget, if this had been done beforehand.

36. The ITAP considers the alignment of the project with the priorities of the host country government and the sector. The alignment is considered to be high as the health services involved are high. Overall, the ITAP rates country ownership of the funding proposal as high.

### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

37. The total investment amount is USD 37 million with USD 33 million from GCF with a co-financing ratio of 11 per cent. This is considered a low co-finance ratio for an activity that requires significant government contributions (provincial and district levels).

38. A valuation and economic benefits has been conducted using aspects such as avoided costs on morbidity and mortality. The project scenarios generate consistently moderate or high returns on investment, with the internal rate of return being 10 per cent and the external social discount rate of 9 per cent. The financial viability of the project is not calculated as project interventions are considered to be cost-neutral. The expected economic benefits are largely substance-based, with no expected financial returns or revenues through commercialization.

39. Overall, the ITAP rates the efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal as medium.

### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

40. Overall, the funding proposal is comprehensive, and although there are various risks and weaknesses (as highlighted above), it shows a credible pathway to achieving notable impacts.

41. The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

42. It is recommended that, together with the Ministry of Health, the project develops a flexible monitoring and evaluation system for the project. This should include national and local governments and clear annual targets of increased health expenditure in the districts involved. The project should also develop a framework to ensure the sustainability of the improved health facility infrastructure approach in additional facilities. This is considered critical for ensuring the sustainability of the project.

43. It is recommended that the AE prepare a monitoring and evaluation system that explains how the monitoring and evaluation system will be used to assess the outcomes of adaptation activities and the quantification of the adaptation beneficiaries.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP244)

## Proposal name:
Climate Resilient Health and Well-Being for Rural Communities in southern Malawi (CHWRBC)

## Accredited entity:
Save the Children Australia

## Country:
Malawi

## Project:
Small

### Impact potential
Thank you for your positive comments and the medium-high rating for the impact potential. We acknowledge that while some activities are more impactful than others, while some activities will have a national impact (such as climate resilient health facilities) and support the Ministry of Health, we recognize there is still a need to extend infrastructure work to other districts in Malawi. The CHWRBC project aims to address this gap by focusing on areas that support the government’s efforts to attract or allocated more resources to the health sector over the coming years.

### Paradigm shift potential
Thank you for your comments. We acknowledge the gap regarding reliance on external funding. Malawi is the world's 8th poorest country, facing severe public financing challenges. Government revenue is largely spent on staff salaries and debt servicing. Reliance on development assistance has been a major challenge in recent years (e.g., 44% devolution in 2023), compounded by climate events, have been severe. Significant efforts are needed to address this gap. The CHWRBC project aims to support the government’s efforts for implementation and showcasing impact to build momentum for progress in the financing and delivery of climate-resilient health services in climate-vulnerable districts.

### Sustainable development potential
Thank you for the medium-high rating and positive comments on the project's contribution to the SDGs, including social, environmental, and gender co-benefits. The AEC acknowledges concerns about sustainability due to the lack of continuous financial support post-implementation. However, we are encouraged by the CHWRBC project’s recognition from the MoH regarding the need for investment in this area. With the projects engagement, support, and guidance provided through the Project Management Unit and Technical Advisory group, it will support the MoH in developing a financing plan and commitment for continued investments in climate-resilient health systems.

### Needs of the recipient
Thank you for the high rating. We agree that Malawi has high vulnerability to climate change and highly dependent on international finance. We believe the design of the CHWRBC project responds well to these needs through its aim of strengthening adaptation to climate change in the health sector.

# Country ownership

Thank you for the high rating along with positive feedback on the alignment of the CHWBRG with relevant national climate change and health priorities, policies and plans, in addition to the potential for co-financing from the private sector, based on previous experiences, and on the significant experience of Save the Children and of the Ministry of Health. Responses to comments on the M&E plan are presented in the Overall Remarks section below.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

Thank you for the medium rating. Despite the 11% co-financing ratio being deemed low by ITAP, we believe private sector co-financing is significant because:
- The private sector grants represent some of the largest in the climate and health sectors.
- We have recently received a $20 million grant from Rockefeller Foundation and are currently hosting the President of Global Health GSK in Malawi, who committed to supporting the project.

Co-financing has brought in strategic partnerships enhancing the project's credibility and reach. This includes parallel financing from Rockefeller Foundation and USAID, as well as providing private sector partnerships with the Clinton Health Access Initiative.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

We are pleased that ITAP has endorsed our project to the Board and address the following comments:
- We acknowledge the recommendation to develop a sustainable financial strategy with the MoH. Malawi aims for self-reliance by 2063, with each sectoral budget increasing over time. However, current healthcare expenditure costs have cost the economy an average of 1.7% of GDP annually over the past 3-5 years. Therefore, significant increases in public health financing are unlikely in the near future.
- We recommend that the project should focus on strengthening and implementing with the MoH, mobilizing resources to expand the work in climate change and health, and developing a Climate Change and Health Roadmap to support boosting funding for a climate-resilient healthcare system.
- The project's M&E plan will be further developed during project inception to comprehensively assess outcomes and outputs on climate resilience.

# Gender equality and Social Inclusion Assessment

## Introduction

1. Malawi is exposed to climate changes, and the country’s Southern Region is prioritized for climate adaptation in the National Adaptation Plan of Action (2006) and draft National Adaptation Plan (2016) due to its high exposure to climate risks and impacts, in combination with high vulnerability. The major climatic hazards include increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heavy rainfall, increasing temperatures, decreasing mean precipitation, increases in precipitation-related extremes and increases in temperature-related extremes, including heat waves, droughts, floods, and tropical cyclones, more frequent and stronger tropical cyclone landfalls, and increases in droughts (as defined in Annex 2, sections 3.1 and 3.2). The increasing frequency of extreme weather events is expected to lead to increased food insecurity, malnutrition, and water scarcity, which will disproportionately affect women, children, and other marginalized groups who face inequality and barriers in daily life. For example, during disasters (e.g., floods), displaced people are more likely to be women and those with young children, leading to higher rates of injury and death, as well as increased poverty and perpetuating vulnerability. Women and girls also become more vulnerable to gender-based violence, including sexual abuse, domestic violence, and child marriage. As another example, studies have also shown that climate change hazards are likely to increase the number of people living in poverty, with a disproportionate burden on women and children. Many women live in rural areas, where women account for 65% of smallholder farmers’, making them particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts.

2. The Climate Resilience Health and Wellbeing for Rural Communities in southern Malawi (GHWRC) project aims to improve practices, institutional capacity, and human capacity to improve the health and wellbeing of 719,000 people (out of which 65% are women), with a particular focus on women, children, and other marginalized groups. The project recognizes that women face significant structural vulnerability which need to be recognized and understood in order to enable equitable benefits from project implementation. This includes the need for gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) assessment for Malawi and the project area, and then for integrating GESI approaches, are embedded across all project activities.

The domains of analysis:

* Law, policy and institutions.
* Gender norms and roles.
* Roles, responsibilities and resources.

# Methodology

The literature review combined primary and secondary methods.

## Literature review of project documents, project reports and published research.

- Focus group discussions with men, women, pregnant and breastfeeding women, and people with disabilities were conducted in five languages using a common set of open-ended question guides and in local languages.
- Focus groups discussions were conducted with key district level staff and representatives of disability NGOs.


## Focus groups with women, and pregnant and breastfeeding women

Focus group discussion guides for women, and pregnant and breastfeeding women covered the same topics as highlighted above under the section on the men's focus groups.

## Focus groups with children

Focus group discussion guides for boys three years and older and girls aged 14 and 15. The focus group discussion guide for children included the following broad topics:

- Their perception and understanding of climate change.
- Their views about the impacts of climate change, with some examples, including whether climate change affected livelihoods.
- Their views on the use of WASH facilities in their schools.
- Their views on priority climate adaptations areas.

## Focus group discussions with Men

Focus groups were designed to cover a range of topics, prompted both by the initial project design and by the need to elicit information on the gendered impacts of climate change (to inform the GESI component of the project. Topics shown in the following broad topics which were used as departure points for discussion) were solicited:

- Their experiences and perceptions of climate hazards affecting their community.


---
**Page 133**
---

* Their perceptions and experiences of the manner in which climate change affects health, and of the most common climate-change-induced diseases, particular attention was paid to how the health of children, pregnant and breastfeeding mothers may be affected by climate change.
* Their perceptions regarding the availability of food (e.g., staple foods, vegetables, fruits, etc.), water, and other resources in their communities (e.g., fuelwood, firewood, etc.); the cost of these resources; and the food groups, etc.) and the existence of any partners supporting the provision of complementary foods.
* Their perceptions of access to antenatal care during disasters and post-disaster periods.
* Their perceptions regarding the availability of health services for pregnant women, children, and breastfeeding mothers to improve the latter's health and nutrition so as to minimize the impacts of climate change.
* Their perceptions regarding the inclusion of women in village-level decision-making structures.


## Focus groups and key informant interviews with people with disabilities

The five focus group discussion and key informant interview guides for people with disabilities included the following topics: what are the barriers that people with disabilities face when accessing health care, mobility, and their roles (including in decision making on any areas e.g., health, nutrition) and types of projects; what are the challenges faced by people with disabilities in accessing health services during disasters and post-disaster periods? What are the challenges faced by people with disabilities in accessing health care services, particularly during climate crises; and what is the representation of people with disabilities in village-level decision structures.


### Key Findings

1. Malawi's gender inequality is pronounced, as indicated by the international gender indices (Table 1), which show that women are disadvantaged.
2. National gender and disability policies and legislation exist, and Malawi is signatory to regional, continental and international equality agreements. Gender and, to a lesser extent, disability are discussed in some national development plans, but there has been little progress on the implementation of health and agriculture.
3. Policy formulation is weak, with few strategies and action plans to operationalize policies, and little evidence of monitoring and evaluating plans, strategies or policies. These challenges are compounded by the lack of a gender-sensitive budgeting process, which limits the accountability and reporting vertically (between line ministries at national and district level) and horizontally (between line ministries and sectoral departments). This is further exacerbated by the (lack of) implementation of gender and social inclusion as cross-cutting issues in sectoral policies.
4. There has been some progress on the decentralization process, with the devolution of resources to districts, and in decision making for a zoom-a-council level While there has been some shift in gender norms among women and men, this is not reflected in the implementation of gender equality and political decision-making processes).
5. Neglected groups include people with disabilities, limited access to resources and assets in multiple domains, and at multiple levels (including down to the intra-household level). Although some parts of Malawi are inhabited by ethnic minorities, there has been little progress on their inclusion in development.
6. Women's land rights are weak. Our daughters inherit our land, but our sons are their "water fields". Matrilineal marital and land tenure systems are still strongly patriarchal. Under customary law, women's access to land is also typically mediated through male family members.

16. Gender-based violence remains a significant problem, including in post-disaster settings[1-4] and girl children continue to have fewer opportunities than boys children, reinforcing situations of gender inequality.

* **Ethnically Malev comprises several tribes:** 5.0 million people (34.4%) are Chews, 3.3 million people (16.9%) are Lomees, 2.3 million people (13.4%) are Yoa, 1.8 million people (10.4%) were from other tribes, and the remaining 5.7% were from other ethnic groups.
* **The population is largely rural:** 86% live in villages (in four major clusters of villages), with a population density of about 23 per km². The population has increased by only 0.7 percentage points between 1992 and 2014, from 54% to 54.7%. The Central Region accounts for 43%, the Southern Central Region and 13% lived in the Northern Region.
* **Women are more likely than men to be unemployed:** Informal employment is more prevalent in rural versus urban areas, and more prevalent among women than men.
* **Labour force participation rate is lower for women (72%) than men (82%) and unemployment higher for women (15% compared with 9%). Women are less likely to receive remuneration paid for their labour (50% of women reporting not being paid for work compared to 36% of men).
* **Low levels of education among women:** Only about one-third of women aged 25–49 years have completed secondary school, compared with two-thirds of men. Women are more likely than men to be illiterate (10% versus 7%). Women are also more likely to live in households headed by themselves, due to their engagement in low-income activities and unpaid care.
* **Limited access to reproductive health services:** This is reflected in high fertility rates, and inadequate access to systems and services including education and healthcare.
* **Obstacles to accessing healthcare include distance from the nearest facility, lack of money, and lack of transportation.** The most commonly reported problems included "distance to the health facility (56%) - distant facilities are often not equipped with essential drugs or equipment, and women have difficulty in obtaining permission to go for treatment (18%). Women have poor control over reproductive decisions because they do not have access to family planning services.
* **Child nutrition is poor:** 37% of children under age 5 stunted (short for their age), 9% wasted (too thin for their height) and 20% overweight or obese (too fat for their height). Feeding practices of only 8% of children aged 6–23 months meet the minimum acceptable standards.
* **The Malawi Population-Based HIV Impact Assessment (MPHIA):** carried out by the Malawi Bureau of Statistics in collaboration with UNAIDS, WHO and other partners. The prevalence rate for women was 12.8%, compared to 5% among adult men. In 2016, 4.3% of young women were living with HIV (ages 15–19 years).
* **Disability prevalence rates for Malawians aged 5 or older with at least one disability:** 15.4% from the general population and 28.7% among those aged 60+ years, compared to a global average of just under one functioning problem or disability**,** while Alzheimer’s prevalence is as 0.9%. Chronic non-communicable diseases (CNDs) are increasing in Malawi, with limited access, particularly at secondary level, where girls have poorer educational outcomes than boys, and women have higher rates of CNDs.
