origin, disability, property, birth or other status” (section X). Specifically, gender equality is one of the fundamental principles, section 13(a) which aims “To ensure equality for women through: (a) full participation in all spheres of Malawian society on the basis of equality; (b) the implementation of the provisions of this Constitution and other laws to promote and protect the rights of women; and (c) the implementation of policies to address social issues such as domestic violence, security of the person, lack of medical care, and access to education.”

20. Malawi is a signatory to major international commitments to achieving gender equality, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action, and the Southern African Development Community Protocol on Gender and Development.

1. The Gender Equality Act was passed in 2013**, with the aim “to promote gender equality, equal opportunities and non-discrimination between men and women in all spheres of Malawian society, to prohibit and provide redress for sex discrimination, harmful practices and sexual harassment; to provide for the establishment of a National Gender Mainstreaming Strategy and Implementation Plan; and to provide for matters connected or incidental thereto.” The National Gender Policy 2015 aims “to reduce gender inequality and enhance ‘participation of women in all spheres of Malawian society, to promote equality between men and women, to advocate for increased access to, retention and completion of quality education for girls and boys; to ensure that women have equal access to health services including reproductive health care, the status of HIV and AIDS; to strengthen gender mainstreaming in all sectors of the economy; to reduce poverty levels among women; to promote women’s economic empowerment; to protect women from violence; and to strengthen the capacity of the National Gender Machinery.”


Note: The Republic of Malawi, Ministry of Labour, Community Development and Persons with Disabilities (CRPD), which provides the international policy framework on disability, in 2009. The Government also developed a draft Disability Act in 2013, which was reviewed by the UN Committee on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.

Disability Act was passed in 2019**, with the aim “to provide for the equalization of opportunities for persons with disabilities; to promote and protect their rights; to ensure that they have access to education; to establish a Disability Trust Fund; and to provide for matters connected or incidental thereto.” The National Disability Mainstreaming Strategy and Implementation Plan 2018-2023, which aims to ensure that concrete steps are taken for people with disabilities to access the same fundamental rights as other citizens. The National Disability Mainstreaming Strategy and Implementation Plan (2018) is a National Disability Mainstreaming Strategy and Implementation Plan** which aims to bridge the gap between the National Disability Policy 2015, the National Youth Policy and the National Sports Policy Act (2010), National Youth Policy and the National Sports Policy. The Disability Act (2019) incorporates provisions for the establishment of a National Disability Trust Fund. The draft Disability Act is also a draft Persons with Disabilities Bill 2019 which seeks to build on the shortfalls in the current Disability Act as well as the UN CRPD, and to strengthen related reporting mechanisms on disability**. Despite availability of all these policy commitments, children and adults with disabilities

continue to experience significant barriers to sanitation, education, health and protection[28]. A range of other policies, laws, strategies and international agreements make reference to gender and Disability [Table 2Errnot a valid bookmark self-reference.].

### 23. In Malawi, individuals with abnormity are recognized as having a disability and are especially vulnerable to human rights violations. The Government has implemented various national development measures, for example, the National Action Plan on Persons with Abnormality (2018), a multi-sectoral National Disability Policy (2017-2025) and the National Youth Policy (2013). In 2016, the Ministry of Justice and Magistrates across the country in prosecuting cases of attacks against persons with abnormality, among other human rights violations. The Ministry of Justice and Prison Services, in collaboration with UNICEF, was working towards the re-introduction of a functional e-case management system, which had developed the capacity to track and monitor all cases of abuse against children and adults with disabilities, including those of mobile court[29].

### 24. Strategies and mechanisms have been put in place to improve the situation of youth[30]. Malawi’s national development frameworks and sectoral policies are concerned with youth development issues, particularly in relation to education, employment, health, social protection, gender equality, youth participation and representation in policy processes. Malawian youth are entitled to the rights enshrined in the Constitution (1964), the National Youth Council Act (2013), the Child Protection Act (1985), ILO Minimum Age Convention (1973), Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC, 1989) and African Charter on the Rights and Welfare of the Child (ACRWC, 1990). Among these is the National Youth Council Act (2013), which includes provisions for the “promotion, coordination and implementation of youth policies and programmes”[31]. Other key strategies include the National Youth Council of Malawi (NYCM); and to further provide for matters incidental thereto or connected with it. The NYCM is a statutory body established under the National Youth Council Act 2013. Also worth mentioning: the Childcare, Protection and Justice Act (2016), the HIV/AIDS Prevention Management and Control Act (2004) and the Anti-human Trafficking Act (2017). The National Action Plan on Trafficking in Persons (2017-2022), the National Strategy on Adolescent Girls and Young women (2015-2025), the National Policy on Children and Youth (2013) and the National Child Labour Elimination Plan on Child Labour (2019-2025). The National Youth Policy (2013) aims at providing an enabling environment for youth to participate in decision-making processes. One of the main strategic objectives of the National Youth Policy (2013) is to provide a framework that guides youth development and the implementation of youth policies and programmes. The policy also provides guidelines on how young people can be involved in decision making, planning and implementation of youth policies and programmes. The policy is a revised version of the 1996 National Youth Policy and was adopted to address the needs of young people in Malawi. The policy has seven priority areas for action: i) youth participation and leadership; ii) youth economic empowerment; iii) strengthening the capacity of youth organizations; iv) promoting gender equality, equity and inclusion[32]; v) youth health and nutrition; and vii) social services, sports, recreation and culture[33]. Whilst these measures are in place to protect children’s rights and promote their development, challenges exist in the implementation of these measures[34].

25. Currently gender, children and disability fall under the remit of the same ministry, the Ministry of Gender, Community Development and Social Welfare (MoGCDSW)**1**, which is mandated to promote gender equality and protect the welfare of Malawian women, men, girls and boys to become self-reliant and active participants in development. The MoGCDSW has developed a National Strategic Plan (NSP) 2016-2030 under which the Ministry include reduced gender inequalities among men, women, boys and girls; enhanced effective and sustainable access of women and children to social services; improved quality of life for women and development of all children; resilience and self-reliance among vulnerable groups of people; and improved lives of persons with disabilities and elderly people.
26. Gender equality is a key element in the Government’s vision for Malawi in Malawi's long-term medium-term development plans. Vision 2050**3** states that gender inequalities shall be addressed to improve the socio-economic status of women, men, boys and girls and to ensure equal opportunities for all.
Fundamentally, gender equality will be advanced at all levels through multi-sectoral approaches and the implementation of the National Strategic Plan (NSP) 2016-2030 under which the Ministry is mandated. Vision 2050 shall have eliminated all gender based discrimination and harmful practices, including child marriage, female genital mutilation/cutting, early pregnancy and teenage motherhood.
gender transformative approaches are promoted as part of approaches to better target different groups, and the implementation of the National Strategic Plan (NSP) 2016-2030 under which the Ministry is mandated. The NSP will also promote gender equality in all sectors, policies, programmes and development interventions. It will ensure that women are not left behind as a result of poverty, lack of education and health services, and that there shall be no violation of the social protection system.
The Malawi Growth and Development Strategy (MGDS) 2017**4** aims to move Malawi to a “productive, competitive and resilient economy” by 2035. The MGDS is underpinned by four pillars: inclusive economic growth; poverty reduction; development; empowerment of persons with disabilities; and social inclusion. It also aims at disaster risk reduction and resilience building, as well as the promotion of gender equality. The MGDS will also build an egalitarian society where women are not discriminated against based on sex, disability and other differences.
The National Climate Change Policy Management 2016**5** includes gender, population dynamics and vulnerability to climate change in all climate change strategies, plans and programmes. And commits to include climate change issues into the development agenda.

Key policies relating to resilience, climate change and disaster risk reduction also pay due attention to the importance of gender equality and social inclusion.
The National Resilience Strategy**6** has four key pillars: inclusive economic growth; poverty reduction; empowerment of persons with disabilities; and social inclusion. The strategy is underpinned by a cross-cutting issue relating to women’s empowerment as a cross cutting issue. The National Resilience Strategy also recognises the reality that resilience is not static, but rather dynamic, and that people are in different categories: hanging in, stepping up and stepping out, with different interventions and trajectories for each.
The National Resilience Strategy**6** states that “the implementation of the National Resilience Strategy shall ensure that all people, irrespective of their geographical location, age, gender, ethnicity, religion or other status are resilient to economic and environmental shocks that affect their lives and livelihoods. To effect this, it is important for resilience strategies to address the underlying causes of poverty and deprivations, and socio-economic inequities of affected people, and be reflected in their design, implementation and monitoring.
The National Resilience Strategy**6** also recognises the importance of gender equality, including through targeted agricultural interventions for women and vulnerable groups.

28. The National Climate Change Management Policy 2016**7** includes gender, population dynamics and vulnerability to climate change in all climate change strategies, plans and programmes affecting the disadvantaged groups into all climate change strategies, plans and Programmes.
And commits to include climate change issues into the development agenda.

31. Despite the existence of an array of policies and laws around gender equality and equal rights regardless of ability, Malawi suffers from considerable gaps in implementation[2]. This gap was particularly exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic, which further undermined progress made under MDG 5 and II were not achieved. Likewise, the National Disability Mainstreaming Strategy and Implementation Road Map (NDMSIR) has been largely ineffective.

32. The gaps in implementing gender or social inclusion policies within adaptation and resilience policy and programs are due to a lack of clear definitions of disability, gender, and social inclusion; weak socio-cultural beliefs and practices hindering GESI progress at all levels; insufficient involvement of women and disabled people with disabilities (DWD) in the development of policies and programs; inadequate data and systematic monitoring and evaluation; a lack of well-defined roles, responsibilities, and coordination mechanisms for disability, gender, and social inclusion; weak mainstreaming of gender or socially inclusive budgeting, and the failure to integrate GESI considerations into resilience policies and programs.

33. While gender focal points have been designated in Ministries, Departments, and Agencies within the public sector to oversee the integration of gender considerations in their respective sectors, they often lack knowledge on disability issues and are not adequately supported by dedicated disability mainstreaming policies. Additionally, policies tend to assign responsibilities to other ministries without clearly defining the mechanisms for coordination between them. Furthermore, many agencies do not have a mandate or incorporate these duties into their strategic planning, budgeting processes, or implementation efforts; and neither do they have adequate resources.

34. In terms of disability issues, the Government of Malawi recognizes the valuable contributions of people with disabilities to political, social, and economic development, however there is still a notable gap between policy rhetoric and practice in mainstreaming disability considerations into national policies and plans of specific disability-related targets and budget allocations within the program-based budgets of many government agencies. This is due to several factors including lack of capacity for disability mainstreaming in different sectors, including financial and capacity limitations; insufficient expertise in disability matters; and a lack of adequate resources to support disability mainstreaming[3]. The country also faces needs full participation of the disabled community itself. In Malawi, processes of policies and programmes are not inclusive of people with disabilities and their organizations, which results in low uptake with disabilities. Government interventions end up focusing on social protection payments, with very little resources allocated for disability-inclusive education programs and providing much-needed assistive devices and disability-friendly infrastructure[4].

35. For people with albinism, additional concerns also persist regarding the prevalence of discrimination, stigmatization, and violence against them including forced marriages, abduction, killings of adults and children with albinism. There are still considerable gaps around: holding all involved perpetrators accountable; ensuring access to justice for victims of crimes committed against persons with albinism; ensuring access to assistive devices and healthcare materials; fostering cross- sector coordination among relevant stakeholders in the development of policies and programs, and involving adults and children with albinism in matters that affect them. There are also identified gaps in resource allocation, coordination, awareness-raising efforts, and capacity building[5].

# Social Norms and Beliefs

## Women as a Highly Patriarchal Society and Gender Inequalities are Deeply Entrenched[1]

Women are expected to be submissive to their husbands and male family members, and poor knowledge by women of their rights in Malawi is a major factor that contributes to this. This patriarchal society constrains the opportunities that are available to women and girls. Women typically have less access to resources than men do, which limits their ability to make choices, different consumption patterns, access to information and participation in decision-making[2].

## The Patriarchal Society

In Malawi, women are expected to be submissive to their husbands and male family members. This means that women have an equal chance of getting a job paid, earning income and owning or inheriting land[3]. However, women are often denied access to resources such as land, education, credit, and financial services because they are seen as less valuable than men in the society. This creates barriers to women's involvement in economic activities[4].

## Polygamy

Polygamous marriages are common in Malawi, with 20% of married women having more than one husband. Women who have multiple husbands face a higher risk of domestic violence and sexual violence, including through threats of divorce and abandonment that force women into accepting their situation or leaving the marriage[5].

## Traditional Gender Approaches

Traditional gender approaches that target women run the risk of disaverting polygamous households from accessing services. Women in these households are often excluded from decision-making processes even though they receive little tangible support from their husbands[6].

## Malawi's Demographic Transition

Malawi is currently experiencing a demographic transition, which is more common in much of Southern and Central regions. However, this descent occurs within the context of a patriarchal social structure that continues to place women at risk for gender-based violence and poor control decisions about it (e.g., which fetus they have or male extended family members)[7].

## Women's Rights

Women are often seen as passive recipients of assistance rather than being supported to play active roles in society[8]. In most Malawian households, women are responsible for household chores such as cooking and cleaning. They also provide assistance rather than being supported to play active roles in society[9].

## Persons with Disabilities

Persons with disabilities are seen as if or different and consequently they are often excluded from even participating in decision-making processes that affect their lives[10]. The majority of persons with disabilities live in poverty, which is a major barrier to their economic and social rights. Those with psychosocial or intellectual disabilities are sometimes treated differently from other people with disabilities because of the negative attitudes towards them.

## Gender-Based Violence

Gender-based violence (GBV) is a serious problem for women and girls with disabilities[11]. The effects of gender-based violence on persons with disabilities, including those with mental health conditions or intellectual disabilities, where the effects of negative social attitudes and perceptions combine with sexism and misogyny and women with these disabilities are disproportionately subject to gender-based violence.

### Violence

High levels of stigma towards especially women persons with psychosocial and intellectual disabilities is even reflected through derogatory terminology used in Malawian legislation and judgments to refer to people with psychosocial and intellectual disabilities.

### Child abuse

Children with disabilities are disproportionately vulnerable to sexual, economic exclusion and discrimination. This extends across multiple facets of Malawian society, economy and culture. Children with disabilities are particularly vulnerable to violence in several areas. This is all closely connected, i.e., health care access for people with disabilities in rural Malawi is hindered by a lack of infrastructure, limited availability of trained personnel, and the cost of services. In recent years to enhance educational access for all, both children and youth still face systematic exclusion from education. Children with disabilities are often excluded from accessing education at primary, secondary and tertiary education. A UNICEF study directly links barriers to education access to child abuse and violence against children with disabilities in Malawi.

### Persons with albinism

Persons with albinism are particularly vulnerable to hate crime attacks and killings due to societal misconceptions that both persons with albinism and albinos command powerful magic for good or evil. In some cases, perpetrators of the attacks and killings are close family members or other trusted members of the public. In 2015, there were 34 reported cases of hate crime against persons with albinism in Malawi. Among these are among the key suspects answering murder charges involving a person with albinism. Additionally, children with albinism face discrimination and exclusion from education, health care programmes, and services. This leads to a variety of problems, the most common of which are financial constraints, lack of access to appropriate medical care, and social isolation. These factors contribute leading to stigma and discrimination, and superstitious beliefs. For example, there are beliefs that if an individual with albinism is killed, their body parts will be used for witchcraft or sold on the black market.

### Cultural practices

Cultural and traditional practices are strong in Malawi, and often impede progress towards equality and social inclusion of persons with disabilities, especially non-sexual enclaves. Examples of cultural practices outlined in the UNICEF report on traditional practices include:

- **a.** Female genital mutilation (FGM): The practice is common among girls who are born or practiced after initiation, when a husband and wife fall ill, or after the death of a child.
- **b.** Kusasa funds: "shaking off of dust", a practice in which a girl or boy who has just gone through initiation is covered into having sex to facilitate the process.

c. Lobola: the paying of dowry, which incentivizes early marriage;
d. Land-granting: a practice in which a widow is stripped of her belongings and cast out of the family home by her in-laws.

45. Traditional beliefs about witchcraft and vampires are also very strong and can lead to victimization and targeting of children and older women, resulting in ostracism, violence and even death[28]. Perceived witches are often targeted for their perceived power over life and death, and sometimes based on the belief that a person would get rich if they raped a woman or girl with a disability.[29] For example, in 2015, a 34-year-old woman was killed by her husband after he believed she had turned his bones into magic[30].

46. Forced child marriage, poverty, famine, firmly entrenched harmful social norms and beliefs are one of the critical obstacles to realizing children’s rights in the country; particularly affecting girls, these include gender-based violence, early pregnancy, and maternal mortality. Children with disabilities are often seen with albinism and witchcraft persecution, all of which violate human rights and slow down social and economic development[31]. Child trafficking is also a serious problem in Malawi, with 20% of children experiencing violent displacement at home, with 17% of these cases being severe; corporal punishment is common in schools. The number of children who are victims of child labour has increased by 45%, from the loss of MK3 billion, equivalent to 4.13% of GDP[32]. Further, child trafficking with a labor-oriented purpose is also a serious problem in Malawi; 70% of children trafficked for this purpose were girls and often forced to go work in tobacco farms in the country’s northern parts. Traffickers exploit teenage girls who are vulnerable due to poverty or lack of education[33]. Child trafficking also takes place from Malawi to other African (i.g., Tanzania, Zambia, Mozambique) or Middle Eastern countries such as Saudi Arabia and Oman. About 51% of children aged 5-17 were involved in child labour, and almost 70% of those children worked in agriculture[34].

Roles, Responsibilities and Resources

47. Access to healthcare is gendered in Malawi; this creates risks for pregnant women. Maternal mortality rates are high due to lack of access to skilled birth attendants, especially during antenatal care in the first trimester of pregnancy, and by the time of birth half of mothers have had access to four or fewer prenatal visits[35]. The lack of access to healthcare services also contributes to both inadequate antenatal care and limited sexual reproductive health and rights services. This is particularly a problem for women with disabilities, who are not targeted for special service provision[36].

48. Rates of adolescent pregnancy are high, with many women having their first pregnancy between the ages of 15-19; although the contraception prevalence rate has increased, there are still significant

# Health care is even less accessible for women with disabilities

## Background

### Low literacy and education attainment

- In the 2018 census: 71.6% of men reported being literate compared with only 65.9% of women.
- Only 43% of women aged 15–19 years have completed primary school, while 10% have completed secondary school or above.

### Social background is a strong driver of educational attainment

- Children of educated mothers are far more likely to attend school and complete their education than children whose mothers did not receive any formal education.
- Only 47% of women aged 25–39 years who were born in the Republic of Malawi have completed secondary education (versus 16% intended to facilitate access to education), only 41% of whom are employed, compared with 80% and 85%, respectively, for men.

### Barriers to education

- Only 3.2% of primary children have disabilities, and only 2.3% in secondary schools.
- Nine percent of children aged 6–17 years do not attend school because their parents cannot afford the fees.
- Barriers to education for children with disabilities include being refused entry, bullying, or abuse.

## Conclusion

**Page 145**
---

harassment from peers and/or teachers, difficulties in transport, inaccessible infrastructure, and non-inclusive teaching pedagogy. Girls with disabilities face increased stigma and discrimination, as well as school-related gender-based violence [16]. Additionally, there is low participation of people with disabilities in decision-making processes at all levels, including in the development of policies, guidelines, learning materials, and encouragement for their participation [10]. Due to significant safety and security concerns, many parents are reluctant to send their children to school.

54. Malawi does not invest sufficiently to counter the extensive deprivations endured by its children, owing to the poor quality of education, inadequate access to health services, and lack of social protection for children [17]. Funding for a number of social sectors that are crucial to the wellbeing of children are highly dependent on donor funding. For example, in 2015-16, only 38% of total government expenditure was allocated to education, nutrition and WASH remain highly dependent on donor support (over 90%) [18]. Child protection remains a particular concern as only about $4 million is allocated per year, and social protection in general is overwhelmingly donor-dependent [19].

Within the household, roles are strongly gendered [20]. Men typically dominate within the household due to their greater control over resources. Women have less access to productive assets such as land or livestock, for example what crops to grow or whether or not to migrate for work. This has persisted overtime, even though women’s economic empowerment is a key priority of the government and development partners [21]. Women also have less responsibility (sole or joint) for decisions relating to their own healthcare, to caring for children or elderly or other family members, and in some cases, to managing household finances. Women are more likely than men to cook for dinner, when to visit their own family members. Findings from a recent project showed that the women who had control over their own income earned was 20% more than those whose husbands controlled it [22].

56. Over time, there have been increases in the number of women to be able to make decisions, particularly around household purchases (from 38% in 2010 to 49% in 2015) and in making household purchases (from 30% in 2010 to 55% in 2015-16), although this is typically thought to be due to an increase in women’s economic empowerment. Women are more likely than men to make decisions about food, water, and sanitation [23]. In general, decision-making capacity is linked to age, education, and employment status [24].

There have been some efforts by the government to address gender inequality through policies that may have control of resources and greater decision-making powers. However, perceived contradiction of gender equality with traditional values has led to a lack of implementation in some areas. For example, under the “Building Resilience and Adapting to Climate Change” programme [25], one woman noted that after her husband died, she stated for the first time that she could make decisions about household purchases. However, she was still subject to her husband’s family, who retaliated by denying her access to land or cutting off familial support. Where women in male-headed households had been able to earn their own money, for example through livestock farming [26], they were often unable to retain the income.

Decision-making, leadership and participation

58 Decision making in the public sphere in Malawi remains male-dominated. Despite having recently had a female president (Joyce Banda from April 2014 to May 2014), women’s representation in parliament remains low. In 2013, only one woman was elected as a Member of Parliament by the National Assembly Education Act. Scores for Malawi in the political participation index of the Global Gender Gap Index are fairly low (Table 1). In the 2015 election, there were no female candidates from the ruling party, and the first female candidate was the first ever female speaker of parliament.

59 Less than one-third of women at national level are involved in political participation by women at community level. The 2015-16 Afrobarometer survey found that Malawian women are less likely than men to be involved in political activities (34% vs. 48%). Women are also less likely to participate in public meetings or offer to raise an issue (23% vs. 26%), attend a campaign rally (49% vs. 63%) and contact an MP (5% vs. 17%).

60 The Afrobarometer survey shows increasing support for women’s equal participation in politics relative to men, but this is not reflected at the national level. Women are more likely than men to say that women offer a better alternative for leadership.

61 Although women hold leadership positions, although the numbers of women in leadership positions has increased, most traditional leaders are still men. This is underpinned by patriarchal and social norms, which gender equality initiatives have not yet been able to change significantly (see Box 2). Decision-making fora remain more accessible to men than to women, and notable exceptions (where a woman holds the position of chairperson) are rare.

62 In local governance structures, for example at district, group village, area and village level, increasing efforts are made for gender-equal representation, as well as for participation by people with disabilities. However, there is still a lack of awareness about disability issues in these structures, for example those undertaken by UN agencies and NGOs.

63 People with disabilities face significant barriers to full and equal access, as barriers that prevent disabled people from participating in community events and committees. There is almost no presence of women with disabilities in local governance structures. The Malawi Constitution (2015) provides for the establishment of five special interest groups in local councils, it does not explicitly include provisions for the representation of women or persons with disabilities.

64 Consequently, women’s voices are even more invisible within the decision-making landscape. This is largely due to negative attitudes and lack of understanding about disability issues amongst men and women with disabilities. Limited levels of education and economic security for women and girls with disabilities also contribute to their exclusion in accessible formats for campaigns, political and decision-making processes at all levels.

65 Children are not included as a specific group in the Malawi Constitution or in the national policy making and capacity building for children, such that children can feel empowered in society. The Malawi government has developed a National Policy on Children (2014) which includes provisions for consultation and involves minimal participation[**]. Further, even though children’s views may be sought through consultations, they are not necessarily put into practice, and very often younger children

**Page 147**

Access to and Control of Resources

Land ownership in Malawi is highly unequal. Recognizing the importance of land as a productive asset, the law provides for equal inheritance rights between sons and daughters. However patriarchal norms and traditional cultural beliefs typically override the law and disadvantage women. Whilst the population of Malawi is dominated by the youth, this cohort has poor access to productive resources such as land, livestock, tools and finance. A recent study found that only 22%16 of the youth in the Central region of Malawi have access to farming land17.

Limited access to credit markets also hinders women’s ability to engage in both subsistence agriculture and income-generating activities with low returns18. Within agricultural households, women tend to be responsible for food production while men tend to grow cash and export-oriented crops. Whilst women and men may work together to grow crops, the division of labour is often unequal. Women are more likely than men to lack access to markets as they both physical the example lack of access to a bicycle or capacity to afford transport19. However, women in Malawi produce up to 80% of the country’s food.

Recognizing the gender gap in agriculture, a number of climate-smart agriculture programmes have achieved significant improvements in productivity and increased access of women to resources20. Positive changes in the gender gap in agriculture is anticipated to improve incomes for women and increase their ability to invest in productive assets such as livestock.

Whilst access to formal credit remains low among women, rotating savings and credit associations and other informal lending schemes are more common. However, these schemes are often dominated by women who are typically the registered members. However, when income is generated through such

# Gender-Based Violence (GBV)

## Gender-Based Violence (GBV) is a "serious, prevalent, and deeply entrenched problem in Malawi" with 41% of women reporting having experienced GBV at some point in their lives [1]. The 2015 Demographic and Health Survey found that 34% of Malawian women (ages 15 and over) had experienced physical violence by a current or former partner. Women appear to be high tolerance for GBV, with 10% of men and 13% of women believing that a husband is justified in beating his wife if she does not obey him [2]. The survey also found that 45% of women who had been beaten argued, going out without telling him, neglecting the children and refusing sexual intercourse [3].

## Strategies to address GBV include: strengthening the legal framework for addressing the problem, including the Constitution, Vision 2050, and the Gender Equality Act. The National Action Plan on Violence Against Women (2014-20) and the National Action Plan to Prevent Gender-Based Violence (2014-20).

## Survivors of GBV are often stigmatized by their communities and may experience greater poverty. Marital rape is not specified in the penal code, so although women can seek justice under the Prohibition of Female Genital Mutilation Act 2005 and the Child Protection Act 1994, it is rare in practice this rarely happens.

## Gender-based violence among adolescents is unacceptable high, as one in five girls and one in seven boys have experienced sexual violence. The most common perpetrators are family members (37% of perpetrators are parents) [4].

## Child marriage is also prevalent, and in 2015 Malawi had the highest rate of child marriage in the world. The 2015 Marriage, Divorce and Family Relations Law increased the minimum age of marriage for women to 18 years old.

15 to 18, but the Constitution allows children as young as 15 to marry with parental consent [10]. The average age of marriage for girls (as compared with 23 for men), for 47% of women marry before the age of 18. A significant concern is marriage at age 15: 9% of girls in rural areas and 4% of girls in urban areas are married by this age [10]. This trend has been recognized by the National Strategy on Ending Child Marriages 2018-23.

79. The prevalence of child marriage is also a major driver of young women aged 15–19 beginning childbearing in 2015–16[4], and is a major driver of young women of school-going age dropping out of school [10].

80. There is a notable lack of data concerning the nature and scope of sexual violence targeting women and girls with disabilities, and the extent to which they are exposed to other forms of violence such as intimate partner violence and other forms of GBV as women who do not have a disability. They are often treated as invisible and their experiences of GBV are rarely documented or reported [10]. Women with disabilities receive less education than women without disabilities, and are less likely to be employed in paid work. Women with disabilities face barriers around relationships or access and reproductive health care[4]. This is based on social norms and biases that view women with disabilities as being unable to have sexual relations or bear children [10].

81. Women with disabilities are more vulnerable than other women to the negative impacts of climate change, including increased exposure to climate hazards such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, storms, and wildfires. They face barriers around access to information about climate change and its effects on their lives.

82. The gendered health impacts of climate change are described in the Feasibility Study [Amenz 3. Section 2]. This study highlights that women with disabilities have a higher vulnerability than other women, including women who do not have disabilities, to the negative impacts of climate change and extremes. Women face multiple barriers and vulnerabilities in the context of climate change and extreme weather events. These include limited access to information about climate change and its effects on their lives.

83. Women with disabilities are more vulnerable than other women to the negative impacts of climate change, as they are often economically dependent on men and lack the resources to adapt to climate-related challenges [10]. Moreover, women have limited power and decision-making authority.

both in their households and in their communities in Malawi. They lack control over finances and assets, and their representation in community politics is often inadequate (1). This lack of influence hampers their ability to participate in climate adaptation strategies and policies.

### 83. Childbearing

Childbearing is a significant factor in women's vulnerability as they are responsible for securing water, food, and fuel for their households; climate change exacerbates these responsibilities. Women often have multiple roles within the household, which can be time-consuming and energy demanding (1). For instance, gendered rules mean that women typically have responsibility for fetching water and cooking, while men may be more involved in income-generating activities. This has implications for women and girls in terms of time taken to collect water, as well as potentially exposing them to climate-related hazards such as floods or droughts (1). For example, the 2015 Cyclone Idai had significant impacts on women who walked long distances to access water (1). In addition, collecting water in times of scarcity places a greater burden on women and girls. The 2019 Malawi Flood Post Disaster Needs Assessment (PDNA) found that 86% of women reported that their mental health has been affected by climate change (1).

### 84. Women are often disproportionately affected by disasters, both in the short- and long-term

For instance, during Cyclone Idai in March 2019, a total of 35,706 households were affected; the largest number of women were counted among the internally displaced people, with 63% of them in shelters (1). Women are also more likely to be left behind when families evacuate their homes during disasters. This is often due to coping strategies and concerns about increased pregnancy as a result. In the longer term, the floods had significant impacts on women's livelihoods; for example, women were less likely than men to engage in income-generating activities due to lack of opportunities and loss of livestock assets. Similarly, in 2015, Cyclone Idai displaced over 374,000 people across Malawi (1). The Women's Environment & Development Organization (WEDO) found that women were more likely than men to be affected by the disaster; for example agricultural livelihoods which had been developed at their dignity in housing camps due to the lack of provision of gender-sensitive WASH services.

85\. The sexual and reproductive health and rights of women are particularly affected in times of natural disasters \({ }^{1}\). Women are disproportionately affected by chronic malnutrition and are more vulnerable to climate-induced food and nutrition insecurity, particularly during pregnancy and breastfeeding\(\mathrm{M}^{\text {a }}\) . 86\. In contrast to the long-term effects of disasters, child marriages are known to increase following shocks and disasters. The literature suggests that this is due to economic hardship and lack of access to education \({ }^{345}\). For instance, after Covid-19, a helpline reporting child marriage saw an \(82 \%\) increase in calls, whilst teen pregnancy rates also increased by 70\% \({ }^{6}\).

87\. A study conducted by the University of Michigan found that during the 2015 drought in Malawi, the number of marriages and pregnancies increased. This was associated with a higher number of early marriages in \(49 \%\) of households \({ }^{345}\). The study also found that women who were married before age 18 had an \(87 \%\) increase in childbearing compared to those who were not married at the time of the drought. This was associated with an increased number of early marriages in \(60 \%\) of households \({ }^{345}\). These effects occur against a backdrop of increasing climate shocks and disasters, which are expected to continue as global temperatures rise.

130 million of whom were children under the age of five**. The storms also leave 100,000 more children and youth out of school.

89. With Tropical Cyclone Freddy at least 2.3 million people have been affected (including 51 per cent women), with 476,000 displaced in 23 districts, including Phalombe District, Chikwawa District, and Machinga Districts, affecting 4.85 million people since the start of the outbreak, over 58,000 cumulative cases as at 19 March 2023, with 67 deaths reported in 23 districts, including Phalombe District, Chikwawa District and Machinga Districts, affecting 4.85 million people since the start of the outbreak, affecting 3.8 million people in 21 districts. An estimated 573,000 children under five, and 229,000 pregnant women were affected by Cyclone Freddy. Over 600,000 houses have been damaged or destroyed, with 400,000 homes severely damaged, access to health services has been hampered due to damaged road infrastructure. The Protection Cluster estimates that 125,000 children under five and pregnant women were affected by Cyclone Freddy. The Education Cluster estimates that 5.3 million children will need education support/127.

90. Beyond the direct impact of Cyclone Freddy on children, there are also indirect effects on other vulnerable groups such as children and adults with disabilities. For instance, beyond the effects of cyclones, floods, and landslides, people with disabilities face additional risks in terms of access to public services including specialised health services, and the loss of assistive services, often provided by family members.

91. People with disabilities, particularly women and girls, face an elevated risk from the consequences of climate change. The effects of climate change on people with disabilities are complex and constitute critical elements that influence how persons with disabilities are exposed to the effects of climate change. These include increased vulnerability due to poverty, lack of access to public services, migration status, religion, and sex can further increase the vulnerability of certain individuals with disabilities. People with disabilities face additional risks in terms of access to public services including specialised health services, and the loss of assistive services, often provided by family members.

92. Focusing on children, Malawi is among the top 10 countries ranked as having a high climate risk for children under five years old. The country has experienced an increase in extreme weather events with increased intensity, threatening the health, nutrition, education, development and the survival of millions of children. In 2022, Tropical Cyclone Freddy affected over 4 million people (15% of Malawi’s population) and displaced nearly 6.5 million people (20% of Malawi’s population), including 3.5 million children**. As another example, in 2022 tropical storm Ana caused flooding in the north-eastern part of the country, affecting over 180,000 households with infrastructure damage, roads, schools and health facilities and damaging crops, affecting over 965,000 people (37% of Malawi’s population), including children under the age of five**. The storms also keep 100,000 more children and youth out of school.

# Community Profiles: Gender and Social Inclusion Focus

93. Profiles of issues represented by communities across all six districts are presented here, based on gendered focus group discussions (women in each district), children (one each in four of the six districts), pregnant and breastfeeding women (one each in two districts) and key informant interviews with representatives of disability fora, people with disabilities, and other groups. The profiles include a summary of the main issues identified, including those among people with disabilities and women who are pregnant or breastfeeding.

The sections below outline the major findings from the focus group discussions.

## Summary of community-level consultations

The sections below outline the major findings from the focus group discussions.

### Focus groups with women, and pregnant and breastfeeding women


94. Climate hazards to which women and pregnant women and breastfeeding mothers reported being exposed include floods, droughts, and heavy winds. Floods, droughts and unpredictable rainfall cause crop loss and variable production levels, while also affecting water availability for drinking and cooking. No irrigation systems are in place, so people resort to open defecation practices. They are also often forced to eat contaminated food. The lack of food production is exacerbated by the fact that many women have been displaced from their homes due to floods. In addition, the poor quality of roads makes it difficult for them to access markets and health facilities.

95. Major floods in January and February 2022 were particularly problematic. Stagnant water after the flood caused a number of deaths among children under five years old. The heavy rains that followed heavy rains and heavy winds damaged cause damage to houses and schools, causing homelessness and the need for schoolchildren to walk long distances to attend classes. In addition, when food is stored in houses, flooding can cause it to spoil – as was reported for maize in Ntchisi. The women also reported that they had to move their livestock to higher ground during heavy rains and heavy winds. When losing houses, people are often moved to camps which encourages the spread of diseases.

96. Displacement is a particular problem in the wake of extreme events, including after the summer 2022 floods. Women and pregnant women and breastfeeding mothers are particularly vulnerable when trying to obtain food and rebuild their homes. Promised post-flood support did not materialize, and many


---
**Page 154**
---

women were forced to seek shelter in school blocks after damage to their houses. Women, pregnant women and lactating mothers also particularly noticed the stress and anxiety that extreme events bring because of the uncertainty it creates, which continues long after the actual extreme event is over.

Focus group discussions revealed that food insecurity was a major concern for pregnant women and breastfeeding mothers. The women noted some extremely remote locations (e.g., villages in TMA Monokoro and Mafinga) where there were no food groups available to them because of the flooding due to the increased healthcare needs caused by the extreme event. Many even end up giving birth in the community health center or at home, which is a major concern for pregnant women as well as in the case of babies born to HIV-positive women, the lack of medical support creates risks for the baby.

Pregnant women and breastfeeding mothers also noted that they were not able to access food groups. Surveillance assistants have provided training on the six food groups and the importance of these for children, but there are no food groups in their communities. The food groups include: fruits, roots, vegetables, sweet potato, Chinese vegetables, eggs, Amaranthus and fish.

It was noted that the nutritional value of the food groups is not sufficient to meet all needs in the morning should be augmented by ground nut flour or cooking oil - or avocados which can more easily be obtained if cost is a barrier. If there are no food groups available for pregnant women, breastfeeding mothers need to know what other foods they can eat that will meet their nutritional needs.

If the flooded land does not permit growing or obtaining nutritious food, then there are no solutions that can be implemented in the short term and the community must rely on external support which is limited and overlooks the longer-term agricultural needs, hence highlighting the need for a long-term adaptation approach.

A variety of actions have been taken to adapt to these circumstances, often with external support. In Machinga, pregnant women were provided with food groups in their communities by the district health team informed by nutritional needs, with training provided by the World Food Programme. Concerns were raised that this was not sustainable and that there is a need for more community-based solutions.

The existence of care groups in the community was previously supported by UNICEF, USAID, Save the Children and other partners. However, the sustainability of these groups has been poor with support for implementation having been largely lacking, meaning that only a small number of the groups that were established have continued to function. The lack of food groups is also a major concern following extreme events such as floods and heavy rains because of the impacts on mobility. However, in Machinga, there are no food groups available at this time due to flooding.

Women reported in Mangochi that they do not have access to sweet potato.

100 Women reported in Mangochi that they were not involved in the decision-making structures, including the district health team and the community. They also noted that they did not know the norms for, example they particularly mentioned a group that oversees issues for children under 5 years old. Women need access to information on how to identify signs of malaria and severe acute malnutrition (SAM) used to identify malnourished children in order to identify when hospital treatment is required.

100 In Mangochi, women noted that they were not able to access health facilities as they prefer to tend their gardens rather than waiting for emergency food to be provided. In Mangochi, women tended to rely on the community health center and the district health team (DHT) in Machinga, Lilongwe (Mlawa and Mlanga) to reduce vulnerability to floods, heavy rain and heavy winds, and in Nchise women noted that they were not able to access health facilities as they prefer to tend their gardens.

102 Nutrition and WASH-related training to group leaders is also necessary so that they can cascade this information down to the community. The training should be provided by the district health team or DHT facilities should look like so people can install them in their own homes to reduce disease transmission, and community members should be encouraged to use these facilities. This will help to improve the sustainability of care groups. Pregnant women and breastfeeding mothers need mopsites to reduce risks related to waterborne diseases such as cholera. The training should also include how to ensure climate-resilient health and nutrition, including through being able to access the six food groups.

Focus groups with children

The sections below outline the major findings from the focus group discussions.

103. The children understood climate change to be changes in the weather, linked to temperature and rainfall. They identified that climate change affected health through floods, droughts and extreme heat which may lead to waterborne diseases such as cholera. Children also noted that they were aware of people experiencing climate change hazards, with a particular focus on the floods. The children reported that floods had led to damage to their homes and schools, and to loss of crops, livestock, fish, trees, and animals and crops in farms. These effects were reported to have consequences for people's livelihoods – the children noted that reduced harvests lead to reductions in food intake. In Mangochi, where people


---
**Page 155**
---

## Limitations of the education facilities increase the vulnerability to climate change

When weather patterns are disrupted by climate change, schools in Mangochi are already oversubscribed with more than 400 students. The number of classrooms is insufficient, so some students have to sit on the floor or under trees during lessons. In addition, the school buildings are divided into four groups and the groups rotate the use of the classroom—although this still means that when it rains, the children cannot go outside for playtime. Girls are more vulnerable than boys because girls, and many (particularly) boys practice open defecation which increases risk of disease. There is a terrible lack of sanitation facilities in the school buildings, especially for girls who have to use bushes or trees as toilets.

The children's main adaptation priorities include reliable water supply and basin for use in washing hands, and latrines as more classrooms and desks.

## Focus groups with women

The sections below outline the major findings from the focus group discussions.

### 10.5 Climate hazards to which men reported being exposed included heat waves, floods and heavy winds. Heat waves affect crops by leading to crop damage and dehydration and in some cases, death of plants. This has also had implications for agricultural production and availability of water availability, while floods cropping up off the river banks, in the worst cases, even in dam (dams) which are meant to hold back flood waters, typically better. In addition, wind variability, water availability, it warn, have implications on food security and nutrition security and nutritional status of children. The women, who was interviewed, reported that they were more affected by climate change than men.

### 10.6 Floods were reported in Mangochi, with particular mention of the January-February 2003 floods when heavy rains caused flooding in the area. In some cases, people had to move their livestock and crops away from the river banks because of proximity to the Bwange and Mzimba rivers were particularly affected by flooding. Floods destroy crops in the fields and also cause damage to houses and other infrastructure. The women reported that they lost food security as a result of floods. In some cases, women noted that many had their food intake reduced to one meal per day each flood. Floods also impact on income generation activities such as fishing and horticulture. Women in Ntcheu reported the destruction of gullies and deposition of sand. In Mangochi, in order to compensate for these impacts, fertilizer has to be applied at a higher rate than usual because it is not possible to use the same amount of fertilizer with regard to the onset of the rainy season.

### 10.7 Rainfall variability also as bridges also impacted the accessibility and ability of women to procure food there, for example in Mponda it was mentioned that the bridge close to Makwanda trading post was washed away by floods. The lack of a functioning bridge has had an impact on access to health infrastructure impacts the capacity of women to access antenatal and other care health. In Mangochi they noted that the flood destroyed the main road leading to the district hospital and the health centre in Kochie. Men in Ntcheu also reported that floods impeded access to health care because some roads were washed away by floods. The lack of a functioning bridge has had an impact on the bridge being impassable. Among other things, they noted that difficulties for women in accessing contraception services as well as antenatal and postnatal care services. In Ntcheu, they reported that the damage to infrastructure impacted the capacity of the Health Surveillance Assistants to move around and provide services to communities. The lack of a functioning bridge has had an impact on the community with lack of attending staff, and in increase in pregnancy complications. Heavy winds also cause destruction of crops and livestock.

### 10.8 Many community members reported direct experience with malaria, cholera, and diarrhea. Malaria is largely a seasonal disease, with high cases in the rainy season due to the quantity of stagnant water present on the ground. In Ntcheu, they noted that there were many cases of malaria and that many cases of malaria occurred. Cholera is a particular risk during incidences of flooding as most households do not have access to latrines or toilets and instead use bushes or trees for defecation.

In Mangochi many lakeside communities drink water from the


---
**Page 156**
---

109\. In both Mangochi and Ntcheu the community reported knowledge on improved nutrition and complementary feeding practices. Mothers in Mangochi were aware of the importance of breast-feeding and encouraged growth of vegetables such as mpuro, rape and tomatoes in backyard gardens. Per Malawi health guidelines, mothers are supposed to exclusively breast-feed for six months but this has not been followed by many women. Mothers have been trained on complementary feeding practices through the training they have received from agriculture officials in meeting the needs of the six food groups and how to prepare them. They were also taught about the importance of vitamin A, iron, zinc, vitamins to fight off sickness, minerals for strong bones and water for dry digestion and body cooling". They also reported that mothers are encouraged by their husbands to breast-feed their children until they are 18 months old. Mothers are encouraged to introduce complementary foods at six months but this is not always followed as many women still continue to breast-feed even after the child has turned one year old. In Ntcheu, mothers were also trained on complementary feeding practices and were given groundnuts and soya flour, vegetables, a range of fruits and plain water. However, breastfeeding is encouraged until 18 months.

110\. Small investments such as chicken and goats are kept in the area, but crop agriculture is now rare because of flooding. In Mangochi, women grow sweet potatoes, tomatoes, rape, mpuro, onions and some orange fresh sweet potatoes. In Mangochi the lake is now the main source of livelihood through fishing. For people living with disabilities, there are no specific services or support groups in place but there are organizations that provide support for example Plan International Malawi provides sweet potato vines and cassava seeds to women who have been trained on how to grow these crops. The organization also provides vegetable seeds and pesticides. Churches Action for Relief and Development (CARD) and United Methodist Church of Malawi (UMCM) provide training in agriculture, including the use of improved cooking stoves. The Community Information Programme (CIP) has distributed sweet potato vines in a partnership with the nutrition department (under agriculture). In Ntcheu, women grow vegetables such as mpuro, rape and tomatoes. They also grow maize, beans, eyeight and blood sugar leaves. World Vision and the Department of Nutrition are also involved in the distribution of seeds to women who have been trained on how to grow these crops. Women are demotivated from growing crops because of flooding.

111\. Men reported that women are included in major committees in the village. However, the examples they gave were mostly women's village committees and care groups. The women also reported that women play a key role in decision-making at household level and healthcare priorities, which include the need for improved WASH through toilets and benches; better bathing facilities to reduce the spread of diseases such as cholera; and access to health services in the hospital and only provides free support to chola cases and delivery of children to pregnant women. The section also reported that women are involved in decision-making at household level on what seedsings, because towland lakecommunities in Mangochi are particularly vulnerable to flooding. And they are also involved in decision making about which crops to grow, choosing drought-resistant and early maturing crops that grow well in a shorter and more unpredictable rainy season.

Focus groups and key informant interviews with people with disabilities

The sections below outline the major findings from the focus group discussions.

There are a number of disability-focused organisations operating within the six project districts. All districts have at least one organization, which is registered under the Disability Act 2013, supported by the Federation of Disability Organisations in Malawi (FEDOMA) which include representation from all sectors. The role of FEDOMA is to provide a platform for people with disabilities and their families to access support, for example through the provision of wheelchairs and hearing aids. However, despite some improvements in service delivery, there are still many challenges that need to be addressed such as risk reduction activities.

114\. Ntcheu district has two organizations: the Association for People with Disabilities in Malawi (APDM), FEDOMA, Sightavers, and other NGOs and government sectors that are supporting people with

### Project-specific conclusions and recommendations

#### Project-specific conclusions and recommendations

119. In light of the existing gender discrimination and social inclusion issues throughout Malawi and in the target areas of the project that have been highlighted, a list of project-specific conclusions and


30


---
**Page 158**
---

recommendations follows for improving the rights and access to opportunities for women, children and people with disabilities. Overarching recommendations are presented, followed by specific national- level, district/area level, community level and project operations levels.

Overarching Project Recommendations:
Recommendation 1: Increase awareness of, and participation of women, youth and people (women, men) with disabilities in any community-level decision-making structures. Work will be done with women, men and people with disabilities to increase their capacity to participate effectively at all levels of women’s participation, pressures/norms for men, value of working together, communication, and non- violent conflict resolution, gender equality, and the importance of women’s leadership roles in communities and reinforce women's participation.

Recommendation 2: Facilitate and promote consultation procedures across all aspects of the project that are inclusive. These efforts made to ensure inclusion of women and youth and those who are from marginalized or excluded groups including women with HIV and AIDS, etc. This will be done by ensuring that women’s voices are heard in decision-making processes and concerns about climate risks and adaptation strategies while avoiding the potential influence of harmful gender norms and practices. To ensure that all community members, especially vulnerable members in adaptation and resilience activities, the project can ensure that male, female and youth facilitators are trained to work with these participants.

Recommendation 3: Take opportunities to raise awareness and train stakeholders on the importance of inclusive approaches that contribute to gender equality and social inclusion.

Recommendation 4: Address gender norms through working both with women and boys and girls, promoting equal access for women and men in decision-making processes and participation and leadership. Interventions should be designed to change negative attitudes about the role of women and girls and promote their active participation in all aspects of life. This will include engaging people with disabilities and those who are from marginalized or excluded groups (women with disabilities, etc.) at community, sub-national, national and regional levels.

Recommendation 5: Meaningfully collaborate with partners and organisations of particular groups (i.e., children, youth, women, men, people with disabilities) to ensure that their needs and concerns are addressed in the project. This includes facilitating the involvement of individuals and groups who face heightened vulnerability or exclusion from decision-making processes at all levels including national, sub-national, local and community- making processes.

Recommendation 7: Training and information is presented in a GESD sensitive manner and tailored to the context and needs of particular groups of participants including women, youth with disabilities, children and those who are literate. This includes being translated into local languages, and how to use gender-sensitive tools and materials (e.g., audio-visual presentations, or multimedia), including in radio, braille broadcasts, social media, SMS and visual materials). Visual materials should challenge harmful gender norms and practices and promote the active participation of women with disabilities. This also includes ensuring that relevant information and appropriate mechanisms to measure progress are in place. These mechanisms address vulnerability and accessibility concerns for all participants.

National level approaches:
At the national level, the following approaches will be prioritized:

120. Conduct training in coordination with the MoCoSWD on their developing capacity building programs for wider government Ministries, Departments and Agencies (MDAs) and government gender focal points that have been established in relevant MDAs to mainstream gender across the respective sectors.

121. Facilitate training on GESD and climate resilience for interested and engaged government stakeholders, ensuring that all training done, and materials generated are done in a GESD sensitive manner.


---
**Page 159**
---

### District/town level approaches

At the district/town level, the following approaches should be prioritised:

1. **Promote gender and disability-sensitive planning:** Ensure that policies are informed by gender and social differences in vulnerability to climate-related health impacts, and their inclusion in policy documents and priority-setting processes.
2. **Strengthen capacity for data collection:** For the health early warning system to include sex, age, and disability-disaggregated data.
3. **Facilitate training on the health early warning system:** For health care facility staff which highlights the importance of including women and girls, pregnant and breastfeeding women, elderly people with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups in surveillance activities while reflecting their vulnerabilities.
4. **Work with community leaders and planning partners:** To ensure that the rollout of malaria, cholera, diarrhea and nutrition support interventions proactively targets women, pregnant and breastfeeding women, the elderly and women with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups to provide equitable vulnerability reduction.
5. **Ensure district/town level committees are sensitised:** To the need for inclusive representation in their structures and recognise the need to positively target messages in a GESI-sensitive manner to different groups of people.
6. **Actively include gender and disability-focused district council staff (for example the district social welfare officer):** At district level in district and community level activities as a way of supporting the strengthening of health care facilities across the district.

### Community level approaches

At the household and community level, the following approaches should be prioritised:

1. **Support mobile health unit staff:** To maintain up-to-date registers of marginalised groups, including boys and girls, pregnant and breastfeeding women, the elderly and women with disabilities.
2. **Ensure that men in the community are meaningfully engaged:** And sensitised to the nature of health vulnerability of pregnant and breastfeeding women and children, including through observation of household activities.
3. **Ensure that the mobile health unit and events proactively target marginalised groups:** Both physically (e.g., through door-to-door visits, mobile clinics, mobile toilets, mosquito nets) and through active targeting of health content and interventions (delivered within transportable units or at community venues). This can be achieved by working with participatory facilitators, loudspeaker, visual materials that have been co-created - targeted approaches to reach the most vulnerable groups.
4. **Ensure that the mobile health unit and community level health staff (i.e., health surveillance assistants and other community leaders representing different social groups):** Offer public health care messaging.
5. **Ensure district/town level health care staff are sensitised:** To the need to collect sex, age and disability-disaggregated data.

# Gender equality and social inclusion action plan

## Drawing on the rich information from the GESI assessment, this project is designed to take an investment approach in order to ensure that all women and men are able to participate fully in development and inclusion by addressing the power relationships at the root of inequality with the aim of ensuring that all the activities implemented will be inclusive and reflect the lived experience of vulnerable and excluded groups. This will lead to a more equitable, more sustainable, more successful and resilient community.

The GESI Action Plan will use an approach which starts with reflections on how power is wielded, how decisions are made and who benefits from them. It will also consider what can be done in order that the implementation of activities is inclusive and reflects the lived experience of vulnerable and excluded groups. This will lead to a more equitable, more sustainable, more successful and resilient community.

The project will provide the focus on GESI change on the health of the most vulnerable and marginalized groups: women, girls, youth, men and boys with disabilities. The burden of responding to health shocks often falls disproportionately on women, since they usually bear the responsibility for caring for children and other family members. By using the information gathered in the GESI Analysis,


---
**Page 161**
---

it will guide the community engagement in finding solutions to these problems that are equitable and sustainable.

145. To do this, the project will use a GESI and intersectionality lens, looking at different people's vulnerabilities and taking their capacities into account in the design and implementation of the project.
This methodological approach is based on the premise that women and girls are disproportionately affected by climate change, physical and psychosocial risks such as sexual and gender-based violence or perpetuate inequality.
